I. AN OVERVIEW OF THE ISSUES RAISED AND THE ANSWERS
FOUND
The present judgment deals with seventeen commercial suits filed
before this Court. In more than half of these suits, neither parties, through
any means, are at Delhi. In each of these suits, the court within whose
territorial jurisdiction the parties are situated is competent to adjudicate upon
the lis sought to be agitated before this Court. They however, while
disturbing the horizontal allocation of territorial jurisdiction, knock this
Court, on novel and ingenious grounds.
2. It is vehemently argued on behalf of the plaintiffs that this Court has
been entertaining and deciding cases of parties located across the length and
breadth of India. It has done so on the basis that part of cause of action has
arisen within Delhi or that either of the parties carries on business at Delhi.
It is argued by counsel that in the world of IP litigation, a suit for
infringement along with passing off can be filed at a place where a website
having the infringing products is accessible and commercial transactions can
get concluded on the website. This, it is submitted, constitutes a part of the
cause of action and makes a suit maintainable under Section 20(c) of the
Civil Procedure Code, 1908.1
3. It is also submitted that a defendant or a plaintiff ―carries on
business‖ at a place from where the website advertising, promoting, or

1
―CPC‖.

26
offering for sale, the goods or services in question, is accessible. It is
clarified by learned counsel that for jurisdiction to be conferred, the website,
also, need not be hosted by the defendant, nor is it necessary that the listing
(if any) be made by the defendant. Importantly, it has been argued, that
because the plaintiff or a person set up by him, has placed an order on an e-marketplace like Flipkart or Amazon for the defendant‘s goods, and given
the address of Delhi, jurisdiction is manifestly made out. Such a transaction,
the Court may observe, the plaintiff or his agent could have done from
Ladakh, Tamil Nadu, Arunachal Pradesh, or Gujarat. The argument of
counsel, however, remains that this Court must entertain such a suit because
its agent gave the delivery address of Delhi.
4. A statute or a provision cannot be interpreted in a manner that confers
an unbridled, unguided and uncanalised right on the plaintiff to create
jurisdiction within the territory of a given Court and obligates it to decide
that lis. Such an exercise of an uncircumscribed right is bound to create a
mockery of the concept of territoriality and the ―local limits‖ of the court.
5. In today‘s digital world, every website is accessible pan-India, if not
worldwide; services can be availed of through the internet in every remote
corner of the country, and goods can be delivered to most, if not all, places.
If the position of law is what has been stated above, jurisdictional rules
would have no meaning. The horizontal division of jurisdiction in the world
of IP disputes would cease to have any application. The jurisdictional
provisions under the CPC, which allocate jurisdiction amongst courts, may
as well be given a burial.

27
6. In some of the decisions passed by this Court undoubtedly jurisdiction
has been exercised in the manner canvassed by counsel. After enjoying,
effectively, a pan-India jurisdiction, it becomes difficult for a court today to
draw a Lakshman Rekha and restrain itself from stepping outside its bounds.
This issue, however, demands closer consideration. The question is not one
of losing territory but of institutional discipline. If jurisdiction is naturally
made out, the Court must entertain the suit without hesitation. But if a
dispute ought to be agitated before another court and is brought to Delhi,
almost forcibly, and such a suit is entertained, the other rightful and natural
forum which should have exercised jurisdiction over the matter gets
deprived of the opportunity to adjudicate upon that cause of action.
Institutional discipline and respect require this Court to refrain from
entertaining such a suit. The statutory and constitutional scheme evinces a
carefully structured division of courts‘ jurisdiction. Constitutional morality
demands that this Court respect that scheme and abides by it.
7. It may be possible that some of the features that this Court has,
arguably, no other High Court does. Its role in the advancement of IP law in
this country, and its implication abroad, simply cannot be overstated.
8. The establishment of the IPD, following the abolition of the
Intellectual Property Appellate Board, was accompanied by the IPD Rules
and specialised Patent Suit Rules. These reflect an understanding that patent
and other technology disputes may call for a different approach to pleadings,
technical evidence, confidentiality and case management.
9. In fact, the World Intellectual Property Organization‘s International
Guide to Patent Case Management for Judges too identifies several

28
procedural features of the Delhi model: concurrent expert evidence or ―hot-tubbing‖,2
remote evidence,3preservation of evidence and litigation-hold
notices,4guidance on damages,5consolidation of proceedings,6
confidentiality clubs,7summary adjudication,8technically qualified
assistance,9and mediation or early neutral evaluation.10
10. The filing procedure before this Court is also uniquely suited for
commercial disputes. Actions in fresh, pending and disposed of cases are
ordinarily filed electronically by the advocate or litigant in person. Court
fees are capable of being deposited electronically, and hearings are
conducted in physical, hybrid or video conferencing mode.
11. The Registry communicates objections to the filing advocate or
litigant by email, SMS or web hosting,11 and once the objections are cleared
the case is processed for listing. Any urgent matter filed before 12 noon is
put before the Court for hearing on the following working day,12 and the
daily cause lists of this Court notify that fresh petitions and applications
filed before 12 noon are listed before the roster Bench accordingly. The
practical consequence is that a plaintiff whose filing is free of defects may
ordinarily expect the matter to be taken up on the next working day. This is

2 Rule 16, IPD Rules.
3 Rule 15, IPD Rules.
4 Rule 18, IPD Rules.
5 Rule 20, IPD Rules.
6 Rule 26, IPD Rules.
7 Rule 19, IPD Rules.
8 Rule 27, IPD Rules.
9 Rules 31 and 32, IPD Rules.
10 Rule 37, IPD Rules.
11 Rule 18.3 of the e-Filing Rules.
12 Rule 12 of Chapter I of the Delhi High Court (Original Side) Rules, 2018.

29
a unique feature which perhaps may not be available in any other court. It
guarantees quick listing, hearing, and adjudication of disputes.
12. Two related features of this Court also give litigants a measure of
certainty after a matter is instituted. First, an order is ordinarily recorded and
uploaded on the website of this Court on every date on which a matter is
listed, whether before the Bench or before the Joint Registrar, including
where the proceeding is confined to an adjournment or to directions on
pleadings. The record of proceedings is, therefore, available to the parties,
and to anyone examining the file, without reliance on oral accounts of what
transpired. Second, every matter ordinarily leaves the courtroom with a fixed
next date, and that date is reflected on the case status page. Where a matter
does not reach, it is ordinarily assigned a fresh date of listing. Matters are,
thus, not left to languish without a date, and parties and counsel are able to
plan the progress of the litigation, including compliance with timelines
under the Commercial Courts Act, 2015, with reasonable predictability.
13. Last among these features, and one the most important is the Bar
practising before this Court. It includes a large body of advocates and law
firms who devote their practice to commercial and intellectual property
litigation. This Court also has the advantage of being assisted by lawyers
who are specialists and authorities in their fields. The doyens of India‘s IP
Bar practice before this Court.
14. The uniqueness of this Court, thus, lies not simply in assigning IP
matters to designated judges, but in building an institutional and procedural
framework suited to the peculiar legal, scientific and commercial questions
presented by IP disputes.

30
15. India‘s IP landscape to a large extent has grown under the Banyan
Tree, which is the Delhi High Court. While the Banyan provides shelter and
comfort, it also stunts the growth of other trees below it.
16. There is no reason to believe that other High Courts will not rise to
the occasion, create a specialised machinery like this Court, and make IP
litigation conducive either before them, or the district courts.
17. This Court, without any doubt, must not command a reputation that
no matter how minuscule a connection the lis has with Delhi, the plaintiff or
the defendant, the High Court of Delhi, would entertain a suit.
18. In Aligarh Muslim University v. Vinay Engineering Enterprises (P)
Ltd.,
13 the Supreme Court in the context of another premier High Court
observed ―we are surprised, not a little, that the High Court of Calcutta
should have exercised jurisdiction in a case where it had absolutely no
jurisdiction.‖ In Oil and Natural Gas Commission v. Utpal Kumar Basu,
14
which arose after the decision in Aligarh Muslim University, the Supreme
Court, noted that the institutional integrity of a court is predicated upon the
manner in which its members conduct themselves. If a lis having no material
connection with a given court is entertained, suspicions arise and eyebrows
get raised. Para. 12 of the decision is extracted as under:
―12. …It must be remembered that the image and prestige of a court
depends on how the members of that institution conduct themselves. If an
impression gains ground that even in cases which fall outside the territorial
jurisdiction of the court, certain members of the court would be willing to
exercise jurisdiction on the plea that some event, however trivial and
unconnected with the cause of action had occurred within the jurisdiction of

13 (1994) 4 SCC 710.
14 (1994) 4 SCC 711.

31
the said court, litigants would seek to abuse the process by carrying the
cause before such members giving rise to avoidable suspicion. That would
lower the dignity of the institution and put the entire system to ridicule…‖
19. Bearing in mind considerations of predictability, fairness, and the
larger public interest, this Court has attempted to re-examine the caselaw on
jurisdiction in IP suits. The position of law, as the discussion below shall
reveal, is not as laissez-faire as learned counsel have contended it to be. A
brief snapshot of the manner in which caselaw has been dealt with in the
present judgment is as under:
20. The decisions of the Supreme Court in Patel Roadways Limited v.
Prasad Trading Company,
15 Dhodha House v. SK Maingi,16 and Indian
Performing Rights Society v. Sanjay Dalia and Anr.,
17 and of this Court in
Ultra Home Construction Pvt. Ltd. v. Purushottam Kumar Chaubey &
Ors.18 authoritatively declare the meaning of the expression ―carries on
business‖, as it appears in Sections 20(a) and (b) of the CPC and Sections
62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright Act, 195719 and the Trade Marks Act,
1999,20 respectively. The judgment of this Court in World Wrestling
Entertainment, Inc. v. Reshma Collection & Ors.,
21 despite noticing the
decision of Dhodha House, appears to have fallen foul of its material
conclusions. The underlying reasoning of WWE, more importantly, becomes
completely unworkable after the decision of the Supreme Court in Sanjay
Dalia and of this Court in Ultra Home.

15 (1991) 4 SCC 270. Followed in New Moga Transport Co. v. United India Insurance Co. Ltd. and Ors.,
(2004) 4 SCC 677.
16 (2006) 9 SCC 41.
17 (2015) 10 SCC 161.
18 2016 SCC OnLine Del 376.
19 ―Copyright Act‖.
20 ―Trade Marks Act‖.
21 2014 SCC OnLine Del 2031.

32
21. In the subsequent chain of decisions of our Court, which begin from
Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd. v. Veda Seed Sciences Pvt. Ltd.22 and
end at ITC v. Adyar Gate Hotels Ltd.,
23 it was not pointed out to the learned
Division Bench that WWE is no longer a binding precedent on the issue
raised in these cases. The observations and findings given by the learned
Division Bench in these judgments, thus, may not be binding as they were
rendered on the basis of WWE.
22. Similarly, for claims concerning passing off, the decisions of our
Court in Glen Raven Mills Inc. v. Vaspar Concepts Pvt. Ltd. and Anr.,
24
and Indovax Pvt. Ltd. v. Merck Animal Health and Ors.,
25 were not
canvassed before the Court in Burger King Corporation v. Techchand
Shewakramani and Ors.
26 The decision in Burger King having been
rendered without adverting to earlier applicable judgments on the issue of
passing off, may not be binding insofar as it relates to a claim for passing
off.
23. The judgment of the Division Bench of our Court in Banyan Tree
Holding (P) Ltd. v. A. Murali Krishna Reddy and Anr.,
27 continues to
govern jurisdictional issues when a plaintiff pleads a cause of action for
infringement or passing off on the basis of a website. Banyan Tree, inter
alia, requires the plaintiff to, prima facie, show using material/documents,
that some commercial transactions using the website were entered into by

22 2025 SCC OnLine Del 8727.
23 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4852.
24 1995 SCC OnLine Del 724.
25 2017 SCC OnLine Del 9393.
26 2018 SCC OnLine Del 10881.
27 2009 SCC OnLine Del 3780.

33
the defendant with a user of its website within the forum State. The decision
of another Division Bench in Tata Sons Pvt. Ltd. v. Hakunamatata Tata
Founders and Ors.,
28 dispenses with this requirement in Banyan Tree on
the basis of a decision of this Court in Millennium & Copthorne
International Limited v. Aryans Plaza Services Pvt. Ltd. and Ors.,
29 and of
the Court of Appeal of England and Wales in Lifestyle Equities CV v.
Amazon UK Services Ltd.30
24. It was, however, not pointed out to the learned Division Bench in
Tata Sons that the decision in Millennium & Copthorne International
Limited was rendered by a learned Single Judge of our Court, which could
not have disturbed the mandatory requirements of Banyan Tree. It was also
not brought to the notice of the Division Bench that the law in England was
fundamentally different from that declared in Banyan Tree. The finding
rendered by the Division Bench that a ―looming presence‖ and an ability to
access the website may be sufficient to constitute ―targeting‖ appears to be
in conflict with both Banyan Tree and the law prevailing in England, as
propounded in the judgment rendered by the UK Supreme Court,
31 in appeal
against the judgment of the Court of Appeal in Lifestyle Equities.
25. The use of an ―investigator‖ to enter into trap transactions so as to
concoct jurisdiction in a forum which otherwise has no jurisdiction is also
impermissible, as declared in Banyan Tree. The decisions of our Court in
Machinenfabrik Rieter AG and Anr. v. Tex Tech Industries India Pvt. Ltd.

28 2022 SCC OnLine Del 2968.
29 2018 SCC OnLine Del 8260.
30 [2022] EWCA Civ 552.
31 2024 UKSC 8.

34
and Anr.,
32 and Reiter AG and Anr. v. Kavassery Narayanaswamy
Venkatasubramanian,
33 hold that Banyan Tree’s dictum concerning trap
transactions does not apply to offline transactions. It was, however, not
pointed out to the learned Benches in these cases that the conclusion of
Banyan Tree on the issue of trap transactions was itself based on cases
which concerned offline transactions. The decisions in Machinenfabrik
Rieter AG and Reiter AG may not, therefore, be binding precedents on this
issue.
26. Accordingly, the decisions which govern this Court are: first, Patel
Roadways, Sanjay Dalia, and Ultra Home to interpret the expression
―carries on business‖ under Section 20(a) of the CPC and Section 134(2) of
the Trade Marks Act and 62(2) of the Copyright Act; second, Glen and
Indovax for determining where the cause of action for a passing off action in
the offline world has accrued; third, Burger King, to adjudicate on
territoriality for suits concerning trademark infringement in the offline
world; and fourth, Banyan Tree, for determining jurisdictional issues when
a plaintiff pleads cause of action for infringement or passing off on the basis
of a website.
27. The Court is conscious that finding some difficulty in the application
of the aforenoted decisions, a reference order has been passed by a co-ordinate Bench,
34 to decide the issue of jurisdiction in infringement and
passing off suits. The filing of fresh suits, however, has not come to a halt.
Many matters require urgent interim relief to be granted and the issue of this

32 2021 SCC OnLine Del 1825.
33 2025 SCC OnLine Del 4379.
34 2026:DHC:7198.

35
Court‘s territorial jurisdiction necessarily requires an adjudication. There
cannot, it may be stressed, be a vacuum in law.
28. The Supreme Court in the case of Ashok Sadarangani v. Union of
India and Ors.,
35 was requested to abstain from pronouncing judgment on
an issue which was the subject matter of a reference to the larger bench.36
The Supreme Court, while rejecting the plea, observed as under:
―29. …the pendency of a reference to a larger Bench, does not mean that all
other proceedings involving the same issue would remain stayed till a
decision was rendered in the reference…Till such time as the decisions cited
at the Bar are not modified or altered in any way, they continue to hold the
field.‖
Thus, through the present judgment, this Court intends to decipher, pending
the reference made to a larger Bench,
37 the position of law governing
territorial jurisdiction for infringement and passing off claims, arising under
the law governing the intellectual property rights of the parties.
29. Learned counsel appearing for the parties in the captioned matters
have been heard, and their written submissions and compilation of
judgments, concerning the issues dealt with in the present judgement have
also been perused. A thoughtful consideration has been given to the
submissions made in the instant batch of matters.
30. A lis concerning infringement and passing off is governed by Section
20 of the CPC. A suit for infringement, additionally, is covered by Sections

35 (2012) 11 SCC 321.
36 (2012) 11 SCC 321, para. 23.
37 Hindustan Unilever Limited v. Kwick Living (I) Pvt. Ltd., CS (Comm) 904/2026, judgment dated
25.08.2026.

36
62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright and Trade Marks Act, respectively.
38 For
ease of reference these provisions are reproduced, in seriatim, as under:
Section 20 of the CPC:
―20. Other suits to be instituted where defendants reside or cause of
action arises.
Subject to the limitations aforesaid, every suit shall be instituted in a Court
within the local limits of whose jurisdiction—
(a) the defendant, or each of the defendants where there are more than one,
at the time of the commencement of the suit, actually and voluntarily
resides, or carries on business, or personally works for gain; or
(b) any of the defendants, where there are more than one, at the time of the
commencement of the suit, actually and voluntarily resides, or carries on
business, or personally works for gain, provided that in such case either
the leave of the Court is given, or the defendants who do not reside, or
carry on business, or personally works for gain, as aforesaid, acquiesce in
such institution; or
(c) the cause of action, wholly or in part, arises.
Explanation.—A corporation shall be deemed to carry on business at its sole
or principal office in India or, in respect of any cause of action arising at any
place where it has also a subordinate office, at such place.‖
Section 62 of the Copyright Act:
―62. Jurisdiction of court over matters arising under this Chapter.
(1) Every suit or other civil proceeding arising under this Chapter in respect
of the infringement of copyright in any work or the infringement of any
other right conferred by this Act shall be instituted in the district court
having jurisdiction.
(2) For the purpose of sub-section (1), a ―district court having jurisdiction‖
shall, notwithstanding anything contained in the Code of Civil Procedure,
1908 (5 of 1908), or any other law for the time being in force, include a
district court within the local limits of whose jurisdiction, at the time of the
institution of the suit or other proceeding, the person instituting the suit or
other proceeding or, where there are more than one such persons, any of
them actually and voluntarily resides or carries on business or personally
works for gain.‖

38 Cumulatively termed as ―special provisions‖.

37
Section 134 of the Trade Marks Act:
―134. Suit for infringement, etc., to be instituted before District Court.
(1) No suit—
(a) for the infringement of a registered trade mark; or
(b) relating to any right in a registered trade mark; or
(c) for passing off arising out of the use by the defendant of any trade mark
which is identical with or deceptively similar to the plaintiff‘s trade mark,
whether registered or unregistered, shall be instituted in any court inferior
to a District Court having jurisdiction to try the suit.
(2) For the purpose of clauses (a) and (b) of sub-section (1), a ―District
Court having jurisdiction‖ shall, notwithstanding anything contained in the
Code of Civil Procedure, 1908 (5 of 1908) or any other law for the time
being in force, include a District Court within the local limits of whose
jurisdiction, at the time of the institution of the suit or other proceeding, the
person instituting the suit or proceeding, or, where there are more than one
such persons any of them, actually and voluntarily resides or carries on
business or personally works for gain.
Explanation.—For the purposes of sub-section (2), ―person‖ includes the
registered proprietor and the registered user.‖
31. The case law pertaining to specific aspects may be analysed. It is
important, however, to caveat the discussion that follows by noting that
since certain judgments deal with multiple issues, a straitjacket
categorisation has not been possible. A judgment under a given heading may
not exclusively deal with the topic identified in the heading.
II. ANALYSIS
A. THE PRAYERS TO BE TESTED
32. If a single suit has been filed alleging infringement and passing off,
jurisdiction for both claims needs to be individually established before the
suit can be termed as being maintainable.

38
33. In Dhodha House, the Supreme Court examined the decision of the
Allahabad High Court holding that a suit under the Trade and Merchandise
Marks Act, 1958, and the Copyright Act, 1957, was primarily under the
former, for which resort cannot be made to Section 62 of the latter Act.
Dismissing the appeal, SB Sinha J. observed:
―54. For the purpose of invoking the jurisdiction of a court only because two
causes of action joined in terms of the provisions of the Code of Civil
Procedure, the same would not mean that thereby the jurisdiction can be
conferred upon a court which had jurisdiction to try only the suit in respect of
one cause of action and not the other. Recourse to the additional forum,
however, in a given case, may be taken if both the causes of action arise
within the jurisdiction of the court which otherwise had the necessary
jurisdiction to decide all the issues.‖
34. Similarly, Dabur India Ltd. v. K.R. Industries39 arose from a suit
filed before a learned Single Judge of this Court alleging copyright
infringement and passing off qua a product‘s carton which the plaintiff
claimed was protected as ―artistic work‖ within the meaning of the
Copyright Act. It was held that, insofar as the relief of passing off is
concerned, jurisdiction in Delhi is not made out as the defendant resided in
Andhra Pradesh, and there was no documentary evidence to show that the
respondent was selling goods in Delhi. The said view was affirmed by the
Division Bench and the case travelled to the Supreme Court. SB Sinha J.
dismissed the appeal and upheld the view taken by the Division Bench and
the Single Judge. The Supreme Court held that where jurisdiction for a suit
for passing off is not made out before a given court, merely by clubbing
prayers for infringement and passing off, and relying on Section 62(2) of the
Copyright Act, jurisdiction cannot be established.

39 (2008) 10 SCC 595.

39
35. In Dabur India, the Court held that a composite suit meant a suit
founded on an infringement action where incidental powers of the court are
required to be invoked. In such a case, the plaintiff seeks a remedy which
can, even otherwise, be granted by the Court. A composite suit is not one
where two different causes of action have been clubbed together. An action
for passing off is distinct from an infringement claim, and Section 62 cannot
be relied upon in such a case by clubbing both these causes in a single suit.
36. From the discussion above concerning the manner in which prayers in
a suit are to be tested, it may be concluded that a suit for infringement is
different from that of passing off. In a given case jurisdiction may be
established for infringement but not for passing off. If a composite suit is
filed seeking reliefs for both infringement and passing off, it must be
established that jurisdiction is made out for each relief. If it is found that a
court does not have jurisdiction to proceed with any of the reliefs, the suit
cannot proceed.
37. Having noted the aforesaid, the specific provisions that can be
invoked to plead jurisdiction may be examined.
B. “CARRIES ON BUSINESS”
38. The expression ―carries on business‖ finds mention under Sections
20(a) and (b) of the CPC, and Sections 62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright
Act, 1957 and Trade Marks Act, 1999, respectively. While the CPC
provision allows a suit to be instituted where the defendant ―carries on
business‖, the special provisions allow for a suit to be filed where the
plaintiff ―carries on its business‖.

40
39. In Patel Roadways Ltd. v. Prasad Trading Company,
40 the Supreme
Court interpreted the phrase ―carries on business‖ in the context of Section
20(a) of the CPC and the effect which the explanation to the said provision
has on its construction. While two appeals were disposed of via a common
order, the underlying facts of the appeals were identical. For the present
purposes, reference may be made to one of them. M/s. Patel Roadways (P)
Ltd., the defendant, carried on the business of a carrier and transported
goods on hire. It had its principal office at Bombay and branch offices at
various places, including at Tamil Nadu. A contract was entered into
between the defendant and M/s. Prasad Trading Company, the plaintiff, who
was a dealer in cardamom. Under the contract, the plaintiff entrusted goods
to the defendant at its sub-ordinate office at Tamil Nadu. After the goods
were transported by the defendant and kept in a godown at Delhi, they got
destroyed and damaged in a fire. Contending that the fire was a result of
negligence on the part of the defendant‘s staff, the plaintiff instituted a suit
at Tamil Nadu.
40. Before the trial court, an objection of territorial jurisdiction was
raised. It was the defendant‘s case that since it carried on business at
Bombay, the courts at Bombay had jurisdiction to entertain the lis under
Section 20(a) of the CPC. The jurisdiction of the courts at Madras was, in
turn, ousted owing to the operation of an exclusive jurisdiction clause in the
contract between the parties. This objection came to be rejected by the Trial
Court, and also by the High Court in revision.

40 (1991) 4 SCC 270.

41
41. The Supreme Court found that the explanation to Section 20 is in two
parts: the first part states ―a corporation shall be deemed to carry on
business at its sole or principal office in India‖, and the second part states
―in respect of any cause of action arising at any place where it has also a
subordinate office, at such place‖. The two parts of the explanation were
found, by the Supreme Court, to deal with distinct scenarios; the first dealt
with a case where a defendant-corporation has a sole office or a principal
office at a particular place. In this case, the corporation shall be deemed to
carry on business at that place where it has its sole office or principal office.
42. The second case dealt with by the explanation is where the principal
office of a corporation is at one place, and the sub-ordinate office is at
another place. In this case, if the cause of action has accrued at the place
where the sub-ordinate office is situated, the corporation shall be considered
to be carrying on business at that place. The Courts within whose
jurisdiction a corporation has its principal office would then not have
jurisdiction to entertain the lis. Paras. 9 and 12 of the decision in Patel
Roadways read as under:
―9. …The Explanation is in two parts, one before the word ―or‖ occurring
between the words ―office in India‖ and the word ―in respect of‖ and the
other thereafter. The Explanation applies to a defendant which is a
corporation, which term, as seen above, would include even a company such
as the appellant in the instant case. The first part of the Explanation applies
only to such a corporation which has its sole or principal office at a
particular place. In that event the courts within whose jurisdiction the sole or
principal office of the defendant is situate will also have jurisdiction
inasmuch as even if the defendant may not be actually carrying on business
at that place, it will ―be deemed to carry on business‖ at that place because
of the fiction created by the Explanation. The latter part of the Explanation
takes care of a case where the defendant does not have a sole office but has
a principal office at one place and has also a subordinate office at another
place. The words “at such place” occurring at the end of the

42
Explanation and the word “or” referred to above which is disjunctive
clearly suggest that if the case falls within the latter part of the
Explanation it is not the court within whose jurisdiction the principal
office of the defendant is situate but the court within whose jurisdiction
it has a subordinate office which alone shall have jurisdiction “in
respect of any cause of action arising at any place where it has also a
subordinate office‖.
****
12. We would also like to add that the interpretation sought to be placed by
the appellant on the provision in question renders the Explanation totally
redundant. If the intention of the legislature was, as is said on their behalf,
that a suit against a corporation could be instituted either at the place of its
sole or principal office (whether or not the corporation carries on business at
that place) or at any other place where the cause of action arises, the
provisions of clauses (a), (b) and (c) together with the first part of the
Explanation would have completely achieved the purpose. Indeed the effect
would have been wider. The suit could have been instituted at the place of
the principal office because of the situation of such office (whether or not
any actual business was carried on there). Alternatively, a suit could have
been instituted at the place where the cause of action arose under clause (c)
(irrespective of whether the corporation had a subordinate office in such
place or not). This was, therefore, not the purpose of the Explanation. The
Explanation is really an Explanation to clause (a). It is in the nature of a
clarification on the scope of clause (a) viz. as to where the corporation can
be said to carry on business. This, it is clarified, will be the place where the
principal office is situated (whether or not any business actually is carried
on there) or the place where a business is carried on giving rise to a cause of
action (even though the principal office of the corporation is not located
there) so long as there is a subordinate office of the corporation situated at
such place. The linking together of the place where the cause of action
arises with the place where a subordinate office is located clearly shows that
the intention of the legislature was that, in the case of a corporation, for the
purposes of clause (a), the location of the subordinate office, within the
local limits of which a cause of action arises, is to be the relevant place for
the filing of a suit and not the principal place of business. If the intention
was that the location of the sole or principal office as well as the location of
the subordinate office (within the limits of which a cause of action arises)
are to be deemed to be places where the corporation is deemed to be
carrying on business, the disjunctive ―or‖ will not be there. Instead, the
second part of the Explanation would have read ―and, in respect of any
cause of action arising at any place where it has a subordinate office, also at
such place‖.
(Emphasis supplied)

43
43. It may also be clarified that in Patel Roadways jurisdiction was
sought to be established exclusively through Section 20(a) of the CPC. The
case of the defendant in the said case was that despite cause of action
(wholly or in part) not accruing at its principal office, which was at Bombay,
jurisdiction of that forum Court was made out by an application of Section
20(a) of the CPC. It is this contention that the Supreme Court rejected.
44. The following conclusions can be drawn from the decision of the
Court in Patel Roadways: first, if a corporation has a sole office, it can be
sued at that place under Section 20(a) of the CPC as it shall be deemed to
carry on business at that place; second, if a corporation has more than one
offices, meaning there is a principal office and other sub-ordinate office(s),
and they are located at different places, then:
a. If no cause of action (wholly or in part) has arisen where the
defendant-corporation has its subordinate office, then the
corporation can be sued at the place where it has its principal
office.
b. If cause of action (wholly or in part) has arisen where the
defendant-corporation has its subordinate office, and no cause
of action has arisen at the company‘s principal office, then a
defendant-corporation can only be sued at the place of its sub-ordinate office and not at the place where it has its principal
office.

44
45. In Dhodha House v. SK Maingi,
41 the Supreme Court decided two
civil appeals by a common order. The first, being 6248/1997, arose from a
decision of the Allahabad High Court, and is dealt with at para. 33 of this
judgment. The discussion under this head need not be gone into for the
present purposes. The second civil appeal 16/1999 arose, originally, from a
suit filed before this Court for trademark and copyright infringement, along
with an action for passing off. The defendant and the plaintiff were found to
be carrying on business in diesel engines at Rajkot, Gujarat. Jurisdiction was
sought to be invoked, inter alia, by relying on Section 62 of the Copyright
Act, and claiming that the plaintiff ―carries on business‖ at Delhi.42 An
interim injunction application of the plaintiff was rejected on the ground that
jurisdiction was not made out. Upon an intra-court appeal being preferred
before the Division Bench, the same was allowed.
46. The order of the Division Bench,43 at para. 3 notes that the jurisdiction
of Delhi was invoked on three counts. Firstly, in view of Section 62(2) of
the Copyright Act. Secondly, on grounds that the defendants had sought
registration of the trade mark for sale at Delhi as well. Thirdly, by invoking
Section 20(c) of the CPC claiming that goods bearing the impugned mark
were being sold at Delhi. The Division Bench did not delve into the third
ground and found jurisdiction to be made out based on the first two. In order
to conclude that the plaintiff was ―carrying on business‖ at Delhi, the
Division Bench relied upon the pleading made in the plaint that the

41 (2006) 9 SCC 41.
42 (2006) 9 SCC 41, para. 13.
43 1998 SCC OnLine Del 181.

45
plaintiff‘s goods are sold in Delhi. Mere sale of goods was equated with a
corporation carrying on business at that place.
47. The decision of the Division Bench was assailed before the Supreme
Court, which held that merely because a corporation‘s goods are available in
the market at Delhi or are actually being sold at Delhi, would not mean that
the corporation is carrying on business at that place.44 This view expressed
by the Supreme Court at paras. 47 and 51 of the decision is in
contradistinction to what was held by the Division Bench of this Court. It
must be stressed that this observation of the Supreme Court in Dhodha
House forms an essential part of the ratio decidendi of the said decision.
48. At para. 46, while relying on the authoritative commentary of Mulla
on CPC, SB Sinha J., expounded on the meaning of the phrase ―carries on
business‖ and observed as under:
―46. The expression ―carries on business‖ and the expression ―personally
works for gain‖ connote two different meanings. For the purpose of carrying
on business only presence of a man at a place is not necessary. Such
business may be carried on at a place through an agent or a manager or
through a servant. The owner may not even visit that place. The phrase
―carries on business‖ at a certain place would, therefore, mean having an
interest in a business at that place, a voice in what is done, a share in the
gain or loss and some control thereover. The expression is much wider than
what the expression in normal parlance connotes, because of the ambit of a
civil action within the meaning of Section 9 of the Code. But it is necessary
that the following three conditions should be satisfied, namely,
―(1) The agent must be a special agent who attends exclusively to
the business of the principal and carries it on in the name of the
principal and not a general agent who does business for anyone
that pays him. Thus, a trader in the mofussil who habitually sends
grain to Madras for sale by a firm of commission agents who have
an independent business of selling goods for others on commission,

44 (2006) 9 SCC 41, paras. 47 and 51.

46
cannot be said to ‗carry on business‘ in Madras. So a firm in
England, carrying on business in the name of A.B. & Co., which
employs upon the usual terms a Bombay firm carrying on business
in the name of C.D. & Co., to act as the English firm's commission
agents in Bombay, does not ‗carry on business‘ in Bombay so as to
render itself liable to be sued in Bombay.
(2) The person acting as agent must be an agent in the strict sense
of the term. The manager of a joint Hindu family is not an ‗agent‘
within the meaning of this condition.
(3) To constitute ‗carrying on business‘ at a certain place, the
essential part of the business must take place in that place.
Therefore, a retail dealer who sells goods in the mofussil cannot be
said to ‗carry on business‘ in Bombay merely because he has an
agent in Bombay to import and purchase his stock for him. He
cannot be said to carry on business in Bombay unless his agent
made sales there on his behalf. A Calcutta firm that employs an
agent at Amritsar who has no power to receive money or to enter
into contracts, but only collects orders which are forwarded to and
dealt with in Calcutta, cannot be said to do business in Amritsar.
But a Bombay firm that has a branch office at Amritsar, where
orders are received subject to confirmation by the head office at
Bombay, and where money is paid and disbursed, is carrying on
business at Amritsar and is liable to be sued at Amritsar. Similarly
a life assurance company which carries on business in Bombay and
employs an agent at Madras who acts merely as a post office
forwarding proposals and sending moneys cannot be said to do
business in Madras. Where a contract of insurance was made at
place ‗A‘ and the insurance amount was also payable there, a suit
filed at place ‗B‘ where the insurance company had a branch office
was held not maintainable. Where the plaintiff instituted a suit at
Kozhikode alleging that its account with the defendant Bank at its
Calcutta branch had been wrongly debited and it was claimed that
that court had jurisdiction as the defendant had a branch there, it
was held that the existence of a branch was not part of the cause of
action and that the Kozhikode Court therefore had no jurisdiction.
But when a company though incorporated outside India gets itself
registered in India and does business in a place in India through its
agent authorised to accept insurance proposals, and to pay claims,
and to do other business incidental to the work of the agency, the
company carries on business at the place of business in India.‖
[See Mulla on the Code of Civil Procedure, 15th Edn., Vol. I, pp.
246-47.]‖

47
49. From the aforenoted, it is clear, therefore, that as per Dhodha House,
―carrying on business‖ at a place means:
a. Having an interest in a business at that place, a voice in what is
done, a share in the gain or loss and control thereover.
b. If it is contended that a corporation is carrying on business
through an agent, at the bare minimum, that agent must be a
special agent who attends exclusively to the business of the
principal and carries on the business in the name of the
principal.
c. The activities must also be an essential part of the business.
d. Most importantly, mere availability or sale of goods at a given
place does not amount to ―carrying on of business‖. This ruling
is the most essential part of the ratio decidendi in Dhodha
House.
50. The principles expounded in Dhodha House were aptly applied by
this Court in Sholay Media Entertainment and Anr. v. Yogesh Patel and
Ors.
45 In this case, a suit was filed for trademark infringement along with
passing off. It was alleged that the defendant owned www.sholay.com,
which violated the plaintiff‘s mark. To establish jurisdiction, it was averred
that the defendant advertised SHOLAY, in a magazine called ―IT-Information Technology‖, which was circulated and sold in Delhi. The
magazine issue was also stated to be accompanied by a free CD, that directly
connected the user to the defendant‘s website. Relying on Dhodha House,
S. Ravindra Bhat J. (as his Lordship then was), found that goods being

45 2010 SCC OnLine Del 4738, per S. Ravindra Bhat J.

48
available, or even sold, in Delhi would not be sufficient to conclude that a
corporation ―carries on business‖ at a given place; the ―essential part of the
business‖ ought to take place there. The decision in Sholay Media
Entertainment, thus, represents a perfect and apt application of the Dhodha
House principles.
51. A departure from the position of law enunciated in Dhodha House
appears to have been made in World Wrestling Entertainment, Inc. v.
Reshma Collection & Ors.
46 The suit was filed for trademark and copyright
infringement, along with an action of passing off. The plaintiff-company
was incorporated in the State of Delaware, United States and all the
defendants resided in Mumbai. It was an admitted position that the
defendants did not carry on business within the territory of Delhi.
Jurisdiction was sought to be established by the plaintiff by relying upon
Sections 134(2) and 62(2) of the Trade Marks Act and Copyright Act. Upon
there arising a doubt as to the territorial jurisdiction of the Court, arguments
took place on this issue. In exercise of powers under Order VII Rule 10 of
the CPC, vide a detailed order47 dated 04.10.2013, the learned Single Judge
returned the plaint for want of territorial jurisdiction.
52. Para. 8 of the order of the learned Single Judge in WWE notes the
grounds on the basis of which jurisdiction of this Court was sought to be
invoked. It reads as under:
―8. In Paragraph 32 of the plaint, the plaintiff claims jurisdiction of this
court under Section 134(2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999 and Section 62(2)

46 2014 SCC OnLine Del 2031 (DB).
47 2013:DHC:5122.

49
of the Copyright Act, 1957 by claiming that the plaintiff carries on business
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court in the following manner –
i. The plaintiff‘s programmes consisting of its various characters are
broadcast at Delhi
ii. The plaintiff‘s products such as its merchandising goods and books are
available in Delhi
iii. The plaintiff‘s goods and services are sold to consumers in Delhi
through its websites which can be accessed in Delhi‖
53. From paras. 21 to 23, the Court painstakingly analysed the issue at
hand and concluded that the plaintiff, under the specialised provisions, is not
―carrying on business‖ within the territory of Delhi. It was observed that if
mere accessibility of a website was sufficient to constitute carrying on of
business, the plaintiff, could file a suit wherever its website was accessible.
Upon testing the purported presence of the plaintiff in India on the
touchstone of the principles laid down in Dhodha House, Vipin Sanghi J.
(as his Lordship then was) found that the plaintiff was not ―carrying on
business‖ at Delhi. Jurisdiction under Sections 134(2) of the Trade Marks
Act and 62(2) of the Copyright Act was, thus, not made out. Paras. 21 to 23
of the decision deserve to be reproduced and read as under:
―21. I may deal with the plaintiff‘s submission regarding territorial
jurisdiction on account of its website being accessible in Delhi first, as bulk
of the submissions have been advanced on this issue. Section 134(2) of the
Act uses the expression ―carries on business‖. The objective of inserting
Section 134(2) was to provide the plaintiff a convenient forum to file a suit
for infringement, as long as the plaintiff voluntarily resides, carries on
business or personally works for gain at such place. The said provision goes
beyond the normal rules laid down in the C.P.C. for vesting jurisdiction in
Courts. The test to be satisfied as regards the aspect of carrying on business
has been laid down in Dhodha House (supra). The sum and substance of the
criteria laid down in Dhodha House (supra) is that an essential part of the
plaintiff‘s business, coupled with an element of control exercised by the
plaintiff, must exist in such place where the plaintiff claims to be carrying
on business either on its own or through an exclusive agent.

50
22. The aforementioned criteria will be equally applicable to a party that
concludes transactions with its customers over the medium of internet as
opposed to a physical retail space. Internet is another medium which
provides audio-visual interactive facility for doing business. Merely because
it is a more advanced or superior medium, it does not impinge on the
determination of the issue whether the merchandiser is carrying on business
at a particular place. Prior to the advent of e-commerce over the internet,
and even now, business was and continues to be done over telephone and
fax. Merely because a person has embarked upon an ecommerce business
model, the tests which apply for determination of issues, such as – when and
where the contract is made, or whether the vendor carries on business at the
place where the merchandise may be sold, or service may be offered, would
not change and would be the same as apply to communications over
telephone and fax (see Bhagwan Goverdhandas Kedia Vs. Girdharilal
Parshottamdas & Co. AIR 1966 SC 543). In such cases, the plaintiff would
have to show that it actually has business interest at the relevant place; a
voice in what is done; a share in the gain or loss and some control thereover.
For example, the plaintiff could show that it has an exclusive C&F operator
in the forum State, or an exclusive dealer that stocks the goods or services of
the plaintiff in the forum State – to be delivered or shipped as per a
consumer‘s convenience. The plaintiff could show that it has a
registered/branch office to address a consumer‘s dissatisfaction over the
plaintiff‘s merchandise or services. The plaintiff can show that it has an
exclusive tie up with an agent to manufacture the plaintiff‘s goods for a
particular geography. In the present case, no essential part of the plaintiffs
business is carried on at Delhi. In fact, the plaintiff has not pleaded to
having any branch office or exclusive agent anywhere in India, let alone
Delhi. Merely because the website of the plaintiff is accessible in Delhi is
not sufficient to clothe this court with jurisdiction.
23. If the plaintiff‘s submission were to be accepted, it would mean that the
plaintiff could file a suit at any place which provides internet access to the
plaintiffs website, irrespective of the defendant‘s location, only on account
of the plaintiff‘s website being accessible in that State. The insertion of
Section 134(2) was with a view to enable the plaintiff to institute a suit
where it has actual and substantial business interest so that an additional
forum convenient to the plaintiff is available. If the intent of the legislature
had been to vest jurisdiction solely on account of accessibility of plaintiff‘s
website at the place of suing, then the same would have been expressly
provided for, especially keeping in mind plaintiff‘s submissions that the
legislature has recognized new media on account of various amendments.‖
54. The learned Single Judge in WWE had also dealt with the plaintiff‘s
grounds invoking jurisdiction under Section 20(c), which did not, ultimately,

51
become an issue before the Division Bench. However, the reasoning of the
learned Single Judge on this issue is exceptionally lucid and deserves to be
reproduced:
―28. …The broadcast of the plaintiff‘s programs in Delhi is also not
sufficient to confer jurisdiction. Just as mere advertisement in a journal or
paper is not sufficient to confer jurisdiction (See ONGC Vs. Utpal Kumar
Basu, 1994 (4) SCC 711). Similarly, broadcast of the plaintiff‘s programmes
is insufficient to vest jurisdiction in this Court.
29. The plaintiffs submission founded upon ―New Media‖ has no merit.
Wherever the legislature thought it necessary, it amended the laws to make
provision for the ―New Media‖. The Parliament has not amended the Act,
the Copyright Act, or the C.P.C even while amending other legislations, and
enacting the I.T. Act, in the manner as contended by Mr. Anand. The ―new
media‖, as aforesaid, does not change the basic concepts or legal principles
which apply to trade and commerce. They continue to remain the same as
applicable to the ―old media‖ i.e. telephone and FAX. Even otherwise, I am
of the view that the proposition advanced by Mr. Anand has no merit as, its
acceptance would lead to undue hardship to those resorting to e-commerce
and open them to multifarious litigations in every nook and corner of the
world where their goods or services may be sold over the internet, even
though they do not meet the requirements laid down in Banyan Tree (supra).
The same would stifle the growth of e-commerce.‖
55. This order of the learned Single Judge was assailed before the
Division Bench which allowed the appeal.
56. The Division Bench in WWE found that jurisdiction under the special
provisions can be invoked as the plaintiff was ―to a certain extent, carrying
on business‖ at Delhi. The facts that constituted carrying on of business
were firstly, customers in Delhi making offers to purchase merchandise and
goods. Secondly, these goods being confirmed/accepted by the plaintiff
through its website. Thirdly, money being paid from Delhi. The Court
opined that the availability of transactions through the website at a particular
place is virtually the same as a seller having shops in that place in the

52
physical world. As per the Division Bench, if there was a shop of the
plaintiff in Delhi from where it sold its various goods and services, ―it could
not be denied that the plaintiff carried on business in Delhi.‖ Para. 24 of the
said decision captures the reasoning and is extracted as under:
―24. But, we are not concerned with the question of cause of action between
the appellant/plaintiff and its customers in Delhi because the defendants are
not such customers and they are, in any event, all residents of Mumbai.
What we are examining is whether the third condition specified in Dhodha
House (supra) is satisfied or not. In other words, if the contracts and/or
transactions entered into between the appellant/plaintiff on the one hand and
its customers are being concluded in Delhi, can it not be said that the
essential part of the business of the appellant/plaintiff, insofar as its
transactions with customers in Delhi are concerned, takes place in Delhi?
The offers are made by customers at Delhi. The offers are subject to
confirmation/acceptance of the appellant/plaintiff through its website. The
money would emanate or be paid from Delhi. Can it not then be considered
that the appellant/plaintiff is, to a certain extent, carrying on business at
Delhi? In our view, it would be so. Because of the advancements in
technology and the rapid growth of new models of conducting business over
the internet, it is possible for an entity to have a virtual presence in a place
which is located at a distance from the place where it has a physical
presence. The availability of transactions through the website at a particular
place is virtually the same thing as a seller having shops in that place in the
physical world. Let us assume for the sake of argument that the
appellant/plaintiff had a shop in Delhi from where it sold its various goods
and services. In that case, it could not be denied that the plaintiff carried on
business in Delhi. This is apart from the fact that the appellant/plaintiff may
also have been regarded as having voluntarily resided in Delhi. When the
shop in the ―physical sense‖ is replaced by the ―virtual‖ shop because of the
advancement of technology, in our view, it cannot be said that the
appellant/plaintiff would not carry on business in Delhi.‖
57. Quite a few issues arise concerning the reasoning adopted by the
Division Bench in WWE. They shall be cumulatively considered after taking
note of the decisions in Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home.
58. The decision of the Supreme Court in Sanjay Dalia, originally arose
by the filing of CS (OS) 527/2007 before this Court. The plaintiff in the said

53
case was a non-profit-making company having its registered office at
Mumbai. The plaintiff claimed that it was established for the purposes of
monitoring, protecting, and enforcing the rights, interests, and privileges of
its members (comprising authors, composers, and publishers of literary
and/or musical works), as well as on behalf of the members of other sister
concerns who are owners of copyrights in their literary and musical works.
Defendant no. 1 was the Chief General Manager of defendant no. 2-Cineline
Cinemas. Defendant no. 2 was claimed to have 33 screens up and running in
various locations and 97 screens in the making.
59. It was alleged in the plaint that the defendants had been indulging in
continuous violation of the rights of the plaintiff by communicating to the
public literary and musical works from the plaintiff‘s repertoire without
having obtained a license from the plaintiff. An objection of territorial
jurisdiction was raised on the ground that the entire cause of action had
arisen in Mumbai. Both the plaintiff and the defendant no. 2 were also stated
to have their principal offices in Mumbai. The assignment deeds, which
formed the fulcrum of the plaintiff‘s case, were also executed in Mumbai by
artists residing in Mumbai. The plaintiff, however, relying on Section 62(2),
claimed that it was ―carrying on business‖ in Delhi through its branch
office, and thus was entitled to maintain its suit at Delhi.
60. A novel argument was put forth by the learned senior counsel48
appearing for the defendants therein. He submitted that the explanation to
Section 20(a) of the CPC ought to be read into Section 62(2) of the
Copyright Act and Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. Per contra, the

48 Mr. Rajiv Nayyar.

54
learned senior counsel appearing for the plaintiff submitted, while relying
upon the Joint Committee deliberations with respect to the Copyright Bill,
1955, and the decision of the Supreme Court in the case of Exphar SA,
49
that the explanation to Section 20(a) of the CPC cannot be incorporated into
Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act.
61. Badar Durrez Ahmed J., sitting as a Single Judge of our Court in
Sanjay Dalia vide order50 dated 31.08.2007 accepted the contentions of the
defendants and read into Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act, the explanation
appended to Section 20(a) of the CPC. In categorical terms, the Court found
that ―merely having a branch office at Delhi would not mean that the
plaintiff carries on business in Delhi. The plaintiff would be deemed to carry
on business at a branch office only if cause of action had arisen in Delhi‖.
62. The plaintiff preferred an intra-court appeal which was dismissed vide
order51 dated 19.11.2008. Notable observations were made by the Division
Bench concerning the jurisdictional avenues available to a plaintiff and
when, generally, they can be invoked and curtailed. These observations are
discussed in the later part of this judgment. Specifically, on the appeal which
was before the Division Bench, at the outset, Mr. A.S. Chandhiok, learned
senior counsel with his usual fairness to the Court, had accepted that no part
of the cause of action had arisen at Delhi. His contention, however, was that
Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act does not require the accrual of cause of
action at the branch office, in order to constitute ―carrying on business‖. The
Division Bench noted that while the explanation is absent in the text of

49 (2004) 3 SCC 688.
50 2007:DHC:1031.
51 2008:DHC:3060-DB.

55
Section 62(2), the CPC was the ―grundnorm, the ethos and essence of which
percolates through all other statutes.‖ While affirming the view of the
learned Single Judge, the appeal came to be dismissed by the Division
Bench. This order came to be assailed before the Supreme Court in Sanjay
Dalia.
52
63. The Supreme Court interpreted the additional option available to a
plaintiff under Sections 62 and 134(2) of the Copyright Act and Trade
Marks Act respectively. The Court held that the option is subject to the
caveat that if the plaintiff resides, carries on business or personally works for
gain at a place where the cause of action has also arisen, the suit should be
filed at that place and not at some other places where the plaintiff may have
a branch office, etc. The Supreme Court, thus, incorporated the explanation
appended to Section 20 of the CPC into Section 62 of the Copyright Act and
Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. The decision of the Division Bench
and the learned Single Judge were, resultantly, affirmed.
64. In Ultra Home Construction Pvt. Ltd. v. Purushottam Kumar
Chaubey & Ors.,
53 a Division Bench of this Court headed by Badar Durrez
Ahmed J. after relying upon Sanjay Dalia and Patel Roadways iterated two
significant propositions:
First, that for the purposes of Section 20 of the CPC, when the principal
office and subordinate office of a company are situated at different places,
the company would be deemed to carry on business either at the principal
office, or at its subordinate office if cause of action has accrued at that place,

52 (2015) 10 SCC 161.
53 2016 SCC OnLine Del 376.

56
but not at both places.
54 Second, the application of Sanjay Dalia leads to
four distinct factual scenarios, and the additional option under Sections
134(2) and 62(2) of the Trade Marks Act and the Copyright Act,
respectively, can be exercised only in the following manner:
S.

65. Before delving, further, a brief analysis may be made, concerning the
judgment of WWE, the issues with its reasoning, and the authoritative force
it carries post the decision of the Supreme Court in Sanjay Dalia and in
Ultra Home.
66. Firstly, at the outset it may be noted that the Division Bench‘s
conclusion in WWE that ―it could not be denied that the plaintiff carried on
business in Delhi‖ if it had a shop from where it sold its goods and services,
goes against the express words of Dhodha House. At para. 51 the Supreme
Court in Dhodha House had observed ―…it is possible that the goods
manufactured by the plaintiff are available in the market of Delhi or they
are sold in Delhi but that by itself would not mean that the plaintiff carries
on any business in Delhi‖. It is clear that the sale of goods, in and of itself,
either through a physical store or a ―virtual store‖, would not be sufficient to

54 2016 SCC OnLine Del 376, para. 12.

57
hold that a corporation ―carries on business‖ at that place. Depending upon
the nature of the business engaged in and the kind of activity being
undertaken at a given place, it needs to be determined, whether, among other
things, ―the essential part of the business‖ is conducted at that place.
67. Secondly, while placing reliance on para. 46(3) of Dhodha House, the
Division Bench had found that the case of the plaintiff was akin to the
example given by SB Sinha J. of a Bombay firm having a branch office at
Amritsar, where orders were received, subject to confirmation by the head
office at Bombay and where money was paid and disbursed. Since in
Dhodha House, the branch office at Amritsar, in the example, was
considered as a place where business was carried on, the Division Bench, by
the use of analogy, found that the plaintiff-corporation in WWE was
―carrying on business‖ in Delhi. The example taken note of, however, does
not appear to be applicable to the plaintiff‘s facts; particularly, when
examined in light of the other examples given in Dhodha House. These
examples noted by SB Sinha J. at para. 46(3) of the said decision are:
a. A retail dealer who sells goods in the mofussil cannot be said to
―carry on business‖ in Bombay merely because he has an agent
in Bombay to import and purchase his stock for him. He cannot
be said to carry on business in Bombay unless his agent made
sales there on his behalf;
b. A Calcutta firm that employs an agent at Amritsar who has no
power to receive money or to enter into contracts, but only
collects orders which are forwarded to and dealt with in
Calcutta, cannot be said to do business in Amritsar;

58
c. Similarly, a Life Assurance Company which carries on business
in Bombay and employs an agent at Madras who acts merely as
a Post Office forwarding proposals and sending moneys cannot
be said to do business in Madras;
d. But a Bombay firm that has a branch office at Amritsar, where
orders are received subject to confirmation by the head office at
Bombay, and where money is paid and disbursed, is carrying
on business at Amritsar and is liable to be sued at Amritsar; and
e. When a company though incorporated outside India gets itself
registered in India and does business in a place in India through
its agent authorized to accept insurance proposals, and to pay
claims, and to do other business incidental to the work of
agency, the company carries on business at the place of
business in India.
68. A mapping of the above examples to the facts of WWE would reveal
that a website that has its server outside India, and allows customers in Delhi
to buy products is closer to examples (b) and (c) than example (d), which the
Division Bench took into consideration. It is again important to clarify that
mere conclusion of commercial transactions, in and of itself, is not sufficient
to satisfy the requirement of ―carrying on business‖. This formed part of the
ratio decidendi of Dhodha House, and was expressly mentioned at paras. 47
and 51 of the decision.
69. The view that mere conclusion of transactions in WWE‘s facts could
not constitute ―carrying on business‖ is also strengthened by the nature of
business engaged in by the plaintiff therein. Incorporated in the State of

59
Delaware, USA, the plaintiff-company was engaged in the development,
production and marketing of television programming, live events and the
licensing and sale of branded consumer products featuring its brand. The
plaintiff was stated to have offered a variety of memorabilia including, but
not limited to, apparel, posters, calendars, fact books, sunglasses, water
bottles, key-rings, DVDs, etc. It was stated to have more than 200 licenses
distributing its products in 86 countries, including India. It had been averred
by the plaintiff that it licensed out its well-known trademarks and the
images/characters to different parties for use on merchandise in a wide range
of products.
70. This being the expanse, nature and breadth of the plaintiff‘s business,
a simple purchase of a product/service via the internet could not have
constituted ―carrying on business‖ at Delhi. A perusal of the judgment
would also reveal that while the activity of entering into transactions has
been described in detail by the Division Bench the issue of whether this
activity is an ―essential part‖ of the plaintiff‘s business has not been
analysed. Indeed, owing to this, the conclusion of the Division Bench was
that the plaintiff ―to a certain extent‖ carries on business in Delhi. Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act and Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act,
however, do not allow a plaintiff to sue at a place where it carries on
business ―to a certain extent‖.
71. Thirdly, the conclusion arrived at by the Division Bench would allow
a corporation not having any presence in India to sue a defendant at all
places where it engages in online website sales with customers at a given
place. A foreign corporation can, relying on the additional avenues under

60
Sections 62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright and Trade Marks Act,
respectively, sue a defendant belonging to Ladakh at the courts in
Kanyakumari, if the plaintiff has made such sales at Kanyakumari. Such an
interpretation would allow the additional option, granted to a plaintiff under
the specialised statutes, to be used as a means to harass a defendant and
indulge in forum shopping.
72. While analysing the decisions rendered on this issue and the
applicable statutory regime, this Court is conscious of the settled principle
that what binds the Court is not every line and observation made in the
judgment but the ratio decidendi of the decision. It is the legal principle on
the basis of which a conclusion is arrived at and an order is given, that is to
be followed by Courts.
73. The defendants were residents of Mumbai and the plaintiff was a
multinational company having ample resources and wealth. The additional
options under the Trade Mark and Copyright Act were intended to, as noted
in the Joint Committee report55, provide relief to authors, who were deterred
from instituting infringement proceedings because the Court was at a
considerable distance from their place of ordinary residence.
74. The consequence of there not being a place where the plaintiff is
―carrying on business‖ for the purposes of the special legislations would
simply mean that the plaintiff has to file a suit under Section 20. A plaintiff-corporation like the one in WWE would face no difficulty, at all, by

55 Published in the Gazette of India dated 23.11.1956.

61
instituting the suit at the place where the defendant resides, carries on
business, or personally works for gain.
75. Considering the discussion above, the decision in WWE would not
govern the field on this issue, and would have to be restricted to its own
peculiar and unique facts. Such facts may possibly exist in a case dealt with
at paras. 77-78 below.
76. What remains of Badar Durrez Ahmed J.‘s opinion in WWE after his
own subsequent opinion in Ultra Home and the decision of the Supreme
Court in Sanjay Dalia, which in turn affirmed his Lordship‘s56 decision
rendered by him as a Single Judge, may now be examined. Two situations
may be considered:
77. Firstly, where the plaintiff is entering into commercial transactions,
through the means of internet, with customers only within the jurisdiction of
one court. And secondly, where the plaintiff is entering into commercial
transactions, through the means of internet, with customers within the
jurisdiction of multiple courts.
78. In the first case, if the principles in WWE are applied, a deemed
physical office would be created at the sole place where commercial
transactions are getting concluded. Since that would be the sole office,
applying the dictum in Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, the plaintiff-company
shall be deemed to carry on business at that given place, and jurisdiction
under the special provisions may be made out. This, however, shall be rare
as it would be rather abnormal for an international corporation not having

56 Badar Durrez Ahmed J.

62
any presence in India to have transactions only within the jurisdiction of one
court.
79. In order to bring oneself within this first category, a plaintiff may
deliberately omit to plead that transactions are also getting concluded within
the jurisdiction of other courts. In such a case, a given court may put suitable
questions to the plaintiff, inter alia, under Section 168 of the Bharatiya
Sakshya Adhiniyam, 202357. If it is found that the plaintiff had cleverly
attempted to conceal from the court, facts which had a bearing on
jurisdiction, he need not, it is trite law, be heard on merits at all. He who
comes to the court seeking an equitable relief must come with clean hands
disclosing all that is material.
80. However, if, after being honest and fair to the Court, a company is
found to be concluding contracts only within the jurisdiction of one Court,
jurisdiction may be made out under the specialised statutes, under the WWE
reasoning. This may also, arguably, amount to specifically targeting a given
forum state as per the standard in Banyan Tree,
58 and resort to the
additional forum may, perhaps, not even be required.
81. In the second category, which would be most cases, transactions
would get concluded within the jurisdiction of multiple forum courts. As per
the ruling in WWE itself, there would be multiple deemed physical stores,
all at the same footing, spread across multiple jurisdictions. Amongst these
stores, there would be no ―principal office‖. Resultantly, these sub-offices

57 ―BSA‖.
58 2009 SCC OnLine Del 3780.

63
would be deemed to be ―carrying on business‖ only when cause of action
(as per the standard in Banyan Tree) accrues at that place.
82. Post the decisions in Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, the mere
presence of a store at a given place without the accrual of cause of action,
cannot be considered to be ―carrying on business‖. As was noted by Badar
Durrez Ahmed J. himself as a Single Judge in Sanjay Dalia,
59 ―merely
having a branch office at Delhi would not mean that the plaintiff carries on
business in Delhi. The plaintiff would be deemed to carry on business at a
branch office only if cause of action had arisen in Delhi‖.
83. If it can be established that cause of action as per the Banyan Tree
standard had accrued at the place where the deemed physical stores exist, a
basis to sue, under Section 20(c) of the CPC, would already exist. A resort to
the specialised provisions again may not be required. Thus, for an
overwhelming number of cases, the decision in WWE, post Sanjay Dalia
and Ultra Home, can no longer be made applicable.
84. It may be argued that under certain circumstances the additional
option to sue under Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act and Section
62(2) of the Copyright Act would not, meaningfully, exist for a foreign
company. When could, arguably, such a case arise? Where an international
conglomerate, that has no physical presence in India, wishes to protect its IP
rights, which are purportedly infringed by website sales in India, within the
jurisdiction of multiple courts in India. Inconvenience to such a plaintiff by

59 Which ultimately came to be affirmed both by the Division Bench and the Supreme Court.

64
an insistence to sue under the conventional Section 20 grounds, does not
seem to be a real possibility.
85. Therefore, no real prejudice would be caused if the option, which was
intended to provide small artists in India the opportunity to sue for their
intellectual property, is not made available to an international giant. An
interpretation to the contrary would allow a company to harass a defendant
by filing suits, at any one of the multiple places where its online sales are
taking place. The additional option intended to redress the difficulties of
artists cannot be interpreted in a way that allows the plaintiff to erode the
foundations of conventional jurisdictional rules. In this connection, the
following dictum of CK Thakker J. in Laxman Prasad60 may be taken note
of:
―Section 20 has been designed to secure that justice might be brought as
near as possible to every man‘s hearthstone and that the defendant should
not be put to the trouble and expense of travelling long distances in order to
defend himself.‖61
86. An unmodified and wholesale application of the rule in WWE would
place the defendant be at the mercy of the plaintiff. This would, as was
pithily observed by the Coordinate Bench ―through the very concept of
territorial jurisdiction of courts to the winds.‖
62
87. Thus, owing to WWE being in the teeth of the ratio of Dhodha
House, and after the decisions in Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, no longer
being workable, the decision of the Division Bench no longer governs the
issue.

60 (2008) 1 SCC 618.
61 (2008) 1 SCC 618, para. 35.
62 CS (Comm) 904/2026, judgment dated 25.08.2026, para. 52.

65
88. The decision of the Division Bench in Blueberry Books & Ors. v.
Google India Pvt. Ltd.,
63 does not disturb the conclusion reached above. In
the said case, the Division Bench sat in appeal over an order of the learned
Single Judge, accepting the interim applications made by some of the
defendants, whereby they were directed to be deleted from the array of
parties in the plaintiff‘s copyright infringement suit.
89. The suit filed by the plaintiff in Blueberry Books claimed that the
defendants were infringing the copyrighted work of the plaintiff through
internet applications across several platforms and website applications. At
the outset, it was noted that the plaintiff was a Delhi based firm. On this
short ground, it was found that jurisdiction under Section 62(2) of the
Copyright Act was made out.64 In relation to an argument made that
jurisdiction of this Court was ousted as infringing activity took place outside
borders of India and in USA alone, it was observed that once jurisdiction
under Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act was made out, it was not to be
additionally proven, that the infringing activity also took place within Delhi.
The observations made by the Division Bench at para. 19 were made in
passing, and as noted in the paragraph itself, were in arguendo. They do not
constitute binding precedent.
90. In Allied Blenders & Distillers Pvt. Ltd. v. Agribiotech Industries
Ltd.,
65 a suit was instituted for trademark and copyright infringement along
with a claim for passing off, for its mark and artwork ―OFFICERS
CHOICE‖ and ―OFFICERS CHOICE BLUE‖, registered in favour of the

63 2016:DHC:4326-DB.
64 2016:DHC:4326-DB, para. 14.
65 2020 SCC OnLine Del 2861.

66
plaintiff. Jurisdiction of Delhi, under Section 20(a) of the CPC was invoked.
It was submitted that the defendant ―carries on business‖ at Delhi through
its office bearing the address of Beetal House, New Delhi.
66 This address, it
was argued by counsel therein, was also indicated to be an address of the
defendant on its own website. While hearing the plaintiff‘s application under
Order XXXIX Rule 1 and 2, for ad-interim reliefs, a jurisdictional objection
was raised by the defendant.
91. The Court found that at the ad-interim stage, the website of the
defendant, containing the Beetal House Address under the ―Contact Us‖
page, was sufficient to prima facie conclude that the defendant carried on
business at Delhi. The analysis provided for in Dhodha House for
determining whether a corporation ―carries on business‖ was found to
require completion of pleadings and evidence, and thus, was not delved into.
For ease of analysis, the ―Contact Us‖ page of the defendant therein is
extracted as under:

92. While it is true that what was before the court was simply the plaint
and along with it a document showing the ―Contact Us‖ page of the
defendant, on the basis of that material as well, the Dhodha House test, if
applied, would have led to a different conclusion. The ―Contact Us‖ page
showed two addresses of the defendant. The first was the address to the
defendant‘s registered office and the second was the address to an office
which bore the heading ―REGISTRARS‖. It would, ex facie, indicate that
the Beetal House Address was that of the registrars and not the corporation.
Even otherwise, even if the address of the registrars was to be considered as
that of the corporation, what was ultimately before the court were two
offices.
93. In such a situation, naturally, under the explanation to Section 20 of
the CPC, the defendant-company shall not be considered as being one with a
sole office. It became essential, therefore, to determine where its principal
place of business was and where was its sub-ordinate office. It was not
brought to the notice of the Coordinate Bench that in Sanjay Dalia, itself,
the Supreme Court noted that ordinarily, the residence of a company would

68
be where its registered office is.67 The controlling power, also, as a fact,
generally, was found to be exercised at the registered office, and that office,
is, therefore, the principal place of business.68
94. The defendant in Allied Blenders as per the plaint, was not selling its
goods in Delhi. The compliance officer, as the webpage of the defendant
narrated, was also situated at the registered office i.e., at Jaipur, Rajasthan.
In such a situation, even by a prima facie consideration of the material, it
would appear that the principal place of business was at the registered Jaipur
office. The Beetal House Address at Delhi would then be a sub-ordinate
office. For the purposes of Section 20(a), such a place would carry on
business, only when cause of action also accrued at that place. It would be
difficult, therefore, to hold, even while considering the plaint alone, that the
defendant was ―carrying on business‖ at the Beetal House Address.
95. Lastly, it may also be highlighted that the decision of Allied Blenders
& Distillers Pvt. Ltd., was rendered while adjudicating an ad-interim
interlocutory prayer of the plaintiff. The Court did not render any final
decision on the rights and obligations of the parties. It was merely an ad-interim order subject to the outcome, not merely of the suit, but also of the
interim injunction application. The precedential value of such a judgment is,
therefore, doubtful. In any case, the decision of Allied Blenders & Distillers
Pvt. Ltd., is not a binding precedent and turns on its own unique facts.
96. In Cable News Networks INC v. CTVN Calcutta Television Network
Pvt. Ltd.,
69 a company incorporated in Delaware, US, with its headquarters

67 Morgan Stanley Mutual Fund v. Kartick Das, (1994) 4 SCC 225.
68 (2015) 10 SCC 161, para. 16.

69
in Atlanta, Georgia, filed a suit before this Court, primarily, for trademark
infringement along with a claim for passing off. There was no subordinate
office that the plaintiff had within the jurisdiction of Delhi. The defendant
had its registered office at Kolkata. The plaintiff‘s case was that the
defendant conducted its business under the name Uttar Bangla and
commenced using the name Calcutta News, with the acronym CN only in
2014. The design of the defendant‘s logo with C and N, joined at the bottom,
was contended to approach the plaintiff‘s logo so close as to create
confusion.
