97. The Court observed that the defendant carried on business within the
territorial limits of Delhi. Para. 114 of the said decision contains the
reasoning and reads as under:
―114. Viewed thus, it is clear that the averments in the present plaint, as
enumerated in para 42.2.15 supra, sufficiently assert that the defendant
carries on business within the jurisdiction of this Court. The defendant is
engaged in the business of dissemination of news, over its news channel.
There is a specific assertion, in para 49(a) of the plaint, that the defendant
carries on business within the territory of this Court through its website,
social media pages and YouTube channels. How it does so, cannot be
examined under Order VII Rule 10 of the CPC. Further, para 49 goes on to
assert, in so many words, that the defendants are offering news services
under the impugned logos within the territory of this Court. The business of
the defendant being offering of news services, this assertion is a complete
answer to the submission, of the defendant, that the suit is bad for want of
territorial jurisdiction and has, therefore, to be returned under Order VII
Rule 10.‖
98. The registered office of the defendant in the said case was at Kolkata.
As per Sanjay Dalia, specifically para. 16, the defendant-company would be
deemed to have its principal office at Kolkata. Given that mere sale of goods

69 2023 SCC OnLine Del 2436.

70
cannot constitute carrying on of business, similarly, mere display of news
could also not have attracted Section 20(a) of the CPC. The essentiality
analysis required under Dhodha House was not sufficiently pressed before
the learned Single Judge. Similar observations are to be made concerning
the conclusion at para. 118 of the said judgment that the plaintiff carried on
business at Delhi because its pan-India news channel was viewed in Delhi.
99. In Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd. v. Veda Seed Sciences Pvt.
Ltd.,
70 the plaintiff had filed a suit for infringement of its registered
trademark along with a claim for passing off. The case against the defendant
was that while a Marketing Agreement was entered into between the parties,
under which the defendant was permitted to use the plaintiff‘s mark, the use
undertaken by the defendant was in violation and outside the scope of the
said agreement. The jurisdiction paragraph in the plaint is reproduced at
para. 3.2 of the judgment which reads as under:
―3.2. …
JURISDICTION
50. The present suit is a consolidated suit for infringement and/or passing
off three Plaintiff's Subject Marks, by the same Defendant, arising out of the
same course of transaction. This Hon'ble High Court has jurisdiction to
entertain the present suit under Section 134(2)3of the Trade Marks Act,
1999 since the Plaintiff's registered address is within the jurisdiction of this
Hon'ble Court, i.e., New Delhi, and the Plaintiff ordinarily carries on
business in New Delhi. The two registered marks asserted in the present suit
has been registered with the Delhi Trademarks Registry. Further, the seed
packets of the impugned goods of the Defendant are available on various e-commerce platforms like India Mart etc., which are available for access
within New Delhi, and in any event, are dynamic websites that permits
communication and placement of orders. In addition, the relationship
between parties to the suit arises out of the Marketing Agreement executed
in New Delhi. As such, this Hon'ble High Court of Delhi has territorial

70 2025 SCC OnLine Del 8727.

71
jurisdiction to entertain the present suit. The suit is valued beyond the
minimum pecuniary jurisdiction of this Hon'ble Court and is also a
commercial suit within the meaning of the Commercial Courts Act, 2015.‖
100. The Court found that Delhi courts had territorial jurisdiction to
maintain and entertain the plaint on quite a few grounds, one of which
deserves consideration under the present heading.
101. The Division Bench concluded that the mere fact of the defendant‘s
product being available for sale on an e-commerce website is sufficient to
satisfy the requirement of Section 20(a) i.e., of the defendant carrying on
business within the territory of Delhi. To arrive at this conclusion, the Court
relied upon WWE and what it considered to be its ratio, namely, that a
website allowing the purchase of a product is akin to a physical store of
brick-and-mortar.
102. The reasoning in WWE, has been questioned in this judgement at
paras. 66-71 and 76-87. It has also been detailed as to why it no longer
governs the field. Distinct from the merits of WWE, the applicability of
WWE to Kohinoor Seed Fields is also possibly doubtful. In WWE, the
plaint had averred that commercial transactions were being concluded at
Delhi. Per contra, no such averment was present in Kohinoor Seed Fields.
Even otherwise, unlike a case where a sole office of a corporation is present,
which in turn is deemed as the place where it is carrying on business, where
multiple offices are present, it is essential for the Court to inquire where the
principal place of business is.
103. Additionally, in WWE, the website on which the transactions were
getting concluded belonged to the plaintiff itself, while in Kohinoor Seed

72
Fields, it were third-party marketplaces. Thus, even assuming WWE to be
the law governing the issue raised in Kohinoor Seed Fields, its reasoning of
transactions getting concluded being akin to sales taking place at a physical
store cannot apply to Kohinoor Seed Fields as the website itself was not of
the individual whose store was sought to be created. Thus, owing to it not
being pointed out to the Division Bench that WWE on facts may not be
applicable, and that WWE was and is no longer the authority holding the
field, the decision in Kohinoor Seed Fields, in turn, is not treated to be
binding precedent on this issue.
104. Relying on Kohinoor Seed Fields, three decisions were rendered
which are Raju Kumar v. Vinod Sah,
71 Sauss Home Products Pvt. Ltd. v.
Reckitt Benckiser India Pvt. Ltd.,
72 and ITC Ltd. v. Adyar Gate Hotels
Ltd.73 Each of these three cases involves the application of the rule laid
down in Kohinoor Seed Fields.
105. The material portion of Raju Kumar reads as under:
―20. Section 20 CPC specifically empowers a plaintiff to sue a defendant
before any court within which the defendant principally carries on its
business or where the whole or part of the cause of action has arisen. As we
have held in our decisions in Diamond Modular and Kohinoor-II,
prominently following the earlier Division Bench in World Wrestling
Entertainment, in the e-commerce age, every virtual platform over which the
defendant's goods or services would be available operates as a brick and
mortar store present in every location where that website is accessible.
Though, in Banyan Tree Holding, the Court had held that it is necessary to
show conclusion of a commercial transaction over the said website, that
position now does not survive after the decision in World Wrestling
Entertainment, which postulates that even the possibility of concluding a
commercial transaction would be sufficient. Tata Sons further dilutes the

71 2026 SCC OnLine Del 893.
72 2026 SCC OnLine Del 937, paras. 61-65.
73 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4852.

73
principle by holding that even the possibility of interacting over a website,
without any commercial element involved, would also be sufficient to
invoke territorial jurisdiction of a particular court within whose jurisdiction
the website is accessible.‖
106. The material portion of Sauss Home Products reads as under:
―61. On the aspect of territorial jurisdiction, the Division Bench of this
Court has already held in Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd. v. Veda Seed
Sciences Pvt. Ltd. that the availability of the products of the appellant on the
India Mart website would by itself confer jurisdiction on any Court within
which the website was accessible. ...
****
62. The law has, thus, travelled from Banyan Tree Holding to World
Wrestling Entertainment v. Reshma Collection and thereafter to Tata Sons.
In view of the fact that the appellant's product was admittedly available on
the India Mart platform, it could not be said that the appellant was not
carrying on business within the jurisdiction of this Court. The fact that the
Indiamart platform may have been a B2B21 platform rather than a B2C22
platform, cannot make any substantial difference to this legal position in
view of the law as it has developed on the aspect of territorial jurisdiction.‖
107. In Adyar Gate, the plaintiff-company having its registered office at
Kolkata, West Bengal filed a suit before this Court contending that the
defendant was infringing its trademark ―DAKSHIN‖ by using it at its
restaurant in Chennai. The plaintiff also contended that the defendant was
passing off its services as those of the plaintiff. An ad-interim injunction
was granted by the learned Single Judge of our Court but the main
injunction application was dismissed on grounds of territorial jurisdiction as
also on merits. Assailing this order of dismissal, the Division Bench was
moved.
108. The Division Bench in Adyar Gate clarified that the defendant had no
branch or outlet in Delhi and that it is not physically possible to avail the

74
services provided in the Chennai Dakshin restaurant from Delhi.74 The
Court, however, concluded that jurisdiction at Delhi was made out under
Section 20(a) of the CPC. Two independent reasons were given to arrive at
this conclusion. Firstly, the defendant was carrying on business in relation to
its restaurant in Chennai at Delhi and at every place where the Zomato page
could be accessed and a reservation could be booked using the ―Order
Online‖ link.75 Secondly, and additionally, it was found that jurisdiction was
also made out at Delhi because the defendant was responding to critical
reviews by its customers of its Chennai restaurant on the Zomato page,
which was accessible at Delhi.76 The development in the law taken note of at
para. 98 of the judgment reads as under:
―98. As per the law which has developed over the decisions in Banyan Tree
Holding, World Wrestling Entertainment, Ultra Home Construction, Tata
Sons, Kohinoor Seed Fields and Sauss Home Products, among others,
interactive accessibility is the key to determining territorial jurisdiction. The
accessibility must be interactive. In other words, a mere webpage which
provides information, and with which it is not possible to interact but only
to learn about the goods or services of a defendant, may not confer
jurisdiction over a Court from within whose jurisdiction the website can be
accessed. However, if the website is interactive and, more particularly, if it
is possible to conclude a commercial transaction over the website, territorial
jurisdiction would prima facie exist.‖
109. It can, thus, be seen that Kohinoor Seed Fields and its application in
Raju Kumar, Sauss Home Products, and Adyar Gate are premised on the
application of WWE to Section 20(a) of the CPC and of the purported
dilution of the principles in Banyan Tree in the subsequent decision of Tata
Sons. Paras. 179-186 of this judgment raise certain issues concerning the

74 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4852, para. 88.
75 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4852, para. 98.
76 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4852, para. 102.

75
decision of Tata Sons. It suffices to observe at this stage that there is no real
curtailment of the Banyan Tree principles in Tata Sons. The requirement of
concluding commercial transactions still remains a fundamental requirement
for conferring jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of the CPC. Insofar as the
decision of WWE is concerned, the analysis above has revealed that its
reasoning was at odds with Dhodha House and post the decisions in Sanjay
Dalia and Ultra Home, the judgment in WWE may not govern the field.
110. Cumulatively, it was not pointed out to the Division Bench that the
incorporation of WWE into Section 20(a) of the CPC, and the claim of
Banyan Tree having been diluted, may not be correct. Resultantly,
Kohinoor Seed Fields, and the decisions that follow it, do not constitute
binding precedent on this issue. It is further settled that a judgment is
precedent for what it decides, it can never be binding qua issues which were
not raised before it.
111. At last, the effect of the decision in Kohinoor as seen through Adyar
Gate, may be appreciated through the means of an example. Let there be a
defendant having its registered office at Kanyakumari and a sub-ordinate
office, through the means of an actual flourishing restaurant, at Delhi.
Assume that cause of action may have accrued at any place apart from
Delhi. If the plaintiff, in such a case, files a suit in Delhi, invokes Section
20(a) of the CPC, and contends that the defendant is carrying on its business
at Delhi, the plaint would be returned. The dictum of Patel Roadways would
allow the plaintiff to file at the place of its sub-ordinate office (in this the
restaurant at Delhi) only when cause of action has also accrued at that place.
Meaning, thereby, that a restaurant having a number of employees and a

76
considerable infrastructure, is not in and of itself sufficient to contend that
the defendant is carrying on business at Delhi.
112. But if instead of investing capital, time and human resource at Delhi,
a defendant were to have a website that allows people to make bookings at
its restaurant in Kanyakumari, as per the dictum of Kohinoor and Adyar
Gate, the defendant would be carrying on business at Delhi and become
susceptible to the jurisdiction of Delhi courts under Section 20(a) of the
CPC. It is this conundrum that gets first created by WWE, and the learned
Division Bench is made to apply in Kohinoor and Adyar Gate. As noted
above, owing to the errors in WWE not being pointed out to the Division
Benches, the said decisions are not binding on the issue raised.
113. It may also be noted that a Special Leave Petition, challenging the
decision in Kohinoor Seed Fields, is presently pending before the Supreme
Court, on which notice was issued vide order dated 20.02.2026.
114. A brief reference may also be made to the decision of the Division
Bench in Hindustan Unilever Limited v. Kwick Living (I) Pvt. Ltd.,
77
where, it was observed, inter alia, that a defendant‘s own declaration as to
its ―principal place of business‖ to the Goods and Services Tax authorities,
would be sufficient to satisfy the requirements of ―carrying on business‖
under Section 20(a) of the CPC. Though the ultimate conclusion of the
Division Bench was arrived at on the basis of additional grounds, this
specific finding, may be analysed in some depth.

77 2026 SCC OnLine Del 6703.

77
115. It was not pointed out to the Division Bench that the ―principal place
of business‖, under the Goods and Services Tax Act, 2017, is a defined
term.78 Section 2(89) of the Act reads as under:
―2. Definitions.
In this Act, unless the context otherwise requires:
(89) ―principal place of business‖ means the place of business specified as
the principal place of business in the certificate of registration‖
(Emphasis supplied)
116. Under Section 25 of the GST Act, 2017,
79 a corporation is required to
register itself in each State/Union Territory where it becomes liable for
registration. In every such State it has to provide a ―principal place of
business‖ which is within that State/Union Territory. The meaning of the
expression ―principal office‖ as it appears in the explanation to Section 20,
is, ordinarily, as per Sanjay Dalia, the place of its registered office. The
rationale being that this is generally the place where the controlling power of
the corporation is exercised. The principal office is also at a single place
regardless of the States in which a company may operate.
117. The import, requirements, and constituent elements, of what
constitutes a ―principal office‖ is fundamentally in contradistinction to what
is considered as the ―principal place of business‖ under the GST Act. The
declaration made by the defendant for the purposes of GST, in order to
comply with the requirement of the GST Act, cannot be incorporated to
determine where the corporation has its ―principal office‖ for the purposes

78 ―GST Act‖.
79 ―Section 25. Procedure for registration.
(1) Every person who is liable to be registered under section 22 or section 24 shall apply for registration in
every such State or Union territory in which he is so liable within thirty days from the date on which he
becomes liable to registration, in such manner and subject to such conditions as may be prescribed…‖

78
of Section 20(a) of the CPC. In Hindustan Unilever, the binding and
applicable statutory provisions, were not pointed out to the Division Bench.
Resultantly, the conclusion on this issue, may not have binding effect.
118. From the discussion above, the following conclusions may be drawn
concerning the interpretation of the expression ―carries on business‖:
a. The decision of WWE, does not govern the field concerning the
interpretation of the expression ―carries on business‖ owing to:
first, it being irreconcilable with the judgments in Sanjay Dalia
and Ultra Home; and second, it ignoring material portions of
Dhodha House.
b. The judgment in Kohinoor Seed Fields, which incorporates the
dictum of WWE, and applies it to the expression ―carries on
business‖ under Section 20(a) of the CPC, is not a controlling
authority on the interpretation of this expression. The decisions
of Raju Kumar and Sauss Home which apply the dictum in
Kohinoor Seed Fields, similarly, are not authorities on this
issue.
c. The law, therefore, concerning the meaning and import of the
expression ―carries on business‖ as it appears in Section 20(a)
and (b) of the CPC, Sections 134(2) and 62(2) of the Trade
Marks Act and Copyright Act, respectively, is governed by the
pronouncements of the Supreme Court in, inter alia, Patel
Roadways, Dhodha House and Sanjay Dalia, as also the
dictum of this Court in Ultra Home.

79
d. If a corporation has a sole office, it shall be deemed to carry on
business at that place of its sole office. If, however, there is
more than one office, it must be deciphered where the principal
place of business of that corporation is. This principal office
shall ordinarily be the place of its registered office. Once the
principal office is discovered, the other offices shall be the sub-ordinate offices/branch offices.
e. A corporation shall be deemed to carry on business at its sub-ordinate office if the cause of action for a given suit accrues at
that place. In such a scenario it shall not be open for the
company to sue a defendant at the place of its principal office,
where no cause of action has accrued.
C. “CAUSE OF ACTION, WHOLLY OR IN PART”
119. There are various dimensions to the expression ―cause of action,
wholly or in part‖, as it appears in Section 20(c) of the CPC. It is again
difficult to compartmentalise them in defined silos but for the ease of
exposition, they are being considered under the heads that follow.
(i) Quia Timet Action
120. The learned authors of the celebrated commentary Snell’s Equity,
80 at
para. 18-029, has observed the following in relation to a quia timet action:
―(2) Claimant’s rights not yet interfered with.
Although the claimant must establish his right, he may be entitled to an
injunction even though an infringement has not taken place but is merely

80 John McGhee, QC and Steven Elliott, QC, 34th Ed., Sweet and Maxwell, 2020.

80
feared or threatened; for ―preventing justice excelleth punishing justice‖. This
class of action, known as quia timet, has long been established, but the
claimant must establish a strong case; ―no one can obtain a quia timet order
by merely saying ‗Timeo‖‘. He must prove that there is an imminent
danger of very substantial damage, or further damage, e.g. by showing
that the threatened act ―is attended with extreme probability of irreparable
injury to the property of the plaintiffs, including also danger to their
existence‖. The court applies a two-stage test: ―(a) First, is there a strong
probability that, unless restrained by injunction, the defendant will act in
breach of the claimant‘s rights? (b) Secondly, if the defendant did an act in
contravention of the claimant‘s rights, would the harm resulting be so grave
and irreparable that, notwithstanding the grant of an immediate interlocutory
injunction (at the time of actual infringement of the claimant‘s rights) to
restrain further occurrence of the acts complained of, a remedy of damages
would be inadequate?‖
(Emphasis supplied)
121. In M/s Jawahar Engineering Co. and Ors. v. M/s. Javahar
Engineers Pvt. Ltd.,
81 the plaintiffs claiming to be the registered trade mark
holder of the mark ‗Javahar‘ filed a suit claiming that the defendants had
applied for the registration of the trade mark ‗Jawahar‘ in respect of diesel
oil engines, inter alia, for the Union Territories of Delhi and Chandigarh.
This application was advertised in the Trade Mark Journal.
122. After the completion of pleadings, a preliminary issue on territorial
jurisdiction was framed. The learned Single Judge decided the issue against
the defendant and found that requisite territorial jurisdiction to entertain the
suit was made out. It was held that jurisdiction was established, as an
advertisement in the Trade Marks Journal was published by the Registrar of
Trade Marks, Delhi. Upon an intra-court appeal being preferred, the
Division Bench held that ―the real point which gives the court jurisdiction is

81 1983 SCC OnLine Del 41.

81
not the place where the advertisement has appeared, but the fact that the
trade mark is sought for sale in Delhi amongst other places.‖
82
123. The Division Bench of this Court in the case of M/s PM Diesels Ltd.
v. M/s. Patel Field Marshal Inds. and Anr.,
83 had relied upon the decision
of Jawahar Engineering, to conclude that jurisdiction of Delhi was made
out by an application for registration of trade mark being filed by the
defendant. This order in PM Diesels was assailed before the Supreme Court,
which was decided by the decision in Dhodha House.
124. SB Sinha J. in Dhodha House while disagreeing with the reasoning in
Javahar Engineers observed that an application for trade mark registration
could either be rejected or allowed. The mere filing of an application before
the concerned registrar, therefore, does not affect jurisdiction nor does it
constitute a cause of action, wholly or in part, to maintain a suit.
125. In Pfizer Products, Inc. v. Rajesh Chopra and Ors.,
84 Badar Durrez
Ahmed J. observed that the decision in Javahar Engineers was merely
distinguished in Dhodha House and not disapproved. It was opined by the
Court that Javahar Engineers continued to be good law, even after the
decision in Dhodha House.
126. The decision of the Supreme Court in K. Narayanan and Anr. v. S.
Murali,
85 decisively settles the issue. The case arose from an action of
passing off filed by the plaintiff against the defendant on the ground that an

82 1983 SCC OnLine Del 41, para. 10.
83 1998 SCC OnLine Del 181.
84 2006 SCC OnLine Del 177. This decision has also been referenced by Dr. Sikri J. in LG Corporation &
Anr. v. Intermarket Electroplasters (P) Ltd. and Anr., 2006 SCC OnLine Del 1661 and was also approved
by the Division Bench in Pfizer Products Inc v. Rajesh Chopra, 2006 (32) PTC 301, para. 13.
85 (2008) 10 SCC 479.

82
application seeking pan-India registration of the trade mark was filed by the
defendant before the Trade Mark Registry at Chennai. The High Court of
Madras revoked the leave to sue, which was affirmed by the Division
Bench, and the matter reached the Supreme Court. Relying on Jawahar
Engineers, it was submitted before the Supreme Court that when an
injunction is sought, it is not necessary that the threat should have become a
reality before the injunction is granted or refused; it can even be sought for a
threat which is yet to materialise.
127. The Supreme Court held that a proposed registration of a mark which
may or may not be granted, will not confer a cause of action to the plaintiff,
whether the application for registration is filed by the plaintiff or the
defendant. The material portion of the judgment reads as under:
―11. Let us now consider the submissions of the learned counsel for the
parties. The learned counsel for the appellants argued before us that the
Division Bench of the High Court in its impugned judgment had taken a
contrary view from the judgment of the Division Bench of the High Court
of Delhi in Jawahar Engg. Co. v. Jawahar Engineers (P) Ltd. [1983 PTC
207 (Del)] which has held that the real point which confers jurisdiction on
the court is not the place where the advertisement is published but the
place for which the trade mark is sought for sale. It has also held that when
an injunction is sought, it is not necessary that the threat should have
become a reality before the injunction is granted or refused and it can even
be sought for a threat that is still to materialise.
****
13. The learned counsel for the appellants submitted that when the
respondent filed a trade mark application at the Trade Mark Registry at
Chennai, a threat was communicated regarding the use of the trade mark in
Chennai, and it was immaterial whether there was actual use or not and the
appellants would be entitled to an injunction (being a prohibitive remedy)
against the said mark. The learned counsel for the appellants finally argued
that the respondent had based its application for registration of the trade
mark on use of the mark throughout India without any geographical
limitation from 1-4-1995, which included the city of Chennai, which thus

83
entitled the appellants to file the suit at the High Court of Madras based on
the claims made in the trade mark application.
****
―20. Having heard the learned counsel for the parties and after carefully
examining the aforementioned judgment of the High Court and also of the
learned Single Judge, we do not find any infirmity in the judgment of
Division Bench of the High Court holding that before registration is
granted for the trade mark, there is no right in the person to assert that the
mark has been infringed and that a proposed registration which may or
may not be granted, will not confer a cause of action to the plaintiff,
whether the application for registration is filed by the plaintiff or the
defendant.‖
128. In Deepak Kumar Kheka v. Yogesh Kumar Jaiswal & Ors.,
86
a
registered holder of trademarks had filed a suit before the Commercial Court
at Saket seeking permanent injunction and certain other consequential
reliefs. It was an admitted position that no goods bearing the trade mark
were prevalent in the market. However, on the ground that ―a strong and
credible apprehension that the impugned goods bearing the impugned trade
marks (were) going to be infused into the markets‖ within the jurisdiction of
the Commercial Court at Saket, the suit was instituted as a quia timet action.
Relying on K Narayanan, the Commercial Court allowed the defendant‘s
application under Order VII Rule 11 and rejected the plaint on the ground
that it did not disclose a cause of action. This order was impugned before the
Division Bench of this Court.
129. It was canvassed before the Division Bench that the Supreme Court‘s
observations in K. Narayanan were confined only to cases of passing off.
Rejecting this contention, the Division Bench held:

86 2025:DHC:6176-DB

84
―18. What Mr. Bansal essentially seeks is that we should read the
reference to ―infringement‖, in para 20 of the judgment of the Supreme
Court as referring to passing off.
19. It goes without saying that this Court can hardly seek to rewrite a
judgment of the Supreme Court. Moreover, even in the opening sentence
of para 13 of the same decision, we find that the Court has recorded the
submission of the Counsel for the appellant before it held that the filing of
a trademark application by the respondent at the Trade Mark Registry at
Chennai amounted to a communication of a threat regarding the use of the
trademark at Chennai and it was immaterial whether there was actual use
or not. Clearly, these two observations, read together, indicate that the
Supreme Court is of the view that no application for injunction on the
ground of infringement of the trademark would lie where the defendant
has only applied for a registration of the mark.
20. When we read paras 13 and 20 in juxtaposition, it is clear that the
Supreme Court has held that no cause of action, to maintain a suit for
infringement, would lie merely on the defendant filing an application for
registration of a trade mark.‖
130. It is, thus, clear that the filing of an application for registration of
trade mark does not give rise to a cause of action for filing a quia timet suit
for infringement or passing off.
131. In Allied Blenders & Distillers Pvt. Ltd. v. R.K. Distillery Pvt. Ltd.,
87
a Division Bench of this Court dealt with an order of the learned Single
Judge returning the plaint for want of jurisdiction. The suit was filed by the
plaintiff for trademark and copyright infringement along with a claim for
passing off. The plaintiff‘s trademark in respect of whiskey was ―Officers
Choice‖, whereas the respondent‘s mark was ―Regular Choice‖. The
plaintiff did not have its registered office in Delhi and the defendant did not
reside in Delhi. The infringing goods were sold at Andhra Pradesh and not at
Delhi. The Court, while setting aside the order of the learned Single Judge,

87 FAO (OS) No. 251/2016.

85
held that the suit of the plaintiff was in the nature of a quia timet action
based on the apprehension that the defendant would start selling its whiskey
under the impugned mark and label ―Regular Choice‖ in Delhi.
132. For determining the question of territorial jurisdiction while
considering an application under Order VII Rule 10, it was re-iterated by the
Court in R.K. Distillery that the averments made in the plaint are to be taken
as true and correct. Considering the pleadings made by the plaintiff,
specifically at para. 28 in the plaint, it was found that the plaint could not be
returned. Para. 28 of the plaint reads as under:
―JURISDICTION:
28. This Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to try and entertain the
instant suit by virtue of Section 62 (2) of the Copyright Act, 1957 and
Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act 1999 as the Plaintiff being the owner
of the copyright and the registered proprietor of the trademark in issue has
its office located at A-61/5, Ground floor, G.T. Kamal Road Industrial Area,
Azadpur, Delhi. Moreover, the Plaintiff is also carrying on its business
within the territorial limits of this Hon‘ble Court in a variety of ways such
as (but not limited to) sale of its products under the relevant marks in New
Delhi. The scale of business and activity of the Defendant is not known at
present and it is apprehended as being highly likely that the product sold
under the impugned mark ―Regular Choice‖ will be made available in New
Delhi. As the scope and extent of the Defendant‘s business is not known at
present, it is believed that they have a reasonably large scale business
around India and it is therefore strongly apprehended that they will launch
the impugned product in New Delhi and would be available within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. It is submitted that the threat that the
Defendants will sell and / or offer for sale the impugned product within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court is credible and imminent. There is a
reasonable apprehension that the Defendant is likely to sell its products
bearing the impugned mark and label within the jurisdiction of this Hon'ble
Court. Thus, giving rise to a substantial and integral part of the cause of
action within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. This Hon‘ble Court
therefore has the necessary jurisdiction by virtue of Section 20 of the Code
of Civil Procedure, 1908.‖

86
133. Importantly, the Division Bench in R.K. Distillery further observed
that while the plaint‘s averments for adjudicating an application under Order
VII Rule 10 need to be considered as true and correct, while adjudicating an
application for interim injunction, relatively stricter scrutiny is warranted.
The defendant‘s written statement/reply to the interim injunction application
as also the documents placed by it on record, can be considered at this stage.
For the grant of interim injunction, a plaintiff is required to establish a prima
facie case that jurisdiction does in fact exist. Para. 22 of the said decision
reads as under:
―22. We must make it clear that the considerations with regard to territorial
jurisdiction in the context of Order VII Rule 10, CPC are entirely different
from those in the context of an application under Order XXXIX Rules 1 &
2, CPC or in the context of an issue framed in the suit with regard to
territorial jurisdiction. While in the backdrop of Order VII Rule 10, it is only
the averments contained in the plaint that have to be seen in the case of an
application for the grant of interim relief under Order XXXIX Rules 1 & 2,
the contentions of the defendant in the reply to this application as also those
contained in the written statement are required to be examined to arrive at a
prima facie view. So, while in the case of an Order VII Rule 10 CPC
application, the issue of jurisdiction is decided on the basis of what is stated
in the plaint and, that too, after assuming the statements to be correct, an
application under Order XXXIX Rules 1 & 2, CPC requires the examination
of the contentions of the defendants that may be contained in the written
statement and / or the reply to the application as also the other material
which may be placed by the defendant before the court. Here, there is no
question of considering the pleas of the plaintiff by way of a demurrer.
Furthermore, the standards would be higher when an issue with regard to
territorial jurisdiction is to be finally decided in the course of trial of the
suit. Thus, while for the purposes of an examination under Order VII Rule
10, CPC, the court may come to the conclusion that it has territorial
jurisdiction, this would not come in the way of the defendant in raising a
question as to territorial jurisdiction, both when the issue of temporary
injunction under Order XXXIX Rules 1 & 2, CPC is being considered and
when an issue as to territorial jurisdiction is being decided in the course of
trial of the suit. To put it differently, while a plaintiff may succeed in
demonstrating, for the purposes of Order VII Rule 10 CPC that this court
has territorial jurisdiction and that the plaint ought not to be returned, he

87
may fail in obtaining an order of interim injunction on the ground that the
plaintiff‘s entitlement is itself shaky because the issue of territorial
jurisdiction is highly debatable and prima facie not tenable. Therefore, the
decision of the court in putting down an objection of the defendant and in
rejecting the defendant‘s prayer for return of the plaint under Order VII Rule
10, CPC, would not come in the way of the defendant raising the question of
territorial jurisdiction, both as an objection to the grant of an interim
injunction as also at the time of decision of the issue of territorial
jurisdiction, if framed, at the time of trial of the suit.‖
134. Similarly, M/s Allied Blenders & Distillers Pvt. Ltd. v. Prag
Distillery Pvt. Ltd. and Anr.,
88 was a decision which arose from facts that
were largely similar to those in R.K. Distillery Pvt. Ltd.. The Division Bench
held ―the mere fact that the plaint contained an averment that there was
credible and imminent apprehension that the infringing product would be
launched by the defendants within the territorial jurisdiction of this court,
would by itself constitute a cause of action‖.
135. In Shree Nath Heritage Liquor Pvt. Ltd. and Anr. v. Octaga Green
Power and Sugar Co. Ltd. and Ors.,
89 the Court decided on the preliminary
issue framed by it on the question of territorial jurisdiction. The suit was
filed for design infringement and passing off action by defendant‘s
―GRENADE XXX RESERVE BOTTLE‖ rum product. Neither the plaintiff
nor the defendant resided within the jurisdiction of this Court. The
impugned product also was not sold in Delhi. Jurisdiction was pleaded
contending the suit to be a quia timet action on grounds that the defendant‘s
product is about to be launched in Delhi. Reliance was placed on the
decision of the Supreme Court in Kuldip Singh v. Subhash Chander Jain

88 FAO (OS) No. 49/2017.
89 2017 SCC OnLine Del 9137.

88
and Ors.,
90 where R.C. Lahoti J., while finding the suit in question therein to
be pre-mature, importantly observed:
―6. A quia timet action is a bill in equity. It is an action preventive in nature
and a specie of precautionary justice intended to prevent apprehended wrong
or anticipated mischief and not to undo a wrong or mischief when it has
already been done. In such an action the court, if convinced, may interfere by
appointment of receiver or by directing security to be furnished or by issuing
an injunction or any other remedial process. In Fletcher v. Bealey [(1885) 28
Ch D 688 : 54 LJ Ch 424 : 52 LT 541] , Mr Justice Pearson explained the law
as to actions quia timet as follows:
―There are at least two necessary ingredients for a quia timet action.
There must, if no actual damage is proved, be proof of imminent
danger, and there must also be proof that the apprehended damage
will, if it comes, be very substantial. I should almost say it must be
proved that it will be irreparable, because, if the danger is not proved
to be so imminent that no one can doubt that, if the remedy is
delayed the damage will be suffered, I think it must be shown that, if
the damage does occur at any time, it will come in such a way and
under such circumstances that it will be impossible for the plaintiff
to protect himself against it if relief is denied to him in a quia timet
action‖.
7. Kerr on Injunctions (6th Edn., 1999) states the law on ―threatened injury‖
as under:
―The court will not in general interfere until an actual nuisance has
been committed; but it may, by virtue of its jurisdiction to restrain
acts which, when completed, will result in a ground of action,
interfere before any actual nuisance has been committed, where it is
satisfied that the act complained of will inevitably result in a
nuisance. The plaintiff, however, must show a strong case of
probability that the apprehended mischief will in fact arise in
order to induce the court to interfere. If there is no reason for
supposing that there is any danger of mischief of a serious
character being done before the interference of the court can be
invoked, an injunction will not be granted‖.
(Emphasis supplied)

90 (2000) 4 SCC 50.

89
136. Relying on Kuldip Singh, in Shree Nath Heritage Liquor, this Court
held that even for a quia timet action, a plaintiff has to show that there is a
strong possibility of the defendants selling their product in Delhi, which is
likely to cause injury to its business and trade mark. Significantly, it was
noted that the defendant therein did not have an excise license for Delhi
which would enable it to sell the impugned product. The plaintiff, also, did
not plead that the defendant possesses an excise license which would allow
the defendant to sell the impugned product at Delhi. While stressing on the
fact that neither the plaintiff nor the defendant were in Delhi, the plaint was
found to be an attempt at forum shopping and an outcome of clever drafting.
Resultantly, the plaint was returned.
137. The standard of scrutiny applied by the Division Benches in Prag
Distillery Pvt. Ltd. and R.K. Distillery Pvt. Ltd. for a quia timet action was
far lesser. While the decision in Shree Nath Heritage Liquour could be
justified on the ground that it was passed on the preliminary issue which was
framed after the completion of pleadings, it seems more appropriate to view
it in the context of the additional issues which came to be raised before the
learned Single Judge. The decision of RC Lahoti J. in Kuldip Singh was not
noticed by the Division Benches, nor was the issue of the goods concerned
being regulated by the Excise Act, and requiring a license to sell, adverted
to. Both these aspects have a bearing on whether a cause of action for a quia
timet action was made out. A judgment, it is trite law, is a precedent for
what it decides and not what can be inferred from it. The issues not
presented before a Court can never be said to be decided by it.

90
138. A brief reference may also be made to the decision of Allied Blenders
& Distillers Pvt. Ltd. v. Agribiotech Industries Ltd.,
91 where jurisdiction
was sought to be invoked on the ground that the suit was in the nature of a
quia timet action and cause of action under Section 20(c) of the CPC has
been made out. It was found that ―the mere apprehension of the likelihood of
the defendant launching the allegedly infringing product within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Court, would confer jurisdiction‖.92 The
decision of Shree Nath Heritage, was not adequately canvassed before the
learned Single Judge. In Shree Nath Heritage, the court had observed that a
strong possibility of the defendants selling the impugned goods must be
established. For alcohol, which is an excisable commodity, a license to sell
must exist with a defendant for a quia timet action to be made out.
139. In Novartis AG and Anr. v. Zydus Healthcare Limited and Anr.,
93
a
Division Bench of this Court crystallised the manner in which an application
seeking outright rejection of a quia timet plaint is to be adjudicated. Paras.
50 and 51 of the said decision read as under:
―50. What has to be seen, therefore, in assessing whether a quia timet plaint
can be rejected outright under Order VII Rule 11, is
(i) whether the plaint alleges
(a) a deliberate expressed intention to engage in activity the
result of which would raise a strong possibility of
infringement,
(b) that the activity to be engaged in is imminent, and

91 2020 SCC OnLine Del 2861.
92 2020 SCC OnLine Del 2861, para. 42.
93 2022 SCC OnLine Del 4373.

91
(c) that, if the defendant were to engage in the activity, the
plaintiff would suffer substantial, if not irreparable,
prejudice,
(ii) that the claim of the plaintiff is not merely speculative, in the
hope that sufficient facts, to support the allegations, would be
gleaned on discovery during the course of trial, and
(iii) that the plaint contains cogent, precise and material recitals of
facts.
51. Nothing more is to be seen, when examining an Order VII Rule 11
challenge.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
140. From the discussion above, concerning quia timet actions, it can be
concluded that:
a. The cause of action for quia timet action accrues when the
defendant expresses a deliberate intention to engage in an
activity the result of which would raise a strong possibility of
infringement.
b. The activity to be engaged in must be imminent, and of such a
character that if the defendant were to engage in the activity,
the plaintiff would suffer substantial, if not irreparable,
prejudice.
c. For goods and services that are subject to regulatory control, in
determining whether a quia timet action is maintainable, it is
essential to consider whether the defendant holds the required
licences or permissions, statutory or otherwise, required for
their sale, use or any other commercial exploitation. For
instance, for the commercial sale of liquor, an excise license

92
under the State‘s specific Excise Act is required, for
manufacturing a pharmaceutical product, license under the
Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940 may be required, and for the
sale of insecticides a license under the Insecticides Act, 1968
may be required.
(ii) An Action for Passing Off
141. In Himachal Pradesh Horticulture Produce Marketing and
Processing Corp. Ltd. v. M/s. Mohan Meakin Breweries Ltd.,
94 the plaintiff
had filed a suit for infringement and passing off seeking, inter alia, a
permanent injunction and rendition of accounts. In order to establish
territorial jurisdiction of the Courts at Ludhiana, the plaintiff had averred
that apple juice manufactured by the defendant was sold with the plaintiff‘s
mark in Ludhiana.
142. The High Court of Punjab and Haryana placing reliance on the
opinion of Cornelius J. in Firm Bhagwan Dass v. Watkins Mayor and
Co.,
95 observed that cause of action wholly or in part, for a claim of passing
off, can be said to have arisen only when: first, the defendant has directly
made sales of the goods carrying the impugned trademark within the
jurisdiction of a given Court. Second, the sales are made by the defendant
not to an individual consumer but to a distributor or a wholesaler or a
retailer. Third, such sales are at a commercial scale.
143. The Court in Mohan Meakin opined that if this much was not insisted
upon, corporations can make a mockery of jurisdictional rules and big firms

94 1980 SCC OnLine P&H 297.
95 AIR 1947 Lah 289.

93
could file civil actions as per their own whims and fancies. Para. 9 of the
said decision is worth reproducing and reads as under:
―9. …If this much is not insisted upon, then the defendant can be dragged
into litigation in any part of the country by only adducing flimsy kind of
evidence that has been adduced in the present case by a company which has
its head office at Solan in Himachal Pradesh where the defendant too have
their head office and where it was convenient to the plaintiff to prosecute
the suit and to the defendant to defend the suit. A big firm like Mohan
Meakin Breweries Limited has its ramifications all over the country and it
is easy for it to have dragged this public Corporation into litigation
even at a far off place like Kanya Kumari if the only evidence necessary
to give jurisdiction to that Court was of purchasing of a few bottles by
any agent of Mohan Meakin Limited from someone locally there.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
It must be stressed that the said decision was delivered on the 29th of
September, 1980, approximately 15 years before the official release of
public internet services in India.
144. In Glen Raven Mills Inc. v. Vaspar Concepts Pvt. Ltd. and Anr.,
96
a
suit for trademark infringement along with passing off was filed before this
Court seeking, inter alia, a permanent injunction. The plaint narrated that
the plaintiff instructed its attorneys to investigate the use of the impugned
mark by the defendant. As part of the investigation, one Mr. Debjit Gupta, a
resident of Delhi, placed an order for goods under the impugned mark via
exchange of letters. Thereafter, pursuant to the order, goods were dispatched
by the defendants and were delivered to Mr. Gupta at Delhi.
145. S.K. Mahajan J. concluded that goods could not be stated to be
delivered in Delhi. The Court noticed that freight had been paid by the
plaintiff, and the carrier, thus, was found to be the plaintiff‘s agent. Relying

96 1995 SCC OnLine Del 724.

94
on Section 39 of the Sale of Goods Act, 1930,97 it was held that the delivery
of goods to the carrier/agent was made at Bangalore, which shall be deemed
to be the delivery to the plaintiff. The entire contract for supply of goods
was, thus, completed at Bangalore with no cause of action, wholly or in part,
accruing in Delhi.
146. Further in Glen, this Court analysed, that assuming the delivery did in
fact happen in Delhi, whether jurisdiction was made out under Section 20(c)
of the CPC. Placing reliance on the decisions of Mohan Meakin Breweries
Limited and Bhagwan Dass, the Court observed that in a case of passing
off, it is necessary to prove that the supply made by the defendant at a
particular place was on a commercial scale. There should also be a direct
transaction of sale and purchase between the defendant and a distributor,
whole-seller or a retailer, and such transactions should be of a commercial
proportion.98 Para. 12 of the said decision reads as under:
―12. With a view, therefore, to give jurisdiction to the Court in a case of
passing off, it is necessary to prove that the supply made by the defendant at a
particular place was on a commercial scale. There should have been direct
transaction of sale and purchase between the defendant and a distributor or a
wholeseller or a retailer and such transactions should be of commercial
proportion.‖
147. In Indovax Pvt. Ltd. v. Merck Animal Health and Ors.,
99 originally a
suit for trademark infringement along with passing off was filed, which
subsequently, was confined by the plaintiff to passing off alone. Neither the
plaintiffs nor the defendants resided within the jurisdiction of the court. The
question which arose was whether cause of action can be said to have arisen

97 ―Sale of Goods Act‖.
98 1995 SCC OnLine Del 724, para. 12.
99 2017 SCC OnLine Del 9393.

95
within the jurisdiction of the forum court. To support its case, the plaintiff
along with the plaint had filed an invoice issued in Jind, Haryana to prove
the accrual of cause of action. Subsequently, however, the plaintiff filed on
record three invoices of one ‗Baxi Medical Stores‘ in Delhi, along with the
amended plaint. These invoices were purported to be issued not to the
plaintiff but to third parties.
148. Deepa Sharma J. in Indovax appointed a Local Commissioner in
order to discover the source from where Baxi Medical Stores procured the
stock of the impugned products, sold under the invoices attached by the
plaintiff. The Local Commissioner in its report dated 26.11.2013 stated that
Baxi Medical Stores sourced the stock from Karnal, Haryana and that the
proprietor of Baxi Medical Stores also admitted that he is not a distributor of
defendants and had merely procured the three vials of product INNOVAX
from outside Delhi. The proceedings were found to be an abuse of process
and an attempt by the plaintiff to forum-shop.
149. The Court reiterated the test, inter alia, laid down in Glen, namely,
that for cause of action to accrue in a suit for passing off, the defendant must
have directly made sales of the goods carrying the impugned trademark, not
to an individual consumer, but to a distributor, wholesaler or retailer, at a
commercial scale. Mere availability of goods, it was opined, does not confer
jurisdiction. The invoices put on record by the plaintiff were found to be a
result of the plaintiff‘s own acts. The Court found that the impugned goods
were otherwise not available in Delhi, but were procured from the
authorised dealer of the defendant at Haryana. No cause of action was found

96
to have arisen within the jurisdiction of Delhi and the plaint was resultantly
returned.
150. From the discussion above, it may be concluded that in a suit for
passing off:
a. Cause of action (wholly or in part) can be said to have arisen
within the territorial jurisdiction of a court only if the defendant
has made sales of the goods carrying the impugned trademark
within the jurisdiction of a given Court. Direct sales are made
by the defendant not to an individual consumer but to a
distributor, wholesaler or a retailer. Such sales are at a
commercial scale. Mere availability of goods within the
jurisdiction of a forum court does not confer jurisdiction.
b. Sale of goods within the territorial jurisdiction of the forum
court by a firm which does not act as a distributor, wholesaler,
retailer, or agent of the defendant, does not confer jurisdiction.
c. Where goods are procured by a firm from another firm that is
outside the jurisdiction of the forum court and in turn sold
within the territory of the forum court, jurisdiction shall not, be
conferred.
d. Additionally, delivery of goods to the agent/carrier of the
plaintiff or its investigator, for onward transmission, is deemed
delivery upon the plaintiff or its investigator. This conclusion is
not restricted to suits for passing off, but is a general conclusion

97
arrived at by the application of Section 39 of the Sale of Goods
Act.
(iii) An Action for Infringement in the Offline World
151. In the year 1915, the Allahabad High Court in the case of Kheshtra
Pal Sharma v. Pancham Singh Varma,
100 decided a civil revision filed
against the judgment of the District Judge, affirming the Subordinate
Judge‘s order returning the plaint in a suit for trademark infringement and
passing off on the ground that territorial jurisdiction was not made out. The
plaint averred that the plaintiff had been making and selling in Muttra a
medicine named ―Sudha Sindhu‖. The mark ―Suddha Sindhu‖ was claimed
to be registered in favour of the plaintiff. The defendant, a resident of Gaya,
had advertised for sale at Gaya, a medicine made by him and named ―Asli
Sudha Sindhu‖. Advertisements of the defendant‘s medicine had appeared in
papers published in Muttra, and in circulars and hand-bills distributed in
Muttra.
152. Dr. Tej Bahadur Sapru along with Pandit Motilal Nehru appeared for
the revisionists and argued that if a person advertises as his own, a trade
mark belonging to another, he thereby infringes the mark. The distribution
of hand-bills and the publication of advertisement in Muttra was contended
to be the infringement complained of which gave the plaintiff his cause of
action. Per contra, Dr. Sunder Lal appeared for the respondent and argued
that for jurisdiction of a given court to be invoked there must be actual sale
of the impugned goods within the local limits of a given court.

100 1915 SCC OnLine All 115.

98
153. The Division Bench found actual sale to be dispensable to determine
infringement of a registered trademark. Mere advertisement was found to be
sufficient to invoke jurisdiction of a given Court. The material portion of the
judgment reads as under:
―The question is whether the publication of the advertisement, by the
respondent, of his medicine, Asli Sudha Sindhu, in papers, hand-bills and
circulars published in Muttra is an infringement of the applicant's trademark.
For the purpose of this application we must of course assume that the
applicant is entitled to the trade mark which he claims, and that the
respondent's advertisement is calculated to induce people to believe that they
will get from him the applicant's medicine. No authority has been produced in
support of the argument that such an advertisement cannot be an infringement
of the trade mark. On the other hand several English cases have been cited
which show that it has been held for some years past that a trade mark may be
infringed by means of an advertisement. We think it is sufficient to refer to
the decisions in Jay v. Ladler (1), Bourne v. Swan and Edgar, Limited (2), and
to the injunction which was issued by the House of Lords in the case of Frank
Reddaway v. George Banham (3). On the authorities we must hold that if the
facts are as alleged by the applicant his trade mark has been infringed within
the jurisdiction of the Subordinate Judge of Muttra. We therefore allow this
application, set aside the orders of the courts below and direct that the record
be returned to the court of first instance and the suit restored to the pending
file to be disposed of according to law. Costs here and hereto will be costs in
the cause.‖
154. In Burger King Corporation v. Techchand Shewakramani and
Ors.,
101 the plaintiff had filed a suit for trademark infringement along with a
claim for passing off for its mark ―BURGER KING‖ and ―HUNGRY
JACK‘S‖. All the defendants were based out of Mumbai and the plaintiff
was a US based company. The question before the court was whether
territorial jurisdiction was made out in Delhi. On the website of the
defendant, it was mentioned that street carts containing the impugned mark
of the plaintiff were ―coming soon‖ at Delhi. The threat of the defendants

101 2018 SCC OnLine Del 10881.

99
launching Burger King carts in Delhi was found by the Court to be imminent
and credible, particularly because of, among other things, an article
appearing in the Business Digest magazine on 30.07.2014. Considering the
unique facts and circumstances of the said case, the court found that
jurisdiction for a quia timet was made out in Delhi.
155. In M/s. Intas Pharmaceuticals Ltd. v. Allergen Inc.,
102 a Division
Bench of this Court noted that the dictum in Dhodha House of jurisdiction
not being conferred by goods manufactured by the plaintiff being available
or sold in the market of Delhi, applied while interpreting the phrase ―carries
on business‖ and not while construing Section 20(c). In the said case, the
plaint had averred that the impugned goods were sold and distributed within
the jurisdiction of the forum court. The defendant had not denied this
pleading in its written statement. Finding sale and distribution of goods in
Delhi to be an admitted position, the Court held that jurisdiction of Delhi
Courts is made out.
156. Similarly, in Pfizer Enterprises Sarl v. Cipla Ltd.,
103 a Division
Bench of this Court dealt with a suit for trademark infringement along with a
claim for passing off. Jurisdiction of Delhi courts was sought to be invoked,
inter alia, on the ground that the defendant‘s products under the impugned
mark are available for sale in Delhi and jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of
the CPC was, therefore, made out. Qua the Pfizer appeal104 which arose
from an order of a learned Single Judge dated 16.07.2008, the Division

102 2006 SCC OnLine Del 802.
103 2009 (107) DRJ 735 (DB).
104 Two separate appeals were decided vide a common order in Pfizer Enterprises Sarl. One of the appeals
was the Pfizer Appeal (FAO (OS) 356/2008).

100
Bench noted that the defendant had not specifically traversed the pleadings
pertaining to the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. Resultantly, the
plaintiff would not be required to lead any evidence on this aspect of the
case. Thus, the factum of sale of the impugned goods having been admitted,
jurisdiction was found to be made out.
157. While the conclusion in Burger King of jurisdiction for a quia timet
action being made out was sufficient to adjudicate upon the controversy
which arose in the said case, additional observations were made concerning
the elements of ―cause of action‖ in a passing off and infringement suit. It
was held by the Court that cause of action for a trademark infringement suit
arises when a person ―uses in the course of trade‖ any mark without the
owner‘s consent. After referring to the definition of using a mark under
Section 2(2) of the Trade Marks Act, and other provisions relating to the
rights conferred upon a registered mark holder, the court found that any
unauthorised use of the mark within the jurisdiction of this Court would
constitute a cause of action to sue. Paras. 19 and 21-22 of the decision read
as under:
―19. What constitutes cause of action in the context of a suit alleging
violation of rights in a trade mark, would therefore be the question. In a case
involving trade mark infringement, infringement happens when a person
―uses in the course of trade‖ any mark without the owner's consent. Thus,
use of a mark is the cause of action in an infringement as also in a passing
off action. If use takes place in a territory where the suit is filed, that court
has the jurisdiction to entertain the suit. When there is use of a mark, there is
a cause of action to sue, where the use takes place. it is relevant to point out
that ―use‖ of a trade mark as per Section 2(2)(c) of the TM Act is as under:
―(2) In this Act, unless the context otherwise requires, any reference—
∗∗∗
(c) to the use of a mark—

101
(i) in relation to goods, shall be construed as a reference to
the use of the mark upon, or in any physical or in any other
relation whatsoever, to such goods;
(ii) in relation to services, shall be construed as a reference
to the use of the mark as or as part of any statement about
the availability, provision or performance of such services;‖
****
21. Thus, jurisdiction of a court in a trade mark action, could be invoked
where there is use upon or in relation to goods. The phrase ―in relation to‖
has been interpreted to include advertising, promotion, publicity, etc. Thus,
in addition to actual sale of goods and providing services, if a person
advertises his or her business under the mark in a territory, promotes his or
her business under the mark in a territory or for example invites franchisee
queries from a particular territory, sources goods from a particular territory,
manufactures goods in a particular territory, assembles goods in a particular
territory, undertakes printing of packaging in a particular territory, exports
goods from a particular territory, it would constitute ―use of a mark‖.
22. This scheme of the TM Act is amply clear from a reading of Sections 28
and 29 as also Section 56. Under Section 28, the rights conferred are the
exclusive right to use of a mark. Under Section 29, use of a mark could be
any form of use, including—
• as part of a trade name or a corporate name or name of a business
concern [Section 29(5)];
• use by affixing it to products/services [Section 29(6)(b)];
• use by affixing it to packaging [Section 29(6)(b)];
• use by offering goods/services for sale;
• use for the purpose of import or export[Section 29(6)(c)];
• use on business papers [Section 29(6)(d)];
• use in comparative advertising which is detrimental to distinctive
character or repute of the mark [Section 29(4)];
• use in advertising [Section 29(7)];
• Applying a mark in a territory for purposes of export of goods/services
[Section 56(1)]; and
• Use by which a trade connection is created between the user and the
proprietor [Section 56(2)].‖
158. At para. 23 of the decision in Burger King, the learned Senior Judge
observed that ―principles which apply to infringement, actions to determine
“use” would equally apply to passing off actions.‖ The decisions of this
Court in Glen and Indovax, which were earlier in time, were not pointed out

102
to the learned Single Judge. The finding at para. 23 is in the teeth of the
ratio in Glen and Indovax insofar as an action of passing off is concerned.
Since these judgements were not taken note of by the learned Single Judge,
no justification can be found in Burger King to deviate from the views taken
in the earlier decisions. Thus, the observations made by the learned Single
Judge in Burger King are to be confined exclusively to infringement cases
while Glen and Indovax continue to hold the field for passing off cases.
159. An example of what may not constitute cause of action may now be
considered. In Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd. v. Veda Seed Sciences
Pvt. Ltd.,
105 the plaintiff had filed a suit for infringement of its registered
trademark along with a claim for passing off. Upon an objection of territorial
jurisdiction being raised, this Court found that jurisdiction of Delhi was
made out on a number of grounds. One of these grounds was that the
trademark was registered at Delhi. The Court opined that the factum of
registration at Delhi was part of the cause of action as it was an essential fact
needed to be proved to support the right to judgment.
160. The decisions of the Supreme Court in Alchemist Ltd. and Anr. v.
State Bank of Sikkim and Ors.,
106 and Union of India v. Adani Exports
Ltd.,
107 were, however, not pointed to the Division Bench.
161. In Union of India v. Adani Exports Ltd.,
108 the writ petitioner had
challenged the denial of benefits under an import-export passport scheme
before Gujarat High Court, which had entertained the writ petition, and the

105 2025 SCC OnLine Del 8727.
106 (2007) 11 SCC 335.
107 (2002) 1 SCC 567.
108 (2002) 1 SCC 567.

103
same was assailed before the Supreme Court. Different factual indicia,
prima facie, appearing to favour the jurisdiction of different High Courts
were present. Facts supporting the jurisdiction of the Gujarat High Court
were:
The petitioner carried out business of export and import from Ahmedabad;
non-granting and denial of utilisation of the credit in the passbook was to
affect the business at Ahmedabad; the orders of export and import of the
petitioner are placed from and are executed at Ahmedabad; documents and
payments for export and import are sent/made at Ahmedabad; and credit of
duty claimed in respect of exports were handled from Ahmedabad since
export orders as well as payments were received at Ahmedabad.
162. Facts supporting the jurisdiction of Madras High Court:
Passbook in question was issued by an authority at Chennai; designated
authority, which is the competent person in respect of matters concerning
the Passbook Scheme is at Chennai; the entries in the passbook under the
scheme are to be made by authorities at Chennai; and the export of prawns
made by the petitioner and the import of the inputs, benefit of which the
petitioner was seeking, will also have to be made through the Chennai port.
163. The Supreme Court in Adani Exports held that the Gujarat High
Court did not have jurisdiction to entertain the lis, and it was the Madras
High Court, before which the writ petition should have been filed. It was re-iterated by the Supreme Court that each and every fact pleaded in the
petition does not constitute a cause of action unless the facts have a nexus
and relevance with the dispute. Facts which have no bearing on the lis or the

104
dispute involved in the case do not constitute cause of action (wholly or in
part).
164. In Alchemist Ltd., a company having its registered and corporate
office at Chandigarh, in furtherance of an invitation to offer made by the
State of Sikkim, concerning disinvestment in the Bank of Sikkim, deposited
4.50 crores with the State Bank of India, Chandigarh. Upon the proposal of
the company being rejected, the High Court of Punjab and Haryana was
moved claiming that a part of the cause of action had accrued within its
jurisdiction. The High Court found that no part of cause of action had arisen
within its territory. This finding was affirmed by the Supreme Court noting:
―37. From the aforesaid discussion and keeping in view the ratio laid down
in a catena of decisions by this Court, it is clear that for the purpose of
deciding whether facts averred by the appellant-petitioner would or would
not constitute a part of cause of action, one has to consider whether such
fact constitutes a material, essential, or integral part of the cause of action. It
is no doubt true that even if a small fraction of the cause of action arises
within the jurisdiction of the court, the court would have territorial
jurisdiction to entertain the suit/petition. Nevertheless it must be a ―part of
cause of action‖, nothing less than that.‖
165. If the logic of Kohinoor Seed Fields was to be applied to the facts of
Alchemist Ltd., the factum of proving part-performance at Chandigarh, by
depositing 4.50 crores at the branch office of the State Bank of India, was
material to the judgment. Arguably, it would also be important for such a
corporation to prove that it was actually incorporated. But would the situs of
incorporation have any bearing on the cause of action? Certainly not. In the
facts of Kohinoor Seed Fields, the factum of the trademark being registered
at Delhi has nothing to do with the bundle of facts constituting the cause of
action. Thus, owing to the decisions of Alchemist Ltd. and Adani Exports

105
not being pointed out to the learned Division Bench, the decision of
Kohinoor Seed Fields, may not be binding on this issue.
166. From the discussion above, it may be concluded that in a suit for
infringement of trademark:
a. Cause of action (wholly or in part) can be said to have arisen
within the territorial jurisdiction of a court when the defendant,
in the course of trade, uses a mark which is identical with or
deceptively similar to the trade mark, in relation to goods or
services in respect of which the trade mark is registered, within
the jurisdiction of the forum court.
b. Registration of a mark within the jurisdiction of a given court
does not constitute a part of the cause of action.
(iv) An Action for Infringement in the Online World
167. There are multitude of forums available to a plaintiff in order to sue
for infringement or passing off. For instance, they may sue where the
defendant resides, carries on business, or personally works for gain.
Additionally, under the specialised statutes, if applicable, where the plaintiff
resides, carries on business or personally works for gain. Another set of
forums are to be made available under Section 20(c) of the CPC in the
context of online activity.
168. CS(COMM)-980/2026 is one of the commercial suits dealt with in the
present batch of matters. In the said case, jurisdiction of Delhi courts is
pleaded on the grounds that the ―infringing activities of the Defendant are

106
not geographically limited but are borderless and accessible from
anywhere, including New Delhi, in view of the online activities being
conducted through various online platforms.‖ This case, and specifically the
pleading made, is a prime example of why high jurisdictional thresholds
ought to be set under Section 20(c) of the CPC, where infringement is
claimed through the use of internet. The requirement of proving cause of
action (wholly or in part), in the context of internet activity, cannot
encompass each and every one of the wrongs mentioned in Burger King.
169. Although an internet activity may constitute a statutory wrong under
the Trade Marks Act or the Copyright Act, it may not confer jurisdiction on
a given court, owing to other counter-vailing considerations, that are
required to be balanced with the right of the plaintiff. These considerations
include, inter alia, predictability i.e., a given defendant must have a
reasonable idea of where it may be sued. It ought not to be the case that for a
particular act purportedly done by a defendant it could be equally sued at
Ladakh and at Jhumri Telaiya. Next, the jurisdictional avenues made
available must be fair and reasonable. The places where the plaintiff can sue
cannot be, as is claimed in CS(COMM)-980/2026, at all places where the
so-called borderless infringing activity is taking place. Some degree of
balancing is essential.
170. There is, it may be clarified, no cavil with the age old proposition that
where there is a right there is a remedy. None can question whether a
plaintiff has a right to sue upon infringement taking place. The issue,
however, concerns where it can sue.

107
171. The decision of the Division Bench of this Court in Banyan Tree
Holding (P) Ltd. v. A. Murali Krishna Reddy and Anr.,
109 which was
rendered approximately 17 years ago, recognised the concern noted above.
The Division Bench being conscious of the scheme of the Trade Marks Act,
including the multifarious acts that constitute infringement, provided a high
threshold for when Section 20(c) of the CPC can be invoked. It may again
be stressed that if jurisdictional rules are to have any meaning at all, in the
context of internet activity, high thresholds are necessarily required to be set.
The decision in Banyan may now be examined.
172. The judgment arose from a reference order110 made by a learned
Single Judge of this Court. After considering the submissions made by Mr.
Pravin Anand, the Division Bench framed the following questions:
―7. …
(i) For the purposes of a passing off action, or an infringement action where
the Plaintiff is not carrying on business within the jurisdiction of a court, in
what circumstances can it be said that the hosting of a universally accessible
website by the defendants lends jurisdiction to such Court where such suit is
filed (―the forum court‖)?
(ii) In a passing off or infringement action, where the defendant is sought to
be sued on the basis that its website is accessible in the forum state, what is
the extent of the burden on the Plaintiff to prima facie establish that the
forum court has jurisdiction to entertain the suit?
(iii) Is it permissible for the Plaintiff to establish such prima facie case
through ―trap orders‖ or ―trap transactions‖?‖
173. The material portion of the discussion made by the Court concerning
the first question, reads as under:

109 2009 SCC OnLine Del 3780.
110 2008 SCC OnLine Del 1740.

108
―40. …It appears to this court that for the purposes of a passing off action or
an action for infringement where the Plaintiff is not carrying on business
within the jurisdiction of the forum court, and where there is no long arm
statute, the Plaintiff would have to show that the defendant purposefully
availed itself of the jurisdiction of the forum court. It is not enough merely to
show that the website hosted by the defendant is an interactive one. It would
have to be shown that the nature of the activity indulged in by the defendant
by the use of the website was with an intention to conclude a commercial
transaction with the website user.
****
42. This Court holds that jurisdiction of the forum court does not get attracted
merely on the basis of interactivity of the website which is accessible in the
forum state. The degree of the interactivity apart, the nature of the activity
permissible and whether it results in a commercial transaction has to be
examined. For the ―effects‖ test to apply, the Plaintiff must necessarily plead
and show prima facie that the specific targeting111 of the forum state by the
defendant resulted in an injury or harm to the Plaintiff within the forum state.
For the purposes of a passing off or an infringement action (where the
plaintiff is not located within the jurisdiction of the court), the injurious effect
on the Plaintiff's business, goodwill or reputation within the forum state as a
result of the defendant's website being accessed in the forum state would have
to be shown. Naturally therefore, this would require the presence of the
Plaintiff in the forum state and not merely the possibility of such presence in
the future. Secondly, to show that an injurious effect has been felt by the
Plaintiff it would have to be shown that viewers in the forum state were
specifically targeted. Therefore the ―effects‖ test would have to be applied in
conjunction with the ―sliding scale‖ test to determine if the forum court has
jurisdiction to try a suit concerning internet based disputes.‖
The material portion of the discussion made by the Court concerning the
second question reads as under:
―45. This court holds that in order to prima facie establish that the defendant
purposefully availed of the jurisdiction of this court, the Plaintiff would have
to show that the defendant engaged in some commercial activity in the forum

111 Para. 22 of the decision in Banyan Tree, after relying on the comments of Prof. Thomas Schultz and
Prof. Michael Geist, explained targeting as follows:
―Schultz defines targeting to mean ―in essence that the activity must be intended to have effects within the
territory of the state asserting jurisdiction.‖ According to another scholar, Professor Michael Geist (―Is
There a There There? Towards Greater Certainty for Internet Jurisdiction‖ 16 Berkeley Tech LJ (2001)
1345 at 1357) the principle of targeting is to ―identify the intentions of the parties and to assess the steps
taken to either enter or avoid a particular jurisdiction.‖ Targeting is described as ―something more than
effects, but less than physical presence….‖

109
State by targeting its website specifically at customers within that State. … In
any action for passing off or infringement, it would have to be shown that the
defendant by using its mark intended to pass off its goods as that of the
Plaintiff's. A mere hosting of a website which can be accessible from anyone
from within the jurisdiction of the court is not sufficient for this purpose. Also
a mere posting of an advertisement by the defendant depicting its mark on a
passive website which does not enable the defendant to enter into any
commercial transaction with the viewer in the forum state cannot satisfy the
requirement of giving rise to a cause of action in the forum state. Even an
interactive website, which is not shown to be specifically targeted at viewers
in the forum state for commercial transactions, will not result in the court of
the forum state having jurisdiction. In sum, for the purposes of Section 20(c)
CPC, in order to show that some part of the cause of action has arisen in the
forum state by the use of the internet by the defendant, the Plaintiff will have
to show prima facie that the said website, whether euphemistically termed as
―passive plus‖ or ―interactive‖ was specifically targeted at viewers in the
forum state for commercial transactions. The Plaintiff would have to plead
this and produce material to prima facie show that some commercial
transaction using the website was entered into by the defendant with a
user of its website within the forum state and that the specific targeting of
the forum state by the defendant resulted in an injury or harm to the
Plaintiff within the forum state. Question no. (ii) is answered accordingly.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
174. Importantly, in Banyan Tree, the Court consciously rejected the
contention that purposeful availment ought to be replaced by purposeful
avoidance. The acceptance of purposeful avoidance as a test would allow
jurisdiction to be conferred merely because blinkers and blockers have not
been put qua the forum state‘s access to the website, and commercial
transactions have in turn taken place. Thus, the mere fact that a website has
not restricted its accessibility to a forum court cannot be sufficient to
conclude that targeting and purposeful availment has taken place.
175. In Sholay Media Entertaiment, the Banyan Tree standard was found
to not have been met and the plaint was returned to the plaintiff.
Importantly, this Court relied upon an important decision of the Karnataka

110
High Court in Presteege Property Developers Ltd. v. Prestige Estates
Projects Ltd.,
112 which held that even booking or placing an order through
the internet is insufficient to conclude that a transaction took place within
the jurisdiction of a given court.
176. Next, in Impressario Entertainment & Hospitality Pvt. Ltd. v. S&D
Hospitality,
113 a suit was filed by the plaintiff for the infringement of its
registered trademark along with a claim for passing off. It was contended
that the plaintiff was the owner of the registered trademark ―SOCIAL‖ and
―STONE WATER‖ which it utilised with respect to cafes. While the
registered office of the plaintiff was in Mumbai, it was stated that it ran four
cafes in Delhi. It was claimed that the defendant ran two restaurants at
Hyderabad using the trade mark ―SOCIAL MONEY‖, which infringed the
plaintiff‘s trademark and constituted the tort of passing off. Jurisdiction of
Delhi was sought to be claimed on grounds that the defendant had its
presence on websites like Zomato114 and Dine-Out, from which it could
invite customers of the plaintiff to visit its outlet in Hyderabad.
177. While ordering for the return the plaint, this Court in Impressario
held that the listing of the cafe on the given website was neither sufficient to
constitute cause of action (wholly or in part) nor to claim that the defendant
carried on business within Delhi. Even if it was assumed that customers in
Delhi actually made bookings on the websites, this was found to be
insufficient as the actual transaction was to take place at Hyderabad. The

112 MFA No. 494 and 13696, 2006.
113 2018 SCC OnLine Del 6392.
114 Back then, Zomato did not deliver food.

111
analysis made at para. 19 of the decision deserves reproduction and reads as
under:
―19. Thus even if the defendant has been able to attract customers from
other jurisdiction including by way of Zomato and Dine-Out, the services of
