the defendant cannot be availed unless the customers go to Hyderabad.
Through Zomato and Dine Out the customers will only be able to invite a
customer and resume a table at the restaurant of the defendant at Hyderabad.
The commercial transaction would take place only on the customer availing
the services of the defendant at Hyderabad. Claim of the plaintiff is that at
least one customer through the website booked the table at defendant's
restaurant from Delhi believing that it was a restaurant of the plaintiff. On
the basis of a solitary transaction, through internet plaintiff cannot claim that
cause of action having arisen at Delhi this Court will have territorial
jurisdiction to entertain the suit. Facts pleaded in the plaint do not pass the
tests laid down by the Division Bench of this Court in Banyan Tree Holding
(P) Ltd. (supra).‖
It may be highlighted that the decision in Impressario involved the
application of the Banyan Tree standard to a case of trademark
infringement.
178. In Tata Sons Pvt. Ltd. v. Hakunamatata Tata Founders and
Others,
115 the plaintiff was the holding company of the TATA Group of
Companies that had filed a suit, primarily, for trademark infringement along
with a claim for passing off. The plaintiff owned the registered trademark
―TATA‖ and the defendants were claimed to be using its mark for doing
online trading in cryptocurrency through their websites
―www.tatabonus.com‖ and ―www.hakunamatata.finance‖. It was further
averred that the owner of the websites had its office based in the UK. Behind
the website, there were also a team of individuals who were UK nationals of

115 2022 SCC OnLine Del 2968.

112
Pakistani origin. The question was whether territorial jurisdiction was made
out.
179. The Division Bench began its analysis by citing a decision in
Millennium & Copthorne International Limited v. Aryans Plaza Services
Pvt. Ltd. and Ors.,
116 where a learned Single Judge diluted the Banyan Tree
standard and dispensed with the requirement of showing injury or harm to
the plaintiff within the forum state. The second question answered in
Banyan Tree was what is the burden of proof on the plaintiff to prima facie
establish that the forum court has jurisdiction to entertain a suit. This was
answered by the Division Bench by holding that the plaintiff would have to
plead and produce material to prima facie show that some commercial
transaction using the website was entered into by the defendant with a user
of its website within the forum state resulting in an injury or harm to the
plaintiff within the forum state. This answer given by the Division Bench
was ignored by the learned Single Judge in Millennium & Copthorne
International Limited. A perusal of the decision in Millennium &
Copthorne International Limited would reveal that no reasons were given
to detract from the view in Banyan Tree. The learned Single Judge, thus,
could not have diluted the ruling in Banyan Tree.
180. In Tata Sons, the Court, thereafter, referred to a decision of the Court
of Appeal of England and Wales in Lifestyle Equities CV v. Amazon UK
Services Ltd.,
117 and laid down that ―targeting‖ is to be interpreted as per the

116 2018 SCC OnLine Del 8260.
117 [2022] EWCA Civ 552.

113
dictum in Lifestyle Equities CV.
118 While the decision in Banyan Tree was
premised on the tests adopted in the USA, Tata Sons switched the baton to
the law declared by English courts which was premised on ―targeting‖ as
opposed to specific targeting which was required under the Banyan Tree
standard.
181. Para. 22 of Tata Sons deserves to be extracted, it reads as under:
―22. We are in complete agreement with the above view of the English
court. Even if a website is not directed at customers in a particular country,
the fact that they are not restricted by the website to have access to it, is
enough to characterise it as targeting. Targeting need not be a very
aggressive act of marketing aiming at a particular set of customers. Mere
looming presence of a website in a geography and ability of the customers
therein to access the website is sufficient, in a given case. It may not be
forgotten that in the matters of infringement of trade mark, it is the
possibility of confusion and deception in the mind of public due to
infringing trade mark that is good enough for the court to grant injunction.‖
182. The opinion of the Division Bench that a given website not being
restricted to a forum constitutes targeting does not sit well with the
observations made in para. 41 of Banyan Tree. In the said paragraph, the
argument of ―purposeful availment‖ being replaced with the test of
―purposeful avoidance‖ was rejected. Thus, the mere fact that filters don‘t
exist to block the access at a forum state cannot be sufficient to constitute
specific targeting. The plaintiff must not merely show the lack of blockers
but something in addition to that.
183. The finding that a ―mere looming presence of a website in a
geography and ability of the customers therein to access the website is

118 It may be noted that the decision of the Court of Appeal, though affirmed in its outcome by the Supreme
Court of the United Kingdom in Lifestyle Equities CV and Anr. v. Amazon UK Services Ltd. and Ors., 2024
UKSC 8, was modified to some extent in terms of its reasoning.

114
sufficient‖ appears to be against the ratio of both Banyan Tree and Lifestyle
Equities CV where the UK Supreme Court, in appeal from the decision of
the Court of Appeal, observed as under:
―18. Accessibility of the website was not enough, however, as the court
went on to explain, at para 94: ―On the other hand, the mere accessibility of
the trader‘s or the intermediary‘s website in the member state in which the
consumer is domiciled is insufficient. The same is true of mention of an e-mail address and of other contact details, or of use of a language or a
currency which are the language and/or currency generally used in the
member state in which the trader is established.
****
21. On the other hand, the court continued, at para 64, mere accessibility of
the accused website from the territory covered by the trade mark was not
enough to establish targeting of consumers in that territory. That would have
the consequence that such websites and advertisements which, although
obviously targeted solely at consumers in third states, would wrongly be
subject to EU law.
****
25. The Court of Appeal revisited the issue of targeting in Argos Ltd v
Argos Systems Inc [2018] EWCA Civ 2211; [2019] Bus LR 1728. There,
Floyd LJ (with whom Lord Kitchin JSC and Sir Colin Rimer agreed)
emphasised, at para 48, that the mere fact that a website is accessible from
anywhere in the world, and therefore may attract occasional interest from
consumers there when this is not intended, should not give rise to any
liability. He continued…‖
184. The reasoning adopted in Tata Sons to dilute the decision in Banyan
Tree, thus would have no precedential value.
185. Independently, however, a fact centric limitation could be added to
the rule in Banyan Tree. In Banyan Tree, the defendants were residents of
Hyderabad. The question which the Division Bench, therefore, answered
was: what would warrant jurisdiction being conferred upon a given forum
court under Section 20(c) of the CPC, given that the avenues under Section
20(a) always remain open. In order to prevent Section 20(c) from becoming
an unruly horse, the burden of proof requirement under Banyan Tree was

115
conceived of. However, in the facts of Tata Sons, the defendant had no
presence in India at all. If a given plaintiff cannot invoke the provisions of
Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act and Section 62(2) of the Copyright
Act, then the only avenue which remains available is Section 20(c).
186. If, in such a case, compliance with the burden of proof under the
Banyan Tree standard is demanded, a plaintiff may not be able to sue at all.
Thus, in the peculiar facts and circumstances of a case, particularly, where
the defendants had no presence in India the Banyan Tree standard may be
relaxed. This relaxation is not available where defendants reside in Delhi
and no exceptional situations are presented.
187. Before parting with the decision in Tata Sons, a brief observation
may be made on the decision of Lifestyle Equities CV, which was relied
upon in Tata Sons. While the said decision of the English court was
premised on the language used in the EU regulations, and thus is not per se
applicable, some guidance may be taken in the manner in which targeting is
adjudged.
188. The specific targeting and purposeful availment required by Banyan
Tree is not intended to be a subjective intention of a person to step into a
forum court‘s territory. The Court while seeking compliance with the
Banyan Tree standards is not engaging in an exercise to determine the state
of mind of the person controlling the corporation. The intention is to be
objectively determined by analysing the indicia available on a given
website. The words used by the UK Supreme Court, at para. 30 provide
some guidance: ―the task of the court is to decide whether the foreign
trader’s activities, viewed objectively, from the perspective of the average

116
consumer, are targeted at such consumers in the UK. It is therefore not
necessary to establish that the foreign trader had the subjective intention of
targeting customers in the UK‖.
189. In Cable News Networks INC v. CTVN Calcutta Television Network
Pvt. Ltd.,
119 a learned Single Judge, after relying upon WWE and Tata Sons,
dispensed with the requirement propounded in Banyan Tree of prima facie
proving that an actual commercial transaction took place. This, as has been
noted above, may not be permissible.
190. In Johnson & Johnson Pte. Ltd. v. Abbireddi Satish Kumar and
Ors.,
120 a suit for trademark infringement was filed by the plaintiff claiming
that its ―ORSL‖ marks and trade dress were being infringed by the
defendant. The Court gave the following reasoning at para. 19 to conclude
that jurisdiction at Delhi was made out:
―19. Considering the averments made in the plaint, it is manifest that this
Court has jurisdiction in the present matter. The plaintiff has made
categorical averments in the plaint as regards placing an order for the
products of the defendants in Delhi. Thus, when the website marketing the
goods of the defendants is accessible from Delhi, though the said website
may have stopped its operation subsequently, and products may not have
been ultimately delivered in Delhi, the very fact that the said website could
be accessed with the products of the defendants shown online, at the time of
filing of the suit, this Court would have territorial jurisdiction. The same
would constitute use in relation to goods, as the goods of the defendants
were promoted and advertised through the third-party website, within the
jurisdiction of this Court, at the time of filing of the suit.‖
191. A reading of the said decision would reveal that the learned Single
Judge did not, at all, avert to the decision in Banyan Tree. Reference was
made to Burger King Corporation, and its exhortation on the ―use‖ of a

119 2023 SCC OnLine Del 2436.
120 2025 SCC OnLine Del 688.

117
mark was used to claim that the mere listing of goods on a website is
sufficient to confer jurisdiction. It may be noted that the Banyan Tree tests
apply to infringement cases as well. Burger King Corporation merely
describes the kinds of infringement that may take place by various uses of
the mark. However, if the infringement is online, the Banyan Tree tests
must necessarily govern the field. The only way in which Burger King
could be considered as superseding the dictum in Banyan Tree would be if
it can be said that the Division Bench in Banyan Tree was not aware of the
ways in which a trademark gets infringed under the Trade Marks Act. There
is no indication that the Bench was not aware of the provisions relating to
this aspect.
192. In Sun Pharmaceuticals Pvt. Ltd. v. Artura Pharmaceuticals P.
Ltd.,
121
a co-ordinate bench of this Court relying on Tata Sons Ltd., held
that the requirement to consummate a commercial transaction has been
dispensed with in toto. Resultantly, by merely relying on the ―Contact Page‖
of the defendant‘s website, it was concluded that the Banyan Tree standard
of purposeful availment through specific targeting, was satisfied. As noticed
above, the same may not be permissible, least of all, because of the
foundational factual difference between Tata Sons Ltd. and Sun
Pharmaceuticals. Similar observations may be made regarding the decision
in Ravinder Singh v. Regoshin Healthcare Pvt. Ltd. and Ors.,
122 which was
rendered relying on Sun Pharmaceuticals.

121 2025 SCC OnLine Del 8642.
122 2026 SCC OnLine Del 3716.

118
193. The decision of Kohinoor Seed Fields has already been considered
under the heading of ―carrying on business‖. It has been observed at paras.
102-103 and 111-112 of this judgement, that the incorporation of WWE into
Section 20(a) of the CPC may not be correct. The reliance on Tata Sons to
hold that the principle in Banyan Tree has been diluted has also been
examined, and it has been found that material issues were not pointed out to
the Division Bench. Additionally, it appears that Kohinoor Seed Fields, has
also made an observation on Section 20(c) of the CPC. While there may not
be a detailed analysis of how access to websites constitutes cause of action,
in para. 20.3 of the judgment, the Division Bench has concluded that the
impugned goods being available for sale across e-commerce websites,
including at Delhi, constitutes a substantial part of the cause of action. The
said paragraph reads as under:
―20.3 We need not delve too deep into this aspect, as, in our view, a
substantial part of the cause of action in the present case does arise
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court, because (i) the asserted
trade marks of the appellant stand registered within the jurisdiction of this
Court, (ii) the Marketing Agreement was executed at Delhi and (iii) the
allegedly infringing goods of the respondent are available for sale and
purchase, across e-commerce websites, at Delhi. As such, no occasion
arises to invoke the Ultra Home Construction doctrine.
(Emphasis supplied)
194. This conclusion was reached by the Division Bench despite observing
at para. 19.5.5 that the decision in WWE is not an authority on Section 20(c)
of the CPC. It would be fit to merely observe that the observations made in
the context of Section 20(c) constituted obiter dicta and not binding
precedent.

119
195. The subsequent decision in Rukhmani Keshwani v. Raju Agarbatti
Works & Anr.,
123 however, applies the passing reference in Kohinoor Seed
Fields to the substantive interpretation of Section 20(c). Previously, Courts
attempted to move around the decision in Banyan Tree, recognising its
application in the domain of Section 20(c). In Rukhmani Keshwani, the
Court found that the law has developed past Banyan Tree. Paras. 54 to 56
and 58 of the decision in Rukhmani Keshwani are reproduced as under:
―54. The jurisprudence governing internet-based territorial jurisdiction has
evolved progressively, beginning with the principles enunciated in Banyan
Tree Holding (P) Ltd. v. A. Murali Krishna Reddy, where the Court
differentiated between a passive website and an interactive commercial
platform designed to target consumers within a particular jurisdiction.
55. This principle, however, did not remain confined to the narrow passive
versus interactive website dichotomy. The doctrine was subsequently
expanded and contextualized in WWE (supra), wherein the Court
recognized that the true test is whether the defendant has purposefully
availed itself of the forum by commercially targeting customers therein
through electronic means.
56. The aforesaid principles were further elaborated by one of us (C. Hari
Shankar J.) in Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd. v. Veda Seed Sciences
Pvt. Ltd., where this Court, while noting the transformation of commercial
activity in the digital era, observed that modern ecommerce platforms are
designed to transcend territorial barriers and facilitate commercial
engagement across jurisdictions. This Court emphasized that once a
defendant consciously projects its goods or services through interactive
online interfaces enabling commercial solicitation, inquiry, negotiation, or
purchase by consumers situated within a forum State, such activity
constitutes a material part of the cause of action.
****
58. The ratio emerging from Kohinoor Seed Fields India Pvt. Ltd (supra) is
that the Court must examine the substance of the commercial targeting and
the purposeful availment of the forum, rather than mechanically confining
itself to the existence or absence of completed transactions. Thus, where
online listings are intended to solicit business, display commercial contact

123 2026 SCC OnLine Del 4904.

120
details, facilitate customer interaction, and expand market reach into
multiple jurisdictions, the territorial nexus is sufficiently established.
196. It is difficult to discern a basis in either WWE or Kohinoor Seed
Fields for the conclusions noted in paras. 54 to 56 and 58 above.
197. WWE as has been noted above was a decision rendered in the context
of Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act and Section 134(2) of the Trade
Marks Act. Badar Durrez Ahmed J., explicitly, at para. 8 of the decision
notes that the decision of Banyan Tree shall not have any application. If that
be the case, the finding in Rukhmani Keshwani at para. 55 that the doctrine
of Banyan Tree was ―expanded and contextualised‖ in WWE, may not be
correct.
198. Next, the finding rendered in para. 56 of Rukhmani Keshwani that
Kohinoor Seed Fields has held that an activity constituting the conscious
projection of goods or services ―through interactive online interfaces
enabling commercial solicitation, inquiry, negotiation, or purchase by
consumers‖ also does not seem to find support in the text of Kohinoor Seed
Fields. Similar observations may be made with respect to the ruling in para.
58 of Rukhmani Keshwani that Kohinoor Seed Fields allows for sufficient
―territorial nexus‖ to be made out if online listings are intended to ―solicit
business, display commercial contact details, facilitate customer interaction,
and expand market reach into multiple jurisdictions.‖ In the context of
Section 20(c) of the CPC, this appears to be against the dictum of Banyan
Tree. Further, in para. 59 of Rukhmani Keshwani the following has been
observed:

121
―59. This Bench in Raju Kumar v. Vinod Sah (authored by C. Hari Shankar
J.) relied upon and applied the principles enunciated in Kohinoor Seed
Fields India Pvt. Ltd (supra) while determining territorial jurisdiction in
online trademark disputes. The judgment in Raju Kumar (supra) has since
been affirmed by the Supreme Court in Vinod Sah v. Raju Kumar.‖
199. It appears that an SLP124 was dismissed against the decision of Raju
Kumar v. Vinod Sah. It is trite law, as was also declared in Kunhayammed
and Ors. v. State of Kerala and Anr.,
125 that the dismissal of an SLP in
limine does not amount to a merger of the impugned decision with the order
of the Supreme Court. Thus, the order remains as that of our Court.
200. Before parting, a brief reference may especially be made to the
Copyright Act. The decisions of this Court in Nilesh Girkar v. Zee
Entertainment Enterprises Ltd. and Ors.,
126 and Zee Entertainment
Enterprises Ltd. v. Mohalla Tech Pvt. Ltd.,
127 concern factual matrices
where the conclusion of commercial transactions may not be possible. In
such a situation it would not be appropriate to insist upon a commercial
transaction where none can take place. Making available a copyrighted film
on rogue websites would give rise to a cause of action by it becoming
accessible in Delhi. That is not, however, the end of the inquiry. As the
discussion below shall reveal, considerations of forum conveniens shall have
a role to play. The place where the uprooting of the protected content takes
place, where the plaintiff resides and carries on business, are material
considerations to determine as to whether the cause of action at Delhi is

124 SLP (C) No. 12585/2026, order dt. 30.04.2026.
125 AIR 2000 SC 2587.
126 2025 SCC OnLine Del 6040.
127 2025:DHC:11002.

122
slender/minuscule and the lis is better left to be adjudicated by another court
that is forum conveniens.
201. From the discussion above the following conclusions can be drawn
concerning cause of action for infringement and passing off in the online
world:
a. To establish that cause of action (wholly or in part) has arisen
through the means of website activity, the plaintiff must
demonstrate that the defendant has purposefully availed itself
of the jurisdiction of the forum court by specifically targeting
persons within its territory and it resulting in an injury or harm
to the plaintiff within the forum state.
b. Additionally, the plaintiff must also demonstrate the conclusion
of commercially concluded transactions between the defendant
and persons within the forum court‘s jurisdiction through the
website in question.
c. The requirement of prima facie demonstrating commercially
concluded transactions taking place can be relaxed in a case
where the plaintiff cannot invoke the provisions of Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act and Section 62(2) of the
Copyright Act, and the defendant has no presence in India at
all.
D. “DYNAMIC EFFECT” AND IT CONFERRING JURISDICTION

123
202. In a number of cases concerning the present batch of matters, for
instance CS(COMM) 611/2026, CS(COMM) 917/2026, CS(COMM)
444/2026, and CS(COMM) 874/2026, the plaintiff has claimed jurisdiction
of Delhi on the basis of the elusive expression of the ―Dynamic Effect‖ of
the infringement taking place in Delhi. There is no reference made at the bar
to dictionaries, lexicons, or commentaries to indicate what dynamic effect is.
The arguments of counsel would indicate that it constitutes the effect that an
infringement has at the place where the plaintiff has its presence.
203. A brief reference may also be made to the decision in Adyar Gate,
where jurisdiction was also found to be made out under Section 20(c) of the
CPC. The case concerned an action for trade mark infringement and passing
off filed by ITC Ltd. which had its registered office at Kolkata and a
restaurant at Delhi. Invoking an expression, which may ordinarily lie in the
doctrine of forum conveniens, the Court held that the ―determinative part‖ of
the cause of action had accrued at Delhi. By observing that the use of the
mark by the defendant in its restaurant at Chennai, had an effect on ITC
Ltd.‘s restaurant at Delhi, jurisdiction of this Court was established. By
doing so, the Court, effectively applied the dynamic effect principle using
the text of Section 20(c).
204. The dynamic effect principle and such an interpretation of part cause
of action, in the opinion of this Court, may not confer jurisdiction. The
rationale behind the legislature enacting Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act
and Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act was allowing a given plaintiff to
sue where it resides, carries on business or personally works for gain. If
indeed, the effect, dynamic or otherwise, of an infringement could confer

124
jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of the CPC, why would the legislature have
enacted the specialised provisions? No tenable answer can be found. The
concept of dynamic effect, therefore, appears to be unknown to
jurisdictional principles governing the specialised statutes and the CPC. A
pleading that invokes jurisdiction basis the ―dynamic effect‖ like it does in
the above-noted suits, ought to be ignored.
205. From the discussion above the following conclusions concerning the
principle of dynamic effect can be drawn:
a. If the principle of dynamic effect is applied to cases of
infringement, the amendment to the Trade Marks Act and the
Copyright Act shall be rendered otiose. Jurisdiction, therefore,
cannot be established basis this principle.
b. An attempt to find jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of the CPC
on the basis that the effect of the infringement is felt at the
place where the plaintiff has its presence, also, goes against the
spirit of the specialised provisions. It also renders the
amendments nugatory. Such an interpretation, thus, ordinarily
ought to be avoided.
E. THE USE OF AN “INVESTIGATOR”
206. In Machinenfabrik Rieter AG and Anr. v. Tex Tech Industries India
Pvt. Ltd. and Anr.,
128 a suit was filed for trademark and patent infringement.
The plaintiffs had their registered office in Mumbai, defendant no. 1 was
placed in Coimbatore, Tamil Nadu, and defendant no. 2 was a resident of

128 2021 SCC OnLine Del 1825.

125
Chandigarh. None of the parties had their head office, branch office, or
residence within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. Delhi‘s jurisdiction
was sought to be invoked by making the following pleadings:
―That the plaintiffs instructed their investigator to ascertain the scope and
extent of the defendant no. 1‘s activities. Pursuant to initial investigation,
the plaintiffs‘ investigator placed an order with defendant 1 via an email
dated 26-12-2019; a performa invoice was issued on 27-12-2019; thereafter,
an email was received from a representative of defendant 2 who informed
the investigator that defendant 2 is a supplier of spare parts manufactured by
defendant 1. The plaintiffs, then, made the payment and samples were
despatched by defendant 1 on 29-1-2020 and the same were received by the
plaintiff's investigator on 31-1-2020 in Delhi. Two such separate orders
were also received by the plaintiffs‘ investigator. It was claimed by the
plaintiff that the products so received from defendant 1 at Delhi infringe the
plaintiffs‘ patent. The plaintiffs had also filed documents including invoices
showing delivery of goods in Delhi.‖
207. It was found by the Court that the dictum of the Division Bench in
Banyan Tree, concerning the impermissibility of trap transactions, does not
apply to offline transactions. Para. 13, in which the finding was reached,
reads as under:
―13. In my opinion the above judgment of the Division Bench does not have
application to the facts of this case. That was a case in which territorial
jurisdiction was claimed by the plaintiff based on the defendants alleged
presence in Delhi through their website which was accessible in Delhi. It
was contended that the website is not a passive website since inputs from
customers through interactive web page were being taken. The court was
dealing with the issue of territorial jurisdiction of this Court on account of
the defendants hosting a website which was accessible in Delhi. It was in
those facts that the Division Bench had come to the aforenoted conclusions.
The present case is a case of offline transaction.‖
208. Two issues arise concerning the above-noted conclusion: first, a
learned Single Judge in Indovax (P) Ltd. had already applied the law laid
down in Banyan Tree to offline transactions; no reasons were given for
departing from the said view; and second, a reading of Banyan Tree,

126
particularly paras. 46-56, would reveal that the discussion of Banyan Tree
was not made solely in the context of online transactions. The case law,
taken note of by the Division Bench to arrive at the answer to the third
question formulated in the reference, was not exclusively concerning online
transactions.
209. As a matter of principle as well, allowing jurisdiction to be conferred
through transactions entered into between an investigator of the plaintiff and
a given defendant seems unjust and unfair. Allowing this to happen would
allow litigants to engage in forum shopping and litigation tourism. If this
were to be condoned, all that is required is for a given plaintiff to have a
clever enough agent who orchestrates a well-designed plan to concoct
jurisdiction before a given court. It has been settled by a catena of decisions
that jurisdiction cannot be conferred on a court where none in actuality
exists. By allowing this, effectively, jurisdiction is being artificially created
at a given place which the plaintiff believes is favourable to him. The
―investigator‖ may very well be at Port Blair, or arguably may be asked to
be at that place, when a defendant is at Ladakh. The mere fact that goods
were delivered in Port Blair should not become a reason to force the person
at Ladakh to go to Port Blair.
210. Jurisdiction rules can never be interpreted in a manner that allows a
plaintiff to engage in such a mischief. Courts are not meant to be silent
spectator in this theatre called ―jurisdiction‖ scripted by the plaintiff. The
scene in such scripted plays usually goes in the following manner:
An investigator in Delhi is engaged by a company in Mumbai to get goods
delivered from a company, which operates in Chennai. This Court is moved

127
and a vociferous plea is raised that under Section 20(c) of the CPC cause of
action has partly arisen in Delhi. In order to protect the plaintiff‘s goodwill,
the counsel for the plaintiff eloquently argues that urgent ex parte ad-interim
reliefs are required to be granted.
211. Such ‗gaming of the system‘ cannot be allowed to go by. Transactions
with such an ―investigator‖, in and of themselves, are not sufficient to
invoke the jurisdiction of this Court. Two exceptions, however, are possible:
first, where the plaintiff can prima facie demonstrate, on the basis of his
plaint and the documents attached with it, that but for such an ―investigator‖
being hired to plead jurisdiction, cause of action under Section 20(c) of the
CPC could not be maintained before another court. And second, where ex
facie it can be demonstrated that sales of the impugned goods are taking
place within a forum court, and an investigator merely provides evidence.
For instance, if the defendant has readily supplied its goods within Delhi to
various retailers, distributors and stockists, and the plaintiff‘s investigator
buys these goods from these classes of persons, that report may not be
discarded.
212. However, if in the natural course of events, jurisdiction can in fact be
founded under Section 20(c) of the CPC at a place other than Delhi, the use
of an ―investigator‖ to forge jurisdiction before this court, shall, ordinarily,
be an abuse of the process of law and impermissible. The fact of the plaintiff
being aware of the defendant using his registered trademark, or passing off
its goods, in the ordinary and natural course of the trade, at a jurisdiction
different from Delhi, is most material to the lis. If the plaintiff is aware of
such facts the option of engaging an ―investigator‖ may get foreclosed. Non-

128
disclosure with respect to this, shall amount to the plaintiff coming to the
court with unclean hands. An equitable relief of injunction can never be
granted to such a claimant. It is trite law as was also noted by RC Lahoti J.
in Dorstener Maschinenfabrik AG v. Sand Plast India Ltd. and Ors.,
129
that despite a given claimant satisfying the trinity requirements, an
injunction can be refused on grounds, inter alia, of conduct as it is an
equitable relief.
213. Additionally, it may be noted that despite the plaintiff not disclosing
to the court facts pertaining to the defendant‘s usual, natural, and ordinary
sale/use of the impugned mark, the Court may in exercise of powers
conferred under Section 168 of the BSA put relevant questions to the parties.
The said provision reads as under:
―168. Judge's power to put questions or order production.
The Judge may, in order to discover or obtain proof of relevant facts, ask
any question he considers necessary, in any form, at any time, of any
witness, or of the parties about any fact; and may order the production of
any document or thing; and neither the parties nor their representatives shall
be entitled to make any objection to any such question or order, nor, without
the leave of the Court, to cross-examine any witness upon any answer given
in reply to any such question:
Provided that the judgment must be based upon facts declared by this
Adhiniyam to be relevant, and duly proved:
Provided further that this section shall not authorise any Judge to compel
any witness to answer any question, or to produce any document which such
witness would be entitled to refuse to answer or produce under sections 127
to 136, both inclusive, if the question were asked or the document were
called for by the adverse party; nor shall the Judge ask any question which it
would be improper for any other person to ask under section 151 or 152; nor
shall he dispense with primary evidence of any document, except in the
cases hereinbefore excepted.‖ [

129 1994 SCC OnLine Del 777.

129
214. Sir James Fitzjames Stephen in his speech,130 while elaborating on the
purpose of Section 165 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872 (the precursor to
the BSA), opined:
―…It is absolutely necessary that the Judge should not only hear what is put
before him by others, but that he should ascertain by his own inquiries how
the facts actually stand. In order to do this, it will frequently be necessary
for him to go into matters which are not themselves relevant to the matter in
issue, but may lead to something that is, and it is in order to arm Judges with
express authority to do this that section 165, which has been so much
objected to, has been framed.‖
A brief reference may also be made to the following portion of Sir John
Woodroffe & Syed Amir Ali’s Law of Evidence:
131
“Section 165 is intended to arm the judge with the most extensive power
possible for the purpose of getting at the truth. The effect of this section is
that in order to get to the bottom of the matter before it, the court will be
able to look at and enquire into every fact whatever and thus possibly
acquire valuable indicative evidence which may lead to other evidence
strictly relevant and admissible.”
132
And:
―It is obvious that the judge contemplated by the section is not a mere
umpire at a witcombat between the lawyers for the parties whose only duty
is to enforce the rules of the game and declare at the end of the combat who
has won and who has lost.‖133
215. The subsequent decision in Reiter AG and Anr. v. Kavassery
Narayanaswamy Venkatasubramanian,
134 was rendered on the basis of the
decision in Machinenfabrik Rieter AG; the conclusion reached may be
doubted on the grounds detailed above.

130 Speech of the Hon‘ble Sir James Fitzjames Stephen on the 12 March 1872, in submitting the Report of
the Committee to the Council.
131 21st Ed., 2020, Lexis Nexis.
132 21st Ed., 2020, Lexis Nexis, Vol. IV, Pg. 4873.
133 21st Ed., 2020, Lexis Nexis, Vol. IV, Pg. 4881.
134 2025 SCC OnLine Del 4379.

130
216. From the discussion above, the following conclusions can be drawn
concerning the use of an investigator/agent to establish jurisdiction:
a. As a rule, the use of an investigator or any other person set up
by the plaintiff to find jurisdiction before a forum court
amounts to concocting jurisdiction. The findings of such an
investigator, thus, ordinarily, cannot be relied upon.
b. The two exceptions to this rule are: first, where the plaintiff can
prima facie demonstrate, on the basis of his plaint and the
documents attached with it, that but for such an ―investigator‖
being hired to plead jurisdiction, cause of action under Section
20(c) of the CPC could not be maintained before another court.
And second, where ex facie it can be demonstrated that sales of
the impugned goods are taking place within a forum court, and
an investigator merely provides evidence. For instance, if the
defendant has readily supplied its goods within Delhi to various
retailers, distributors and stockists, and the plaintiff‘s
investigator buys these goods from these classes of persons,
that report may not be discarded.
F. THE DOCTRINE OF FORUM NON-CONVENIENS
217. The conclusions rendered by this Court under this section curtail the
options available to a plaintiff to institute a suit. The objection raised by
counsel, against the reasoning adopted by this Court, is an alleged unbridled
right that the plaintiff has to institute a suit, in the absence of an express bar
contained in the statute. In doing so the counsel have conflated the

131
availability of a forum before which a suit may lie, with an indefeasible
vested right to bring the suit in that forum.
218. This right, perhaps may have existed, at a given point in time; the law,
however, has travelled far ahead. Today, suits ought to be tested on the
ground on forum non conveniens. The development in law was captured in
eloquent words by Lord Denning MR in Smith Kline & French
Laboratories Ltd. v. Block,
135 the material portion of which reads as under:
“The law
It often happens that a plaintiff is entitled to bring proceedings in two or
more jurisdictions. Sometimes it is said that the choice is his. He can choose
whichever of them suits him best. If he can get more damages in one than he
can in the other, then good luck to him. Let him go there. If he will be met by
a time bar in one and not in the other, let him go to the one where he is not
barred. If it is more convenient for the plaintiff in one than it is for the
defendant, then the plaintiff can choose. You need not spin a coin between
the two contestants. It always comes down in favour of the plaintiff, so it is
said, unless the defendant can prove that it would work an injustice to him.
That was the way the English Court of Appeal approached the problem in
St. Pierre v. South American Stores (Gath & Chaves) Ltd., (1936) 1 KB 382,
[1935] All ER Rep 408 and the Supreme Court of Illinois approached it in
James v. Grand Trunk Western Railroad Co., (1958) 152 NE 2d 858. …
The basis of all this reasoning has now been removed. In England by the
House of Lords in MacShannon v. Rockware Glass Ltd., (1978) 1 All ER
625, [1978] AC 795. In the United States by the Supreme Court in Piper
Aircraft Co. v. Reyno, (1981) 454 US 235. The plaintiff has no longer an
inborn right to choose his own forum. He no longer wins the toss on every
throw. The decision rests with the Courts. No matter which jurisdiction is
invoked, the Court must hold the balance between the plaintiff and the
defendant. It must take into account the relative advantages and
disadvantages to each of them: not only the juridical advantages and
disadvantages, but also the personal conveniences and inconveniences: not
only the private interests of the parties but also the public interests involved.
The Court decides according to which way the balance comes down. …”

135 (19831) 1 WLR 730.

132
219. The jurisdictional tension in suits filed before the Intellectual Property
Division,136 at its core, stems from archaic procedural laws being applied to
modern, technology-driven, manifestation of substantive rights. Section 20
of the CPC, as a residuary jurisdictional rule, has been in existence ever
since the Bengal Regulation III of 1793, under the Cornwallis Code. The
essence of the provision has remained the same, throughout the procedural
codes of 1882, 1877 and 1859. The options given to a plaintiff under these
provisions did not take into account actions being founded on the use of
internet. The concept of a ‗pan-India‘ harm, injury, or effect, could not have
been fathomed by the drafters of these provisions. A simple textual
application of Section 20 to a lis concerning intellectual property rights, may
result in unintended consequences as the jurisdictional rule, in most cases, is
a misfit for the action it seeks to govern.
220. Section 20 of the CPC provides a dual umbrella of options to institute
a suit. The first umbrella is defendant centric: a plaint can be presented
within the local limits of a court where the defendant resides (actually or
voluntarily), carries on business, or personally works for gain. This rule
resonates with the Justinian dictum ‗actor sequitur forum rei‘ i.e., plaintiff
follows the defendant‘s forum. It forces a plaintiff to leave his home-ground
and go into the territory of the defendant.137 On occasions this may be
inconvenient. Thus, an option is given to the plaintiff to file where the cause
of action, wholly or in part, has arisen. The first illustration to Section 20
exemplifies this scenario. It is reproduced as under:

136 ―IPD‖.
137 The dictum in M/s RSPL Limited v. Mukesh Sharma and Anr., FAO (OS) No. 145/2016 and Chandra
Kishore Chaurasia v. R A Perfumery Works Pvt. Ltd., 2022:DHC:4454, similarly, also may not remain
good law today.

133
―(a) A is a tradesman in Calcutta, B carries on business in Delhi. B, by his
agent in Calcutta, buys goods of A and requests A to deliver them to the
East Indian Railway Company. A delivers the goods accordingly in
Calcutta. A may sue B for the price of the goods either in Calcutta, where
the cause of action has arisen, or in Delhi, where B carries on business.‖
221. The person ‗A‘ in the illustration has an option to sue the defendant at
Calcutta, which is the place where the cause of action accrued. He need not
go to the far off place viz. Delhi. The object and purpose of allowing a plaint
to be presented before a Court where the cause of action partly accrues is to
enable the plaintiff to sue nearer to himself, not to take the lis away from the
place having a dominant and substantial connection with the case.
222. In Krishna Veni Nigam v. Harish Nigam,
138 the Supreme Court was
moved for the transfer of a case pending before the Family Court, Jabalpur,
to the Family Court of Hyderabad, Andhra Pradesh. The Court held that the
doctrine of forum conveniens is applicable in civil proceedings despite the
plaintiff being the dominus litis. The Supreme Court, therefore, did not find
the powers of transfer vested with it to be in conflict with the doctrine of
forum non conveniens. Para. 13 of the said decision reads as under:
13. We have considered the above suggestions. In this respect, we may
also refer to the doctrine of forum non conveniens which can be applied in
matrimonial proceedings for advancing interest of justice. Under the said
doctrine, the court exercises its inherent jurisdiction to stay proceedings at a
forum which is considered not to be convenient and there is any other forum
which is considered to be more convenient for the interest of all the parties at
the ends of justice. In Modi Entertainment Network v. W.S.G. Cricket Pte.
Ltd. [Modi Entertainment Network v. W.S.G. Cricket Pte. Ltd., (2003) 4 SCC
341] this Court observed : (SCC pp. 356-57, para 19)
****
Though these observations have been made in the context of granting anti-suit
injunction, the principle can be followed in regulating the exercise of
jurisdiction of the court where proceedings are instituted. In a civil

138 (2017) 4 SCC 150.

134
proceeding, the plaintiff is the dominus litis but if more than one court
has jurisdiction, court can determine which is the convenient forum and
lay down conditions in the interest of justice subject to which its
jurisdiction may be availed [Kusum Ingots & Alloys Ltd. v. Union of India,
(2004) 6 SCC 254, para 30] .
(Emphasis supplied)
223. In Dashrath Rupsingh Rathod v. State of Maharashtra and Anr.,
139
a Three-Judge Bench of the Supreme Court speaking through Vikramjit Sen
J. laid down the judicial approach which is to be adopted on jurisdiction, and
specifically for Section 20 of the CPC. The Court found that the doctrine of
forum conveniens has application in civil suits. Para. 12 of the said decision
reads as under:
―JUDICIAL APPROACH ON JURISDICTION
12. We shall take a short digression in terms of brief discussion of the
approach preferred by this Court in the context of Section 20 of the Code
of Civil Procedure, 1908 (hereinafter referred to as, ‗CPC‘), which inter
alia, enjoins that a suit must be instituted in a court within the local limits
of whose jurisdiction the Defendant actually and voluntarily resides, or
carries on business, or personally works for gain, or where the cause of
action wholly or in part arises. The Explanation to that Section is
important; it prescribes that a corporation shall be deemed to carry on
business at its sole or principal office, or, in respect of any cause of action
arising at any place where it has also a subordinate office, at such place.
Since this provision primarily keeps the Defendant in perspective, the
corporation spoken of in the Explanation, obviously refers to the
Defendant. A plain reading of Section 20 of the CPC arguably allows the
Plaintiff a multitude of choices in regard to where it may institute its lis,
suit or action. Corporations and partnership firms, and even sole
proprietorship concerns, could well be transacting business simultaneously
in several cities. If sub-sections (a) and (b) of Section 20 are to be
interpreted disjunctively from sub-section (c), as the use of the word ‗or‘
appears to permit the Plaintiff to file the suit at any of the places where the
cause of action may have arisen regardless of whether the Defendant has
even a subordinate office at that place. However, if the Defendants’
location is to form the fulcrum of jurisdiction, and it has an office also
at the place where the cause of action has occurred, it has been held

139 (2014) 9 SCC 129.

135
that the Plaintiff is precluded from instituting the suit anywhere else.
Obviously, this is also because every other place would constitute a
forum non conveniens. This Court has harmonised the various hues of
the conundrum of the place of suing in several cases and has gone to
the extent of laying down that it should be courts endeavour to locate
the place where the cause of action has substantially arisen and reject
others where it may have incidentally arisen. Patel Roadways Limited,
Bombay v. Prasad Trading Company, AIR 1992 SC 1514 : (1991) 4 SCC
270 prescribes that if the Defendant-corporation has a subordinate office in
the place where the cause of action arises, litigation must be instituted at
that place alone, regardless of the amplitude of options postulated in
Section 20 of the CPC. We need not dilate on this point beyond making a
reference to ONGC v. Utpal Kumar Basu (1994) 4 SCC 711 and South
East Asia Shipping Co. Ltd. v. Nav Bharat Enterprises Pvt. Ltd. (1996) 3
SCC 443.‖
224. In a similar vein this Court in Ansal Buildwell Ltd. v. North Eastern
Indira Gandhi Institute of Health & Medical Science and Ors.,
140 noted
that the doctrine of forum conveniens applies in the context of civil
proceedings and had held as under:
―25. In the context of forum conveniens it may be noted that in an
appropriate case, a court may refuse to exercise its discretionary jurisdiction
by invoking the said doctrine. While invoking the doctrine of forum
conveniens a court may refuse to exercise its discretionary jurisdiction
notwithstanding that some part of cause of action has arisen within the
territorial jurisdiction of the court.‖
225. The chartered High Courts of Bombay, Madras and Calcutta, having
original ordinary jurisdiction, routinely apply the doctrine of forum
conveniens to civil suits. For instance in Madanlal Jalan v. Madanlal,
141 the
Calcutta High Court held that leave to sue may be revoked by applying the
doctrine of forum conveniens. The material portion of the decision reads as
under:

140 2005 SCC OnLine Del 268.
141 1945 SCC OnLine Cal 145.

136
―(g) that in giving or refusing leave or maintaining or revoking leave the
Court will ordinarily take into consideration the balance of convenience and
may, if the balance is definitely in favour of the defendant, apply the
doctrine of forum conveniences;‖
Relying on Madanlal Jalan, in Union of India v. M. Kuppuswamy
Naicker,
142 the Calcutta High Court similarly observed:
―6. It is well settled that in giving or refusing leave or maintaining or
revoking leave the Court would ordinarily take into consideration the
balance of convenience and may, if the balance is definitely in favour of the
defendant, apply the doctrine of forum convenience, vide Madanlal Jalan v.
Madanlal reported in AIR 1949 Cal 495.‖
226. In TN Seshan v. All India Anna Dravida Munnetra Kazhagam,
143
the High Court of Andhra Pradesh decided a challenge to an order declining
to revoke the leave for instituting four suits by plaintiff and granting leave
for a fifth suit. At para. 12 the Court took note of the doctrine of forum non
conveniens, it reads as under:
―12. Before, however, we part with this judgment we propose to clarify that
refusal to revoke the leave is not treated as an order to deny to the
defendants the right to challenge the facts or traverse the facts which appear
to give rise to the cause of action and in case the facts alleged in the plaint
stand rebutted or disproved or not proved by the plaintiff in accordance with
law, the Court may take the view that the Court's ordinary civil jurisdiction
is not attracted and the suit should be filed somewhere else. One of the
settled principles is, while examining whether, on the ground of a part of
cause of action of the suit has arisen within the territorial limits of the
ordinary civil jurisdiction of the court, leave should be granted or not, to test
the locus conveniente i.e., whether it shall be convenient for the plaintiff and
inconvenient for the defendant or whether it shall be convenient for the
defendant and not for the plaintiff, or whether it shall be convenient for
both, if the trial is held in the court and not anywhere else. The principle of
balance of convenience of the parties in this regard is stated in a Bench
decision of the Madras High Court by one of us in Turicorin Alkali
Chemicals & Fertilisers Ltd. v. Cochin Silicate & Galss Industries (4) 1992
(1) LW 308. In the instant case, however such a plea is not required to be

142 1977 SCC OnLine Cal 235.
143 1995 SCC OnLine AP 546.

137
examined at all for it was a plea of inconvenience of the defendant which
prevailed before the Supreme Court and the cases have been transferred
from the Madras High Court to this Court.‖
227. In Arun Digambar Varangaonkar v. M/s Karpe Gruh Udyog and
Ors.,
144 a suit was filed before the Bombay High Court for trademark and
copyright infringement along with a claim for passing off. It was contended
that the defendants were using the plaintiff‘s protected artistic work and
mark in relation to masala products and spice preparations so as to infringe
upon protected works, and also pass off his goods as that of the plaintiffs. It
was contended by the plaintiff therein that he resides at Amravati and also at
Bombay. The products of the plaintiff were sold in Maharashtra, Karnataka,
Andhra Pradesh, Madhya Pradesh and Gujarat. The plaintiff also caused an
enquiry and purportedly found that the impugned preparations of the
defendants were available in Bombay and caused purchases of them to be
made. Two cash memos in respect of the test purchases made by the plaintiff
from Messrs Amruta Traders had also been placed on record to demonstrate
the preparations being the impugned trademark.
228. RM Lodha J. (as his Lordship then was), found that the plaintiff
mainly resides at Amravati and carries on business also at Amravati. The
goods were also manufactured at Amravati. The defendants reside and also
manufacture the impugned goods at Amravati. It was found that two cash
memos produced by the plaintiff, prima facie, showing sale of goods at
Bombay was not sufficient to entertain a suit at Bombay. Invoking the
doctrine of forum conveniens, the Court observed as under:

144 1997 SCC OnLine Bom 132.

138
―10. From the perusal of the plaint itself, it transpires that plaintiff mainly
resides at Amravati and he carries on business also at Amravati. The goods
are also manufactured by Plaintiff at Amravati. The Defendants reside nt
Amravati and they also manufacture the goods concerning the disputed trade
mark and the device at Amravati. Except two cash memos produced by the
Plaintiff along with the plaint there is no other material prima facie showing
the sale of the disputed goods manufactured by the Defendants in Bombay or
within the jurisdiction of this Court. Subsection (2) of section 62 of the Copy
Right provides that the suit in respect of the infringement of the copy right or
any right conferred under the Copy Right Act shall be instituted in the district
court within local limits of whose jurisdiction the person instituting the suit
actually and voluntarily resides or carries business or presently works for
gain. Obviously sub-section (2) of section 62 enables the suitor to file the suit
or initiate civil proceedings arising under Chapter XII of Copy Right Act in
the District Court within whose jurisdiction he actually and voluntarily resides
or carries on business or personally works for gain. This wholesome
exceptional provision is intended to save suitor if he so desires for redressal of
his grievance in respect of the infringement of copy right or any right
conferred the Copy Right Act from suing at the place where defendant resides
or carries on business. Section 62 of Copy Right Act, of course, cannot take
away the jurisdiction of this Court which it has under Letters Patent but at the
same time this Court shall not entertain and try a suit under Copy Right Act
where whole of cause of action has not arisen within its jurisdiction or
material part of cause of action has not arisen within its jurisdiction or where
though some part of cause of action has arisen within its jurisdiction but
balance of convenience strongly and definitely is in favour of suit being
proceeded within some other Court. In applying the doctrine of forum
convenience the court may consider place of residence or business of the
parties, the place where transactions took place, the volume of evidence and
convenience or inconvenience of producing it at a particular forum, expenses
of trial and such other relevant considerations. As already observed earlier,
plaintiff in his plaint has himself come out with the case that he resides at
Amravati and also at Bombay. The principal residence of the plaintiff,
therefore, appears to be at Amravati. The goods are also manufactured at
Amravati. The Defendants, according to Plaintiff himself reside at Amravati
and carry on business at Amravati. The volume of evidence that may be led
by the parties can be conveniently produced at Amravati. The trial at
Amravati shall save the parties from unnecessary expenses and, therefore,
balance of convenience definitely would be if the suit is tried at Amravati.‖
229. The argument that the High Courts of Bombay, Calcutta and Madras
could invoke the doctrine of forum conveniens only because of the wording
of their respective Letters Patent is erroneous. The decisions of the Supreme

139
Court in Dashrath and Harish Nigam were not in the context of Letters
Patent. While Dashrath was concerned with the application of the CPC,
Harish Nigam‘s dictum applies to all civil proceedings.
230. The doctrine of forum conveniens does not require an express
statutory basis for its invocation in civil proceedings. A civil court has
inherent power to decline to exercise jurisdiction on the ground that it is not
the forum conveniens.
231. In Gulf Oil Corp. v. Gilbert,
145 the Supreme Court of the USA
decided on the issue ―whether the United States District Court has inherent
power to dismiss a suit pursuant to the doctrine of forum non conveniens
and, if so, whether that power was abused in this case‖. Authoring for the
majority, Justice Jackson opined that the doctrine of forum conveniens has
application even for intra-state disputes.
―It is conceded that the venue statutes of the United States permitted the
plaintiff to commence his action in the Southern District of New York, and
empower that court to entertain it. [Footnote 3] But that does not settle the
question whether it must do so. Indeed, the doctrine of forum non conveniens
can never apply if there is absence of jurisdiction or mistake of venue.
****
In all cases in which the doctrine of forum non conveniens comes into
play, it presupposes at least two forums in which the defendant is
amenable to process; the doctrine furnishes criteria for choice between
them. The principle of forum non conveniens is simply that a court may
resist imposition upon its jurisdiction even when jurisdiction is
authorized by the letter of a general venue statute. These statutes are
drawn with a necessary generality, and usually give a plaintiff a choice of
courts, so that he may be quite sure of some place in which to pursue his
remedy. But the open door may admit those who seek not simply justice,
but perhaps justice blended with some harassment. A plaintiff sometimes

145 330 US 501 (1947).

140
is under temptation to resort to a strategy of forcing the trial at a most
inconvenient place for an adversary, even at some inconvenience to himself.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
232. Unequivocally, the Supreme Court of Illinois in Torres v. Walsh,
146
held that the invocation of the doctrine of forum non conveniens does not
require a statutory backing. It originates and lies in the common law. The
lucid words of Justice Clark read as under:
―While in Illinois we are without any statutory authorization, we find that
such authority to transfer a case under the doctrine of forum non conveniens
exists at common law, and, therefore, statutory authorization is unnecessary
as it only recognizes and codifies a right that previously existed at common
law. The doctrine of forum non conveniens originated in the common law of
England which Illinois adopted as it existed ―prior to the fourth year of James
the First" (Ill. Rev. Stat. 1981, ch. 1, par. 801).‖‖
233. There is, thus, no requirement for there to be a provision in the CPC,
providing in explicit terms, that the doctrine of forum conveniens applies to
civil suits. A careful analysis of the provisions of the CPC would also reveal
that there is indeed scope to read this doctrine into the Code. A bar on the
application of this doctrine simply cannot be found in the Code. To analyse
this, a few provisions of the CPC, may be extracted as under:
Section 15 of the CPC reads as under:
―15. Court in which suits to be instituted.
Every suit shall be instituted in the Court of the lowest grade competent to
try it.‖
Section 20 of the CPC reads as under:
―20. Other suits to be instituted where defendants reside or cause of
action arises.

146 456 N.E.2d 601, 98 Ill. 2d 338.

141
Subject to the limitations aforesaid, every suit shall be instituted in a Court
within the local limits of whose jurisdiction—
(a) the defendant, or each of the defendants where there are more than one,
at the time of the commencement of the suit, actually and voluntarily
resides, or carries on business, or personally works for gain; or
(b) any of the defendants, where there are more than one, at the time of the
commencement of the suit, actually and voluntarily resides, or carries on
business, or personally works for gain, provided that in such case either
the leave of the Court is given, or the defendants who do not reside, or
carry on business, or personally works for gain, as aforesaid, acquiesce in
such institution; or
(c) the cause of action, wholly or in part, arises.
Explanation.—A corporation shall be deemed to carry on business at its sole
or principal office in India or, in respect of any cause of action arising at any
place where it has also a subordinate office, at such place.‖
234. Sections 15 and 20 of the CPC contain the necessary conditions that
ought to be satisfied by the plaintiff while instituting a suit. A plaint cannot
be instituted before a forum which is not of the lowest grade competent to
try it. A suit not governed by Sections 16 to 19, will have to be instituted in
accordance with Section 20 and comply with the requirements of sub-sections (a) to (c). Satisfaction of Sections 15-20 of the Code determines
whether a forum is capable of adjudicating a lis. The doctrine of forum non-conveniens is, however, concerned with whether a forum ought to adjudicate
a given case. The doctrine of forum conveniens, it is trite law, is applicable
only when a forum is found to possess jurisdiction. It is after that stage, the
inquiry begins: whether it is appropriate to adjudicate that suit. No conflict,
at all, can be found between the doctrine of forum non-conveniens and the
provisions of the CPC.
235. A Code cannot be expected to contemplate every possible issue that
may arise in civil proceedings. Section 151 of the CPC, which saves the

142
inherent powers of the Court, is intended to redress those very situations,
which are not effectively redressed by the remaining portion of the Code.
Reliance may be placed on the decision of the Supreme Court in Manohar
Lal Chopra v. Rai Bahadur Rao Raja Seth Hiralal,
147 where Raghubar
Dayal J. speaking for a Four Judge Bench, observed as under:
―18. …the Courts have inherent jurisdiction to issue temporary injunctions in
circumstances which are not covered by the provisions of Order 39 CPC.
There is no such expression in Section 94 which expressly prohibits the issue
of a temporary injunction in circumstances not covered by Order 39 or by any
rules made under the Code. It is well settled that the provisions of the Code
are not exhaustive, for the simple reason that the legislature is incapable of
contemplating all the possible circumstances which may arise in future
litigation and consequently for providing the procedure for them. The effect
of the expression ―if it is so prescribed‖ is only this that when the rules
prescribe the circumstances in which the temporary injunction can be issued,
ordinarily the Court is not to use its inherent powers to make the necessary
orders in the interests of justice, but is merely to see whether the
circumstances of the case bring it within the prescribed rule. If the provisions
of Section 94 were not there in the Code, the Court could still issue temporary
injunctions, but it could do that in the exercise of its inherent jurisdiction. No
party has a right to insist on the Court‘s exercising that jurisdiction and the
Court exercises its inherent jurisdiction only when it considers it absolutely
necessary for the ends of justice to do so. It is in the incidence of the exercise
of the power of the Court to issue temporary injunction that the provisions of
Section 94 of the Code have their effect and not in taking away the right of
the Court to exercise its inherent power.
****
20. Further, the provisions of Section 151 of the Code make it clear that
the inherent powers are not controlled by the provisions of the Code. Section
151 reads:
―Nothing in this Code shall be deemed to limit or otherwise affect the
inherent power of the Court to make such orders as may be necessary for the
ends of justice or to prevent abuse of the process of the Court.‖
21. A similar question about the powers of the Court to issue a
commission in the exercise of its powers under Section 151 of the Code in
circumstances not covered by Section 75 and Order 26, arose in Padam Sen v.
State of Uttar Pradesh [(1961) 1 SCR 884] and this Court held that the Court
can issue a commission in such circumstances. It observed at p. 887 thus:

147 1961 SCC OnLine SC 17.

143
―The inherent powers of the Court are in addition to the powers
specifically conferred on the Court by the Code. They are complementary to
those powers and therefore it must be held that the Court is free to exercise
them for the purposes mentioned in Section 151 of the Code when the
exercise of those powers is not in any way in conflict with what has been
expressly provided in the Code or against the intentions of the legislature.‖
These observations clearly mean that the inherent powers are not in any way
controlled by the provisions of the Code as has been specifically stated in
Section 151 itself. But those powers are not to be exercised when their
exercise may be in conflict with what had been expressly provided in the
Code or against the intentions of the legislature. This restriction, for practical
purposes, on the exercise of those powers is not because those powers are
controlled by the provisions of the Code but because it should be persumed
that the procedure specifically provided by the legislature for orders in certain
circumstances is dictated by the interests of justice.‖
236. Justification for invoking the doctrine of forum non-conveniens can be
adequately found in Section 151 of the Code. Additionally, the language of
Order VII Rule 10 may also be examined. The words used are under:
―10. Return of plaint.
(1) Subject to the provisions of rule 10A, the plaint shall at any stage of the
suit be returned to be presented to the Court in which the suit should have
been instituted.
Explanation.— For the removal of doubts, it is hereby declared that a Court
of appeal or revision may direct after setting aside the decree passed in a
suit, the return of the plaint under this sub-rule.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
237. The word ―should‖ is defined in the following manner in various
dictionaries:
―Should. The past tense of shall; ordinarily implying duty or obligation;
although usually no more than an obligation of propriety or expediency, or a
moral obligation, thereby distinguishing it from ―ought.‖ It is not normally
synonymous with ―may,‖ and although often interchangeable with the word

144
―would,‖ it does not ordinarily express certainty as “will” sometimes
does.‖148
―Should. 1. used to say or suggest that something is the proper, reasonable, or
best thing to do; used with have to say that something was the proper,
reasonable, or best thing to do but was not done. 2. used to say that something
is expected or correct.‖149
―Should vb the past tense of shall: used as an auxiliary verb to indicate that an
action is considered by the speaker to be obligatory (you should go) or to
form the subjunctive mood with I or we.‖150
―Should:— used to show what is right, appropriate, etc. ….‖151
(Emphasis supplied)
238. A plaint shall be returned if it is not filed in a forum where it ―should
have been instituted‖. This includes a case where it is presented before a
Court having no jurisdiction at all, but may also, additionally, include a
court which is forum non-conveniens. Suits should be instituted at a place
which is forum conveniens. Order VII Rule 10 of the CPC, can, thus,
additionally, be considered as containing sufficient leg-room to house the
doctrine of forum non-conveniens.
239. The argument that the doctrine of forum conveniens is in the teeth of
the transfer provisions contained in the Code is devoid of merit. The
doctrine of forum non conveniens can only be invoked by a court before
which a suit is maintainable. The availability of jurisdiction to decide the lis
agitated before a court is, thus, essential for the doctrine to apply. When the
Supreme Court acts under Section 25 of the CPC to transfer proceedings

148 Blacks Law Dictionary, 5th Ed., 1979.
149 Merriam-Webster‘s Collegiate Dictionary, 1160, 11th Ed., 2003.
150 Collins English Dictionary 30th Anniversary Edition (10th Edn. in 2009).
151 Oxford Advanced Learner's Dictionary, New 8th Edn.

145
from a High Court or other Civil Court in one State to a High Court or other
Civil Court in any other State, it does not apply forum conveniens as that lis
which is being transferred could not be decided by the transferring-court. A
civil suit which gets transferred from one High Court to another under
Section 25 of the CPC could not have been decided by the Supreme Court.
The doctrine, thus, is not in conflict with the powers of transfer exercisable
under Section 25 of the CPC. Even otherwise in Harish Nigam, the
Supreme Court while exercising transfer jurisdiction noted the application of
this doctrine to civil proceedings.
240. There may not be explicit statutory provisions, as there are today, in
England and certain States of the USA, empowering a civil court to invoke
principles of forum non-conveniens but that does not mean in appropriate
cases the doctrine cannot be invoked. Our Code remains of 1908, as has
been noted above, a portion of jurisdictional provisions in the CPC have not
changed since the 1700s. It is on the basis of this legislative text that courts
have to interpret modern internet based IP infringement cases. Merely
because the bare letter of the Code is not keeping with the changing times,
does not mean, that courts ought to be stuck at the bark of words. The
doctrine of forum non-conveniens existed and flourished in both US and
England prior to it gaining statutory recognition. There is no reason why it
must not, in India.
241. What necessitates a suit being required to be filed at the appropriate
and convenient forum? A requirement for certainty, predictability and
fairness. A defendant that utilises internet for its business, does not sign up
to get sued around the country. He also does not sign up to live in a constant

146
state of uncertainty as to which part of the country he/she may get summons
from. Yes indeed the right of the plaintiff may get infringed by internet
activity of the defendant. That right needs to be redressed. Ordinarily the
place that redresses that infraction is the place where that wrong occurred.
This in itself was an indicator to the defendant as to where it may be sued.
This is the rationale and idea, behind making forums competent to
adjudicate on issues that take place within their territories. The underlying
premise of this doctrine, however, changes when the cause of action is
contended to be pan-India or susceptible to being created in any part of India
depending upon the plaintiff‘s acts.
242. While in conventional suits it is the defendant‘s conduct, which, in an
overwhelming number of cases, determines where the cause of action
accrues; however, in cases filed before the IPD, it is the plaintiff who seems
to have control over where cause of action can be generated. Jurisdictional
rules have always been defendant-centric. The plaintiff must not have too
many options to choose from, not merely because there is a possibility of
him choosing a place that is the most inconvenient to the defendant, but also
because his choice determines the legal infrastructure required to handle and
manage the consequences of that choice.
243. Legal rules are intended to govern human affairs. They are to be
framed and postulated in a manner that they are understandable to the
everyday citizen of our country. Jurisdictional rules, similarly, intend to
govern procedural law that determines, among other things, where a suit can
be filed. Why should a person know where they could be possibly sued? At
the outset because litigation is expensive, but also because that is the very

147
purpose for which rules are framed. To give clarity to the citizens as to the
manner in which they may conduct themselves and their business.
244. The plaintiff being dominus litis is a consideration. Similar to another
consideration of the rule of law, certainty and construing provisions in a
manner that they don‘t become susceptible to misuse. The issue boils down
to which value is to be given a higher preference. In this Court‘s opinion the
latter set of factors are to be given more importance. This is not, it may be
noted, something completely novel which is being proposed. When the
Supreme Court in Patel Roadways interpreted the explanation to Section
20(a), it restricted certain forums to which the plaintiff otherwise could have
gone. When the Supreme Court in Sanjay Dalia incorporated Section 20(a)
to Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act and 62(2) of the Copyright Act, it
naturally foreclosed certain forums which otherwise would have been
available to a given plaintiff.
245. Courts have, depending upon the situation that gets presented,
restricted the right of the plaintiff and the options that are available to it for
filing a given suit. Nobody has an inherent vested right to file a suit before a
specific court. The plaintiff can validly insist that a violation of his right
must be remedied. He cannot, however, insist that it must be remedied by a
particular court.
246. The parliament while choosing to continue with the language of the
residuary jurisdictional provision, originally enacted by the British in the
form of the Code of 1908, did not intend to allow a plaintiff to file a suit
before a forum where neither he, nor the defendant resides, carries on
business, or personally works for gain, on the ground that cause of action

148
had partially accrued within the jurisdiction of that forum, in a case where
cause of action had also partially accrued at the place where the plaintiff or
the defendant resides, carries on business or personally works for gain.
There also does not appear to be, subject to pleadings being made to the
contrary, any reason for a plaintiff to move away from his own territory and
away from the territory of the defendant, when he very well could have
instituted suits at those place.
247. The application of forum conveniens and an insistence on filing a suit
before the appropriate forum shall also mean that the plaintiff no longer
needs to go through the trouble to come to Delhi from far off places and
institute an action before this Court on grounds that a slender part of cause
of action has accrued within the ―local limits‖ of this Court. He would file
before the court that is forum conveniens. As noted above, this shall give the
defendant the required predictability of knowing from which court he may
be summoned. The menace of trade mark and copyright infringement indeed
ought to, and must be, remedied by the court most closely connected with
the lis and the parties. This is necessary in order to bring about convenience
in litigation to the plaintiff as also the defendant.
248. Ease of litigation ought be the most foundational pillar of the
litigation policy in a country. Interpretation of rules that burden the plaintiff
or the defendant must be eschewed. In the context of IP disputes, each court
within whose jurisdiction the parties reside, carry on business, or personally
work for gain, or the material, integral and essential part of the cause of
action has arisen, has jurisdiction to adjudicate such a suit. A court must in

149
these cases unhesitatingly entertain a plaint without giving any impression
of being incapacitated.
249. The common denominator in the present batch of matters dealt with in
this judgment is an attempt by the plaintiff to disturb the natural flow of
litigation. Through its own voluntary acts, jurisdiction is sought to be
created at a place distant from its own place of residence or where it carries
on business. An impression is given as though the convenient and more
proximate forum is unable to adjudicate the lis or grant the reliefs prayed
for. Such a notion deserves to be dispelled.
250. Further, text alone may not be conclusive of the construction which is
to be given to a provision. For instance, the bare words of Sections 62(2)
and 134(2) of the Copyright Act and Trade Marks Act, respectively, did not
contain an explanation, proviso, caveat, or bar. Despite it, the Supreme
Court in Sanjay Dalia narrowed the options available to the plaintiff, where
a suit can be instituted. Arun Mishra J.‘s observations in Sanjay Dalia
details the motivation behind putting fetters to the discretion that the
plaintiff had under the special legislations. The material portion of the
judgement reads as under:
―24. If the interpretation suggested by the appellant is accepted, several
mischiefs may result, intention is that the plaintiff should not go to far-flung
places than that of residence or where he carries on business or works for gain
in order to deprive the defendant a remedy and harass him by dragging
to distant place. It is settled proposition of law that the interpretation of the
provisions has to be such which prevents mischief. …
25. …The provisions [Sections 62 and 134(2) of the Copyright Act and Trade
Marks Act respectively] enabled the plaintiff or any of them to file a suit at
the aforesaid places. But if they were residing or carrying on business or
personally worked for gain already at such place, where cause of action has

150
arisen, wholly or in part, the said provisions have not provided additional
remedy to them to file a suit at a different place. The said provisions never
intended to operate in that field. The operation of the provisions was limited
and their objective was clearly to enable the plaintiff to file a suit at the place
where he is ordinarily residing or carrying on business, etc. as enumerated
above, not to go away from such places. The legislature has never intended
that the plaintiff should not institute the suit where he ordinarily resides
or at its head office or registered office or where he otherwise carries on
business or personally works for gain where the cause of action too has
arisen and should drag the defendant to a subordinate office or other
place of business which is at a far distant place under the guise of the fact
that the plaintiff corporation is carrying on business through branch or
otherwise at such other place also. If such an interpretation is permitted,
as rightly submitted on behalf of the respondents, the abuse of the
provision will take place. Corporations and big conglomerates, etc. might
be having several subordinate offices throughout the country.
Interpretation otherwise would permit them to institute infringement
proceedings at a far-flung place and at an unconnected place as
compared to a place where the plaintiff is carrying on their business, and
at such place, cause of action too has arisen….
26. The avoidance of counter-mischief to the defendant is also necessary
while giving the remedy to the plaintiff under the provisions in question. It
was never visualised by the lawmakers that both the parties would be made to
travel to a distant place in spite of the fact that the plaintiff has a remedy of
suing at the place where the cause of action has arisen where he is having
head office/carrying on business, etc. The provisions of the Copyright Act and
the Trade Marks Act provide for the authors/trade mark holders to sue at their
ordinary residence or where they carry on their business. The said provisions
of law never intended to be oppressive to the defendant. The Parliamentary
debate quoted above has to be understood in the manner that suit can be filed
where the plaintiff ordinarily resides or carries on business or personally
works for gain. Discussion was to provide remedy to the plaintiff at
convenient place; he is not to travel away. Debate was not to enable the
plaintiff to take the defendant to farther place, leaving behind his place of
residence/business, etc. The right to remedy given is not unbridled and is
subject to the prevention of abuse of the aforesaid provisions, as
discussed above. Parliament never intended that the subject provisions be
abused by the plaintiff by instituting suit in wholly unconnected
jurisdiction. In the instant cases, as the principal place of business is at
Mumbai the cause of action is also at Mumbai but still the place for suing has
been chosen at Delhi. There may be a case where the plaintiff is carrying on
the business at Mumbai and cause of action has arisen in Mumbai. The
plaintiff is having branch offices at Kanyakumari and also at Port Blair,
if interpretation suggested by the appellants is acceptable, mischief may

151
be caused by such plaintiff to drag a defendant to Port Blair or
Kanyakumari. The provisions cannot be interpreted in the said manner
devoid of the object of the Act.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
251. There has been some debate as to the exact import of Sanjay Dalia.
