There is a divergence of opinion at the Bar on what is meant by the lines
highlighted and quoted above. The question raised pertains to what may be
called the ―Merger Doctrine‖. It provides that if cause of action (wholly or
in part) has arisen where the plaintiff resides, carries on business, or
personally works for gain, he can sue at that place and at no other place.
Whether the Supreme Court in Sanjay Dalia by making these observations
intended to foreclose the avenues under Section 20 of the CPC or do they
remain intact is the issue that has been agitated ad nauseam. This Court, is
refraining from rendering any conclusive finding on this issue. It may only
be noted that the most appropriate and relevant guidance that this Court
could find on this issue was the decision of the Division Bench in Sanjay
Dalia,
152 which was subsequently approved by the Supreme Court.
Vikrmajit Sen J. (as his Lordship then was), opining for the Bench, in
unequivocal terms observed:
―13. In this analysis, we find the Appeal to be devoid of merits. So far as the
facts of the present case are concerned, the cause of action, as laid out in the
Plaint, has arisen in Maharashtra only, where the Plaintiff‘s principal office
is located. Had it allegedly arisen at a place where the Plaintiff does not
have its principal or subordinate office, the suit could have been filed in any
Court holding territorial jurisdiction over any of its offices. Thus, it is
evident that we are, in no wise, whittling down the provisions of either the
Copyright Act or the Trade Marks Act. We hold that if the cause of action
has arisen at a place where the Plaintiff actually and voluntarily resides
or carries on business or personally works for gain, that place is not

152 2008:DHC:3060-DB.

152
only the appropriate but also the only place where a suit can be
instituted ventilating a grievance of violation of copyright, (and since
the provisions are similar) to an infringement of the trademark. In
holding so we are not ignoring the provisions of either the Copyright
Act or the Trade Marks Act; we are only imparting a pragmatic
interpretation to them.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
252. The intent behind the words of the Supreme Court can be gathered
from the decision which was affirmed by it. The words used by the Division
Bench are categorical and unequivocal.
253. The argument of there being a bar in the application of the Merger
Doctrine owing to each sub-clause of Section 20 being independent without
any correlation with each other must be stated only to be rejected. There is
no mention of the expression ―cause of action‖ in the text of Section 20(a).
This sub-clause which is defendant centric, gets narrowed down, as noted in
Patel Roadways, by the application of the explanation. This explanation,
links the place where the defendant has its sub-ordinate office with ―cause of
action‖, which is an avenue mentioned in sub-clause (c) of Section 20. In
other words, the explanation merges the presence of the defendant with the
place where cause of action has arisen, and this results into an option
available with the plaintiff being curtailed. The rationale for this being that
the place where cause of action accrues is convenient to the plaintiff; where
the defendant has a sub-ordinate office is similarly convenient to the
defendant, and resultantly, in the context of the explanation, the appropriate
forum becomes the only forum where a plaintiff could file in terms of the
explanation.

153
254. The very same underlying reasoning is being applied under Section 20
for the Merger Doctrine. The place where the plaintiff resides, carries on
business, and personally works for gain is convenient to the plaintiff; since
cause of action has also accrued there, it may not be inconvenient for the
defendant to be sued at that place. The most appropriate forum both in terms
of convenience, and predictability, becomes this place of merger. The
underlying rationale of Patel Roadways thus assists in arriving at the
conclusion under the Merger Doctrine.
255. Separate from the issue concerning the Merger Doctrine, the decision
in Sanjay Dalia, is being used in the present case, only to highlight that the
absence of textual language may not be conclusive of the construction which
is to be adopted.
256. Maxwell details when the language of a statute may be modified:153
―Where the language of a statute, in its ordinary meaning and grammatical
construction, leads to a manifest contradiction of the apparent purpose of the
enactment, or to some inconvenience or absurdity, hardship or injustice,
presumably not intended, a construction may be put upon it which modifies
the meaning of the words, and even the structure of the sentence.‖
257. Bennion on Statutory Interpretation also mentions that a
construction must not be preferred where owing to a remedy being construed
too widely a counter-mischief would prevail. The court also has to be
mindful of the consequences on public welfare. A counter-mischief unseen

153 Maxwell, On the Interpretation of Statutes (P. St. J. Langan ed., 12th Edn, 1969), Ch. 11, Section 1,
―Modification of the Language to Meet the Intention‖, ―Maxwell‖.

154
by the legislature ought to be mitigated by the Court.154 The material portion
of the commentary is:
―Section 12.1: Presumption that ‗absurd‘ result not intended
12.1
(1) The court seeks to avoid a construction that produces an absurd result,
since this is unlikely to have been intended by Parliament...
Section 10.3: The mischief for which Parliament legislated
10.3
(1) An Act may be concerned with several different mischiefs. Where the
legal meaning of a particular enactment is in question, it is important to
identify the precise mischief that the enactment was intended to remedy...
Section 12.7: Avoiding a disproportionate counter-mischief
...Clearly it would be absurd to suppose that Parliament intended to abolish
one mischief only at the cost of establishing another which is just as bad, or
even worse. As a matter of common sense, rules whose disadvantages
outweigh their benefits ought to be discarded.
EXAMPLE
Lord Bingham of Cornhill said:
‗My Lords, s 4 of the Statute of Frauds was enacted in 1677 to address a
mischief facilitated, it seems, by the procedural deficiencies of the day…It
quickly became evident that if the seventeenth century solution addressed
one mischief it was capable of giving rise to another…(Actionstrength Ltd
(t/a Vital Resources) v International Glass Engineering IN.GL.EN. SpA
[2003] UKHL 17).
Strict construction
Where another mischief would arise if the remedy provided by the Act were
construed widely, the court may avoid or at least reduce it by limiting the
remedy.
It may appear to the court that one of the opposing constructions of the
enactment, if adopted, would create a mischief of its own. The prospect of
this would constitute a negative factor in weighing the applicability of the

154 Diggory Bailey & Luke Norbury, Bennion on Statutory Interpretation (LexisNexis, 7th Edn, 2017), Ch.
12, Section 12.7, ―Avoiding a disproportionate counter-mischief‖, ―Bennion‖.

155
construction in question. The court also has in mind the consequences for
the public welfare.
****
Often it is reasonable to assume that a particular consequence was quite
unforeseen by Parliament. Enacted law suffers by comparison with
unwritten law in that it involves laying down in advance an untried
remedy…‖
258. Further, Bennion on avoiding a disproportionate counter-mischief,
has observed in Section 12.7:
―12.7 The presumption against absurdity means that the courts will
generally avoid adopting a construction that cures the mischief the
enactment was designed to remedy only at the cost of establishing another
mischief.‖
259. The learned author Justice G.P. Singh has further observed that
regard must be had to the subject and object of the Act. The court‘s effort is
to harmonise the words of the statute with the subject of enactment and the
object the legislature has in view. When two interpretations are feasible, the
court will prefer the one which advances the remedy and suppresses the
mischief as envisioned. The relevant portion is extracted below:155
―As stated earlier (Chapter 1, title 2 ―Intention of the Legislature‖, text and
Notes 59 to 71, pp 15 to 17) and as approved by the Supreme Court: ―The
words of a statute, when there is doubt about their meaning, are to be
understood in the sense in which they best harmonise with the subject of the
enactment and the object which the legislature has in view. Their meaning is
found not so much in a strictly grammatical or etymological propriety of
language, nor even in its popular use, as in the subject or in the occasion on
which they are used, and the object to be attained‖. (Workmen of Dimakuchi
Tea Estate v Management of Dimakuchi Tea Estate, AIR 1958 SC 353, p
356). The courts have declined ―to be bound by the letter, when it frustrates
the patent purposes of the statute‖. (Cabell v Markham, 148 F 2d 737 (2d
Cir 1945) (Learned Hand J)). In the words of Shah J, ―It is a recognised rule
of interpretation of statutes that the expressions used therein should

155 G.P. Singh, Principles of Statutory Interpretation, ―Regard to Subject and Object - General‖ (A.K.
Patnaik rev., LexisNexis, 14th Edn, 2016), ―Justice G.P. Singh‖.

156
ordinarily be understood in a sense in which they best harmonise with the
object of the statute and which effectuate the object of the legislature‖. (New
India Sugar Mills Ltd v Commissioner of Sales Tax, Bihar, AIR 1963 SC
1207, p 1213). Therefore when two interpretations are feasible the court will
prefer that which advances the remedy and suppresses the mischief as the
legislature envisioned. (Carew & Co Ltd v UOI, AIR 1975 SC 2260, p
2270). The Court should adopt an object-oriented approach keeping in mind
the principle that legislative futility is to be ruled out so long as
interpretative possibility permits. (Busching Schmitz Private Ltd v PT
Menghani, AIR 1977 SC 1569, pp 1575, 1576). The object-oriented
approach, however, cannot be carried to the extent of doing violence to the
plain language used by rewriting the section or substituting words in place
of the actual words used by the legislature. (CIT v Budhraja and Co., AIR
1993 SC 2529, p 2535).
Having regard to the object of the U.P. Bhoodan Yagna Act, 1953 to
implement the Bhoodan movement, which aimed at distribution of land to
landless labourers who were versed in agriculture and who had no other
means of subsistence, it was held that the expression ‗landless persons‘ in
Section 14, which made provision for grant of land to landless persons, was
limited to landless labourers as described above and did not include a
landless businessman residing in a city. (UP Bhoodan Yagna Samiti v Braj
Kishore, AIR 1993 SC 2529, p 2535).‖
260. Justice G.P. Singh has also observed that it is the court‘s duty to
avoid hardship, inconvenience, injustice, absurdity and anomaly while
selecting out of different interpretations. The doctrine must be applied with
great care and in case absurd inconvenience is to be caused that
interpretation has to be avoided. The relevant discussion is extracted
hereunder:
―(a) Hardship, inconvenience, injustice, absurdity and anomaly to be
avoided
In selecting out of different interpretations ―the court will adopt that which
is just, reasonable and sensible rather than that which is none of those
things‘ (Holmes v Bradfield Rural District Council, (1949) 1 All ER 381, p
384 (KBD) (Finnemore J)), as it may be presumed ―that the Legislature
should have used the word in that interpretation which least offends our
sense of justice‖. (Simms v Registrar of Probates, (1900) AC 323, p 335
(PC) (Lord Hobhouse)). If the grammatical construction leads to some

157
absurdity or some repugnance or inconsistency with the rest of the
instrument, it may be departed from so as to avoid that absurdity and
inconsistency. (Grey v Pearson, (1857) 6 HLC 61, p 106). Similarly, a
construction giving rise to anomalies should be avoided. (Veluswami Thevar
v G Raja Nainar, AIR 1959 SC 422, pp 427, 428). As approved by
Venkatarama Aiyar, J.: Where the language of a statute, in its ordinary
meaning and grammatical construction, leads to a manifest contradiction of
the apparent purpose of the enactment, or to some inconvenience or
absurdity, hardship or injustice, presumably not intended, a construction
may be put upon it which modifies the meaning of the words, and even the
structure of the sentence.‖ (Tirath Singh v Bachittar Singh, AIR 1955 SC
830, p 833).‖
****
―Consideration of hardship, injustice or absurdity as avoiding a particular
construction is a rule which must be applied with great care. ―The argument
ab inconvenient‖, said Lord Moulton, ―is one which requires to be used with
great caution‖. (Vacher & Sons v London Society of Compositors, (1913)
AC 107 : (1911-13) All ER Rep 241, p 252 (HL)). Explaining why great
caution is necessary Lord Moulton further observed, ―There is a danger that
it may degenerate into mere judicial criticism of the propriety of the Acts of
legislature. We have to interpret statutes according to the language used
therein, and, though occasionally the respective consequences of two rival
interpretations may guide us in our choice between them, it can only be
where, taking the Act as a whole, and viewing it in connection with the
existing state of the law at the time of the passing of the Act, we can satisfy
ourselves that the words can have been used in the sense to which the
argument points‖. (Vacher & Sons v London Society of Compositors, (1913)
AC 107 : (1911-13) All ER Rep 241, pp 252, 253 (HL)). According to Brett,
LJ, the inconvenience necessitating a departure from the ordinary sense of
the words should not only be great but should also be what he calls an
―absurd inconvenience‖. Moreover, individual cases of hardship or injustice
have no bearing for rejecting the natural construction (Young & Co v Royal
Leamington Spa Corp, (1883) 8 AC 517, p 527 (HL)), and it is only when
the natural construction leads to some general hardship or injustice and
some other construction is reasonably open that the natural construction
may be departed from. It is often found that laws enacted for the general
advantage do result in individual hardship; for example laws of Limitation,
Registration, Attestation although enacted for the public benefit, may work
injustice in particular cases but that is hardly any reason to depart from the
normal rule to relieve the supposed hardship or injustice in such cases.
(Lucy v Henleys Telegraph Works, (1969) 3 All ER 456, p 465 (CA)). ―It is
the duty of all courts of justice‖, said Lord Campbell, ―to take care for the
general good of the community, that hard cases do not make bad law‖. (East

158
India Co v Odichurn Paul, 7 Moo (PC) 85: (1849) 5 Moo Ind App 43, p 69
(PC)). ―Absurdity‖ according to Willes J, should be understood ―in the same
sense as repugnance that is to say something which would be so absurd with
reference to the other words of the statute as to amount to a repugnance‖.
(Christopherson v Lotinga, (1864) 33 LJ CP 121, p 123). ―Absurdity‖, said
Lord Greene MR, ―like public policy, is a very unruly horse‖. (Grundt v
Great Boulder Pty Gold Mines Ltd, (1948) 1 All ER 21, p 29 (CA)). He
proceeded to add:
There is one rule, I think which is clear … that, although the absurdity or the
non-absurdity of one conclusion as compared with another may be, and very
often is, of assistance to the court in choosing between two possible
meanings of ambiguous words, it is a doctrine which has to be applied with
great care, remembering that Judges may be fallible in this question of an
absurdity and in any event it must not be applied so as to result in twisting
language into a meaning which it cannot bear. It is a doctrine which must
not be used to rewrite the language in a way different from that in which it
was originally framed. (Grundt v Great Boulder Pty Gold Mines Ltd, (1948)
1 All ER 21, pp 29, 30 (CA)).
The alternative construction contended for must be such which does not put
an undue strain on the words used; (Kanailal Sur v Paramnidhi Sadhukhan,
AIR 1957 SC 907, p 911) and does not require recasting of the Act or any
part of it. It must be possible to spell the meaning contended for out of the
words actually used. (Shamrao V Parulekar v District Magistrate, Thane,
AIR 1952 SC 324, p 327).
No doubt in cases of ambiguity that construction which better serves the
ends of fairness and justice will be accepted, but otherwise it is for the
legislature in forming its policy to consider these elements. (IRC v Mutual
Investment Co, (1966) 3 All ER 265, p 268 (PC)).
The courts should as far as possible avoid a construction which results in
anomalies. (NT Veluswami Thevar v G Raja Nainar, AIR 1959 SC 422, pp
427, 428).‖
261. In R v. Roberts, the accused‘s front garden was treated at trial as a
―public place‖ under Section 139 of the Criminal Justice Act, 1988, because
harm could be inflicted from it upon persons in the adjacent street. This
extension was rejected as it would also make the status of a garden depend
upon its wall and could turn part of a room abutting the street into a public

159
place. Para. 5 concludes that ―in our view such a construction could not
possibly be correct.‖
156
262. In Public Prosecutor v. Oie Hee Koi, Section 57(1) of the Malayan
Internal Security Act, 1960, prohibited ―any person‖ from carrying arms
without lawful authority. The Privy Council, while interpreting the
provision, held that although ―any person‖ appeared wide enough to include
everybody, it excludes the soldiers. Words, it was highlighted, had to be
understood in the context and purpose of the Act.157
263. A reference may also be made to the decision of the Supreme Court in
Sonic Surgical v. National Insurance Co. Ltd.158 In this case, insured
premises, the fire policy, and the claim were connected with Ambala, but the
complaint was brought at Chandigarh on the basis of the insurer‘s branch
office being there. The Supreme Court found that allowing a claim to be
filed at a forum where no cause of action accrues but only a branch of the
insurer is present, would amount to plain absurdity. It may also lead to the
parties indulging in bench hunting. The Supreme Court categorically
accepted that it is departing from the plain and literal words, but noted that
such a departure sometimes is necessary. Para. 10 of the decision reads as
under:
―10. …In our opinion, an interpretation has to be given to the amended
Section 17(2)(b) of the Act, which does not lead to an absurd
consequence. If the contention of the learned counsel for the appellant
is accepted, it will mean that even if a cause of action has arisen in
Ambala, then too the complainant can file a claim petition even in
Tamil Nadu or Gauhati or anywhere in India where a branch office of

156R v. Roberts(Leroy Lloyd), [2003] EWCA Crim 2753, [2004] 1 WLR 181.
157 Public Prosecutor v. Oie Hee Koi, [1968] AC 829 (Privy Council).
158 (2010) 1 SCC 135.

160
the Insurance Company is situated. We cannot agree with this
contention. It will lead to absurd consequences and lead to bench-hunting. In our opinion, the expression ―branch office‖ in the amended
Section 17(2) would mean the branch office where the cause of action has
arisen. No doubt this would be departing from the plain and literal words of
Section 17(2)(b) of the Act but such departure is sometimes necessary (as it
is in this case) to avoid absurdity.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
264. The absurdity taken note of by the Supreme Court in Sonic Surgical
gets presented before this Court through the route of Section 20 of the CPC
and on occasions the special provisions. The same concerns of Bench and
court hunting, luxury tourism, and forum shopping arise in connection with
an unrestricted application of Section 20 of the CPC. On the basis of a
remote and minuscule fraction of a cause of action, it is contended that a suit
is to be maintained in Delhi. The interpretation expounded in this judgment
is intended to tame this very unruly horse. But for this, in cases, where
jurisdiction is sought to be established by the plaintiff on the basis that
deliveries took place at a given forum, or that copyrighted material is
accessed at a given place, the plaintiff, can virtually choose any place in
India to sue the defendant at. Plainly, this is absurd and goes against the very
purpose of having jurisdictional provisions.
265. In Horlicks Ltd. and Anr. v. Heinz India (Pvt.) Ltd.,
159
a Division
Bench of this Court held that the principles of forum conveniens do not
apply to civil suits which are governed by the CPC. The decisions of the
Supreme Court in Dashrath and Harish Nigam, were rendered after the
decision in Horlicks. Both these judgments hold that the doctrine of forum
conveniens applies to civil proceedings and civil suits.

159 2009 SCC OnLine Del 3342.

161
266. The view expressed by a Coordinate Bench in Federal Express
Corporation v. Fedex Securities Ltd. & Ors.,
160 that the observations in
para. 12 of Dashrath were not an application of forum conveniens to Section
20, was a passing obiter which does not form part of the ratio decidendi. At
para. 29 of the said decision, the Court concluded that no part of cause of
action had accrued within the jurisdiction of Delhi. The doctrine of forum
non conveniens can only be applied once the court concludes that it does
have jurisdiction but ought to relegate a party to a different forum. In
Federal Express, therefore, there was no occasion for the Court to render
binding findings on the view of the Supreme Court in Dashrath.
267. Importantly, in the celebrated commentary of Mulla on the Civil
Procedure Code,161 the decision of Dashrath is not treated as an exceptional
case but rather is considered under the heading on the approach to be taken
while interpreting Section 20 of the CPC.
268. Though the decisions in Dashrath and Harish Nigam in and of
themselves are sufficient to depart away from the view taken in Horlicks, to
some extent the judgment of the Division Bench of this Court may be
analysed.
269. At the outset, it may be noted that the decision in Horlicks does not
refer to a previously decided Division Bench decision of our Court in
Sanjay Dalia, where our Court, in paras. 5 and 6, applies the doctrine of
forum non-conveniens to civil suits. The judgment in Horlicks, thus, may be
termed as being rendered without adverting to a binding precedent of this

160 2017 SCC OnLine Del 7906.
161 21st Ed., 2026.

162
Court. On this short ground, again, the decision in Horlicks may not be
termed as a precedent. The material portion of the judgment in Sanjay Dalia
reads as under:
―5. A plain reading of Section 20 of the CPC apparently allows the Plaintiff
a multitude of choices where it may institute its suit. Corporations and
partnership firms, and even sole proprietorship concerns, could well be
transacting business simultaneously in several cities. If sub-sections (a) and
(b) of Section 20 are to be interpreted disjunctively from sub-section (c), as
the use of the word ―or‖ appears to allow, the Plaintiff could file a suit
where the cause of action may have arisen, regardless of whether the
Defendant has even a subordinate office at that place. However, if the
Defendant‘s location is to form the fulcrum of jurisdiction, and it has an
office also at the place where the cause of action has occurred, the Plaintiff
is precluded from instituting the suit anywhere else. Obviously, this is
because at every other place would constitute a forum non conveniens.
6. It is noteworthy that the Supreme Court has dwelt on the various
hues of this problem of the place of suing in several cases and has gone
to the extent of laying down that it should be court’s endeavour to find
out the place where the cause of action has substantially arisen and
reject others where it may have incidentally arisen. We need not delve
further on this point beyond making a reference to Oil & Natural Gas
Commission –vs- Utpal Kumar Basu, JT 1994 (5) SC1 and South East Asia
Shipping Co. Ltd. –vs- Nav Bharat Enterprises Pvt. Ltd., (1996) 3 SCC
443….‖
(Emphasis supplied)
270. Additionally, the reasoning in Horlicks may be considered. The
Division Bench held that the principle of forum non conveniens does not
apply to civil suits on the grounds that: firstly, invocation of Section 151 of
the CPC to apply the doctrine of forum conveniens is impermissible as it is
contrary to the substantive provisions of the CPC. Secondly, the doctrine of
forum non-conveniens is purportedly the flip-side of the principle governing
anti-suit injunctions. Since anti-suit injunctions cannot be issued by a

163
domestic forum against a domestic forum, therefore, the doctrine of forum
non-conveniens also cannot be invoked in the domestic sphere.162
271. Concerning the first reason noted above, the discussion made in paras.
234-237 of this judgment reveals that the provisions of the CPC are not in
conflict with the doctrine of forum conveniens. Rather, the wording of the
provisions allow for this principle to be housed in the Code. The second
reason warrants a few observations being made.
272. In Horlicks the Division Bench also took note of the judgments
passed by the Calcutta High Court applying the doctrine of forum
conveniens. The Court, however, was of the opinion that post the decision of
the Supreme Court in Cotton Corporation of India Ltd. v. United Industrial
Bank Limited,
163 the doctrine of forum non-conveniens cannot be applied in
the domestic context. In Cotton Corporation of India, the issue before the
Supreme Court was whether in view of Section 41(b) of the Specific Relief
Act, 1963, a court will have jurisdiction to grant an injunction restraining
any person from instituting any proceeding in a court not subordinate to that
from which the injunction is sought. The Supreme Court found that it is
impermissible. The issue, however, being analysed in this judgment is
slightly different. Anti-suit injunctions and forum conveniens may be two
arms of the same body, but merely because one is cut, does not, ipso facto,
mean that the other needs to be severed.
273. The fundamental rationale for preventing a domestic court to issue an
injunction against another domestic court not subordinate to that from which

162 2009 SCC OnLine Del 3342, para. 65.
163 (1983) 4 SCC 625.

164
the injunction is sought, is hierarchy. The legislature did not find it
appropriate that a court which does not have superintendence and
jurisdiction over another court should be issuing injunction orders
concerning it. This discomfiture of the parliament does not arise when the
doctrine of forum conveniens is invoked. Under this doctrine, the court
which has jurisdiction, and before which a suit is maintainable refuses to
entertain the suit on grounds of appropriateness.
274. Additionally, conclusion (ii) noted in para. 92 of Horlicks, declares
that the doctrine of forum conveniens can be invoked by a writ court. A High
Court exercising writ jurisdiction, however, cannot injunct a party from
approaching another High Court. If the reasoning of Horlicks was taken to
its logical conclusion, the doctrine of forum non-conveniens could also not
be applied to writ proceedings. In any case, if this principle was restricted to
being a flip side of anti-suit injunctions, proceedings under Article 226 of
the Constitution of India, not being suits, could not have been susceptible to
this doctrine. However, writ courts do use, and the Supreme Court has on
numerous occasions, directed the courts to be mindful of the principle of
forum conveniens.
275. The decision of this Court in ICICI Bank Ltd. v. Rathi Syntex Ltd.
and Anr.,
164 noting that the doctrine of forum conveniens does not apply to
civil proceedings was rendered without adverting to the decision of the
Supreme Court in Dashrath. Even otherwise, the decision of Harish Nigam,
is a subsequent decision of the Supreme Court noting that forum conveniens
applies to ordinary civil proceedings. Further, the issues highlighted in the

164 2015:DHC:9246.

165
present judgment were not raised in ICICI Bank. It is trite law that a
judgment is a precedent only on what it decides and not what can be inferred
from it.
276. Before delving further, an argument raised by counsel in a few
matters may be considered. It is submitted by them that the use of the
expression ―in part‖ under Section 20(c) of the CPC, implies that the
doctrine of forum non-conveniens cannot apply to civil suits. For two
reasons this objection holds no force: the first, Article 226 of the
Constitution of India also uses the expression ―in part‖, and it is nobody‘s
case that the invocation of forum non-conveniens in the context of writ
proceedings is impermissible. The second concerns the purpose of this
expression. As opposed to the earlier civil procedure codes, which used the
language ―where cause of action arises‖ for their residuary jurisdiction
provision, the Code of 1908, used the words ―wholly or in part‖ as well.
This expression is merely to clarify that it is not necessary that the entire
cause of action accrues within the jurisdiction of a given court, in order to
clothe it with jurisdiction. It is not intended to require courts to entertain
suits where a fraction of a cause of action has accrued before a given Court.
277. From the discussion above, it can be concluded that the doctrine of
forum non-conveniens has application in civil suits. The Court can,
therefore, in appropriate cases, where a small or slender part of cause of
action has arisen within the jurisdiction of a given court and a substantial,
material and integral part of the cause of action has arisen within the
jurisdiction of another court, relegate a plaintiff to the latter court.

166
278. The principle of forum conveniens was explained in Kusum Ingots &
Alloys Ltd. v. Union of India and Anr.,
165 in the following words:
―Forum Conveniens
We must, however, remind ourselves that even if a small part of cause of
action arises within the territorial jurisdiction of the High Court, the same by
itself may not be considered to be a determinative factor compelling the
High Court to decide the matter on merit. In appropriate cases, the Court
may refuse to exercise its discretionary jurisdiction by invoking the doctrine
of forum conveniens. (See Bhagar Singh Bagga v. Dewan Jagbir Sawhany,
AIR 1941 Cal; Mandal Jalan v. Madanlal, (1945) 49 CWN 357; Bharat
Coking Coal Limited v. M/s Jharia Talkies & Cold Storage Pvt. Ltd. (1997)
CWN 122; S.S.Jain & Co. & Anr. v. Union of India & Ors. (1994) CHN
445; M/s. New Horizon Ltd. v. Union of India, AIR 1994 Delhi 126) ‖
279. This Court in The Indure Pvt. Ltd. v. Government of NCT of
Delhi,
166 noting the caution of this Court not becoming a pan-India superior
court, opined as under:
―36. A petitioner who approaches this Court to assail a decision of an
authority situated in Delhi, when the underlying cause for the said decision
lies elsewhere, effectively attempts to make this High Court a mini-pan
India Superior Court exercising jurisdiction over all events which take place
throughout this Country. There is no gainsaying with the proposition that
every High Court is competent to adjudicate upon a lis which arises from
events or actions taking place within its territory. Merely because the
ultimate order, which is based on events taking place outside Delhi and
takes cognizance of actions outside of Delhi, is passed within the
jurisdiction of this Court, a writ petition ought not be entertained by this
Court.‖
280. Ultimately, the Court concluded that the substance of a matter must be
adjudged, and not the unchanging constant which is present in action to
arrive at a conclusion on whether to entertain an action in the context of
territorial jurisdiction and forum non conveniens. At para. 42 this Court
observed:

165 (2004) 6 SCC 254.
166 2026:DHC:1605.

167
―42. It is the substance of the matter which the Court must consider in
determining the connection with Delhi. An order being passed by an
authority in Delhi is an unchanging constant. This static/uniform facet,
which is unmoved by the nature of the lis, ought not to determine where
territorial jurisdiction would lie.‖
281. Reference may also be made to the doctrine of the ‗centre of
interests‘, applied in the European Union to determine jurisdiction over
claims concerning, inter alia, the infringement of personality or publicity
rights where harm occurs across multiple Member States. Although rooted
in EU Regulations, the doctrine offers useful guidance on the approach to be
taken qua jurisdiction in such disputes. In appropriate cases, the inquiry
while applying the doctrine of forum conveniens may indeed be where the
centre of interests of a given dispute lies.
282. The centre of interests doctrine holds that in cases particularly those
involving publication over the internet and infringement of
personality/publicity rights, the proper forum for instituting legal
proceedings is the jurisdiction where the plaintiff‘s reputation is most
significantly established and where the alleged publication is likely to cause
the most substantial harm. The doctrine shifts focus from the place of mere
publication to the place where the impact on reputation is the greatest.
283. The plaintiff‘s habitual residence, principal place of business, or the
location of their strongest social and professional connections typically
constitutes this ―centre of interests‖. The doctrine aims to prevent artificial
or opportunistic forum selection and ensures that claims are adjudicated at
the appropriate forum.

168
284. Reference can be made to the decision of eDate Advertising GmbH v.
X,
167 which interpreted Article 5(3) of Council Regulation (EC) No 44/2001
of 22 December 2000 on jurisdiction and the recognition and enforcement of
judgments in civil and commercial matters. In the context of personality
rights being infringed across multiple jurisdictions of the Europe, the Court
of Justice of European168 Union held as under:
―48 The connecting criteria referred to in paragraph 42 of the present
judgment must therefore be adapted in such a way that a person who has
suffered an infringement of a personality right by means of the internet may
bring an action in one forum in respect of all of the damage caused,
depending on the place in which the damage caused in the European Union
by that infringement occurred. Given that the impact which material placed
online is liable to have on an individual‘s personality rights might best be
assessed by the court of the place where the alleged victim has his centre of
interests, the attribution of jurisdiction to that court corresponds to the
objective of the sound administration of justice, referred to in paragraph 40
above.
49 The place where a person has the centre of his interests corresponds in
general to his habitual residence. However, a person may also have the
centre of his interests in a Member State in which he does not habitually
reside, in so far as other factors, such as the pursuit of a professional
activity, may establish the existence of a particularly close link with that
State.
50 The jurisdiction of the court of the place where the alleged victim has the
centre of his interests is in accordance with the aim of predictability of the
rules governing jurisdiction (see Case C‑144/10 BVG [2011] ECR I‑0000,
paragraph 33) also with regard to the defendant, given that the publisher of
harmful content is, at the time at which that content is placed online, in a
position to know the centres of interests of the persons who are the subject
of that content. The view must therefore be taken that the centre-of-interests
criterion allows both the applicant easily to identify the court in which he
may sue and the defendant reasonably to foresee before which court he may
be sued (see Case C‑533/07 Falco Privatstiftung and Rabitsch [2009] ECR
I‑3327, paragraph 22 and the case-law cited).‖

167 Joined Cases C-509/09 & C-161/10 (CJEU, 2011) wherein the Grand Chamber.
168 ―CJEU‖.

169
285. Further, in the case of Bolagsupplysningen OÜ v. Svensk Handel
AB,
169 as regards the identification of the centre of interests of a juristic or
legal person, the CJEU held as under:
―40 As to the identification of the centre of interests, the Court has stated
that, with regard to a natural person, this generally corresponds to the
Member State of his habitual residence. However, such a person may also
have his centre of interests in a Member State in which he does not
habitually reside, in so far as other factors, such as the pursuit of a
professional activity, may establish the existence of a particularly close link
with that State (judgment of 25 October 2011, eDate Advertising and
Others, C‑509/09 and C‑161/10, EU:C:2011:685, paragraph 49).
41 As regards a legal person pursuing an economic activity, such as the
applicant in the main proceedings, the centre of interests of such a person
must reflect the place where its commercial reputation is most firmly
established and must, therefore, be determined by reference to the place
where it carries out the main part of its economic activities. While the centre
of interests of a legal person may coincide with the place of its registered
office when it carries out all or the main part of its activities in the Member
State in which that office is situated and the reputation that it enjoys there is
consequently greater than in any other Member State, the location of that
office is, not, however, in itself, a conclusive criterion for the purposes of
such an analysis.
42 Thus, when the relevant legal person carries out the main part of its
activities in a Member State other than the one in which its registered office
is located, as is the case in the main proceedings, it is necessary to assume
that the commercial reputation of that legal person, which is liable to be
affected by the publication at issue, is greater in that Member State than in
any other and that, consequently, any injury to that reputation would be felt
most keenly there. To that extent, the courts of that Member State are best
placed to assess the existence and the potential scope of that alleged injury,
particularly given that, in the present instance, the cause of the injury is the
publication of information and comments that are allegedly incorrect or
defamatory on a professional site managed in the Member State in which
the relevant legal person carries out the main part of its activities and that
are, bearing in mind the language in which they are written, intended, for the
most part, to be understood by people living in that Member State.‖

169 Case C-194/16 (CJEU, 2017).

170
286. From the discussion above concerning forum non-conveniens the
following conclusions may be drawn:
a. The doctrine of forum non-conveniens shall apply to civil suits.
Once a plaint is found to be maintainable under the CPC or the
special provisions, an inquiry may be undertaken as to whether
the plaint is to be entertained.
b. The application of this doctrine shall take into account the
entirety of avenues that are competent to adjudicate a lis and
have jurisdiction to entertain a suit as per the applicable law.
Specifically, in the context of Section 20(c) of the CPC, this
doctrine shall determine whether ―part‖ cause of action
includes merely an incidental, minuscule, or slender part of the
cause of action. The doctrine shall apply to determine whether
the court before which a plaint is presented is the place where
the material, integral and essential part of the cause of action
has arisen.
c. In order to determine where the material, integral and essential
part of the cause of action has arisen, the Court shall, unless an
exceptional situation warrants otherwise, be confined to the
pleaded facts themselves. It is on the basis of the facts pleaded
by the plaintiff himself, the lis and infringement described by
him, that such a conclusion needs to be arrived at.
G. DOCTRINE OF DEMURRER

171
287. In the present batch of matters, the doctrine of demurrer has
frequently been invoked to insulate a plaint from a deeper judicial scrutiny.
An impression is sought to be created that at the initial pre-trial stage, a
Court is but a silent audience in the theatre acted by the parties.
288. In Exphar SA v. Eupharma Laboratories Ltd.,
170 the Supreme Court,
inter alia, held that an averment made in the plaint that the plaintiff is
carrying on business within the jurisdiction of a given court is sufficient to
maintain a suit in that court. It was observed at para. 35 of Dhodha House
that the questions raised in Dhodha House were not raised in Exphar SA,
and the decision in Exphar SA turned on its own facts. A holistic reading of
Dhodha House and Exphar SA would reveal that the Supreme Court in
Dhodha House sought to restrict the application of Exphar SA to its own
facts. Similarly, in Pfizer Enterprises Sarl v. Cipla Ltd.,
171 while
adjudicating the PM Diesels appeal172 the Division Bench found an
averment contained in the plaint that the defendant was selling its goods in
Delhi to be sufficient to confer jurisdiction on this Court, on the basis of the
demurrer doctrine.
289. The law has travelled far beyond accepting a bald pleading of a
plaintiff that jurisdiction is made out. In today‘s day and age a mere
statement that a court has jurisdiction, or that ―cause of action has arisen
within the jurisdiction of this Court‖, or any variation where a provision is
attempted to be reproduced in the plaint, will not confer jurisdiction. The

170 (2004) 3 SCC 688.
171 2009 (107) DRJ 735 (DB).
172 Two separate appeals were decided vide a common order in Pfizer Enterprises Sarl. One of the appeals
was the PM Diesel appeal (FAO (OS) 428/2008)), the facts of which were narrated by the Division Bench
in para. 2 of the judgment.

172
demurrer doctrine cannot be so interpreted so as to preclude a Court from
discovering and acting upon, what appears to be, clever drafting by a
plaintiff to forum shop and engage in luxury litigation.
290. In T. Arivandhandam v. TV Satyapal and Anr.,
173 VR Krishna Iyer J.
had observed:
―The pathology of litigative addiction ruins the poor of this country and the
Bar has a role to cure this deleterious tendency of parties to launch frivolous
and vexatious cases.
2…The long arm of the law must throttle such litigative caricatures if the
confidence and creditibility of the community in the judicature is to
survive…
5. …The learned Munsif must remember that if on a meaningful — not
formal — reading of the plaint it is manifestly vexatious, and meritless, in
the sense of not disclosing a clear right to sue, he should exercise his power
under Order 7, Rule 11 CPC taking care to see that the ground mentioned
therein is fulfilled. And, if clever drafting has created the illusion of a cause
of action, nip it in the bud at the first hearing by examining the party
searchingly under Order 10, CPC. An activist Judge is the answer to
irresponsible law suits. The trial courts would insist imperatively on
examining the party at the first hearing so that bogus litigation can be shot
down at the earliest stage. The Penal Code is also resourceful enough to
meet such men, (Cr. XI) and must be triggered against them. In this case,
the learned Judge to his cost realised what George Bernard Shaw remarked
on the assassination of Mahatma Gandhi:
―It is dangerous to be too good.‖‖
291. Similarly, in K. Akbar Ali v. K. Umar Khan & Ors.,
174 the Supreme
Court observed:
5. It is well settled that while considering an application under Order 7 Rule
11 CPC, the question before the Court is whether the plaint discloses any
cause of action or whether the suit is barred by any law, on the face of the
averments contained in the plaint itself. While considering an application

173 (1977) 4 SCC 467.
174 (2021) 14 SCC 51.

173
under Order 7 Rule 11 CPC, the Court is not to look into the strength or
weakness of the case of the plaintiff or the defence raised by the defendant.
****
7. In any case, application under Order 7 Rule 11 CPC for rejection of the
plaint requires a meaningful reading of the plaint as a whole. As held by this
Court in ITC Ltd. v. Debts Recovery Appellate Tribunal2, clever drafting
creating illusions of cause of action are not permitted in law and a clear right
to sue should be shown in the plaint. Similarly the Court must see that the
bar in law of the suit is not camouflaged by devious and clever drafting
of the plaint. Moreover, the provisions of Order 7 Rule 11 are not
exhaustive and the Court has the inherent power to see that frivolous or
vexatious litigations are not allowed to consume the time of the Court.
****
8. In this case, a meaningful reading of the plaint as a whole makes it
abundantly clear that the relief claimed in the suit is barred in view of the
restricted scope of the power of attorney given by the first defendant to Mr
Zahir Ali.
****
13. The Division Bench of the High Court has done substantial justice by
nipping in the bud, a suit which is ex facie not maintainable for want of
cause of action against the defendants or any of them, thereby saving
precious judicial time as also inconvenience and expenditure to the parties
to the suit.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
292. Reliance may be placed on the recent decision of the Supreme Court
in Manjula and Ors. v. DA Srinivas,
175 which held as under:
―9.3. Once the plaint is presented for institution, and before it is admitted, it
is the duty of the trial Court to verify the contents of the plaint and ensure
that all legal requirements are satisfied before admitting the plaint. A trial
Court cannot mechanically admit the plaint and register the suit. Admission
of the plaint cannot be a mechanical process by which the note of the
Registry is merely endorsed by the Court. If, at the stage of admission of the
plaint, the trial Court, upon a meaningful reading of the plaint, comes to the
conclusion that the plaint is liable to be rejected, it shall reject the plaint. It

175 2026 INSC 465.

174
is not necessary for the trial Court to wait for the defendant to enter
appearance and seek rejection of the plaint. Once the Court finds that the
suit is frivolous, without jurisdiction, instituted without compliance with
prerequisites, fails to disclose a real cause of action, suppresses material
facts, or is barred by law but couched in clever drafting to create an illusion
of a cause of action, it must reject the plaint with costs. [See
T.Arivandandam v. T.V. Satyapal (supra)]. In this context, it would be
useful to refer to the following judgments of this Court and the observations
made therein:
****
9.4. Every plaint must be presented along with the documents relied upon in
the plaint as per Rule 14 of Order VII. A document is ordinarily relied upon
in the plaint, and the narration in the bundle of facts contribute to the cause
of action. Therefore, it is imperative upon the plaintiff to produce such
document. It is trite law that the plaint can be rejected for failure to produce
documents relied upon or referred to in the plaint. There can be no quarrel
with the well settled position that while considering an application for
rejection of plaint, only the averments in the plaint and the documents filed
along with the plaint can be looked into. Though we agree with the learned
Senior Counsel for the Respondent / Plaintiff on this proposition, the plaint
cannot be read in an incomprehensive manner. What is implied in Order VII
is a meaningful reading of the plaint, because the bar under law may be
either express or by necessary implication. [See T.Arivandandam v. T.V.
Satyapal (supra), Sopan Sukhdeo Sable and others v. Assistant Charity
Commissioner and others25 and Madanuri Sri Rama Chandra Murthy v.
Syed Jalal26].
9.5. Similarly, all material facts have to be stated in the plaint. Material facts
are those facts which create a complete cause of action; those facts which
directly bear upon the maintainability or sustainability of the suit; and those
facts upon adjudication of which may bring an end to the lis. Any
suppression of a material fact, which has the effect of creating an illusory
cause of action and eclipsing the legal bar, ought to be dealt with firmly, and
the plaint would be liable to be summarily rejected. It is also settled law that
a person who has suppressed a material fact is not entitled to any relief.
Suppression of a material fact within the knowledge of the party amounts to
fraud upon the Court. The relevancy or otherwise of a fact is to be decided
by the Court, and parties cannot contend that they omitted a material fact on
the assumption that it was not relevant. It is not only the duty of the Court to
summarily reject the claim of a party suppressing a material fact, but also to
ensure that any benefit obtained by such party is undone and status quo ante
restored in its fairness and equity. The consistent view of this Court,
irrespective of whether it is a petition under Article 136 of the Constitution,
a writ petition under Article 226 of the Constitution, or in any proceedings

175
before a civil, criminal, judicial or quasi-judicial forum is that the parties
must disclose all material facts in their pleadings, and that a party, who
suppresses any material fact is not entitled to any relief.
****
28.2. At the same time, the power to reject a plaint at the threshold under
Order VII Rule 11 CPC is a serious jurisdiction to be exercised with due
circumspection. While genuine causes must not be shut out prematurely,
courts are equally bound to prevent misuse of judicial process where the
pleadings, on their own showing, disclose no enforceable right or reveal a
claim barred by law. The provision thus serves as an important filter,
balancing access to justice with the need to prevent frivolous, vexatious, or
legally untenable claims from being carried to trial.
****
29. The conspectus of our discussion and findings may be summarised thus:
****
(vii) Courts below must curtail frivolous suits which are barred by
law, and cases where the cause of action disclosed is illusory, by
piercing the veil of clever drafting and giving a meaningful and
wholesome reading to the plaint and accompanying documents,
preferably at the earliest stage of the suit.‖
293. The doctrine of demurrer also has to be balanced with the obligation
of the Court to nip in the bud frivolous litigation. The Court is bound to
meaningfully read the plaint and if found appropriate pierce through
averments to identify the missing material facts, internal contradictions, or a
legal bar, which have been cleverly omitted only to create an illusion of
jurisdiction before this Court.
294. From the discussion above concerning the doctrine of demurrer the
following conclusions may be drawn:
a. The doctrine of demurrer which required every averment in the
plaint, whatever it may be, to be taken as truth at the pre-trial
stage of a litigation, has been diluted.

176
b. Today, the Court is fully empowered to pierce through clever
drafting where pleadings have been made solely to take
advantage of the demurrer doctrine and to create an illusion of
jurisdiction before this Court.
c. Bald, unfounded, and empty narrations unsupported by any
documents ought to be ignored while determining whether the
Court has the requisite jurisdiction to entertain a suit.
III. CONCLUSIONS
295. The analysis made above would lead to the following conclusions:
A. THE PRAYERS TO BE TESTED
(i) A suit for infringement is different from that of passing off. In a given
case jurisdiction may be established for infringement but not for passing off.
If a composite suit is filed seeking reliefs for both infringement and passing
off, it must be established that jurisdiction is made out for each relief. If it is
found that a court does not have jurisdiction to proceed with any of the
reliefs, the suit cannot proceed.
B. MEANING OF THE EXPRESSION “CARRIES ON BUSINESS”
(i) The meaning and import of the expression ―carries on business‖ as it
appears in Sections 20(a) and (b) of the CPC, Sections 134(2) and 62(2) of
the Trade Marks Act and Copyright Act, respectively, is governed by the
pronouncements of the Supreme Court in, inter alia, Patel Roadways,
Dhodha House and Sanjay Dalia, as also the dictum of this Court in Ultra
Home.

177
(ii) If a corporation has a sole office, it shall be deemed to carry on
business at that place of its sole office. If, however, there is more than one
office, it must be deciphered where the principal place of business of that
corporation is. This principal office shall ordinarily be the place of its
registered office. Once the principal office is discovered, the other offices
shall be the sub-ordinate offices/branch offices.
(iii) A corporation shall be deemed to carry on business at its sub-ordinate
office if the cause of action for a given suit accrues at that place. In such a
scenario it shall not be open for the company to sue a defendant at the place
of its principal office, where no cause of action has accrued.
C. CAUSE OF ACTION (WHOLLY OR IN PART) FOR A QUIA
TIMET ACTION
(i) The cause of action for a quia timet action accrues when the defendant
expresses a deliberate intention to engage in activity the result of which
would raise a strong possibility of infringement.
(ii) The activity to be engaged in must be imminent, and of such a
character that if the defendant were to engage in the activity, the plaintiff
would suffer substantial, if not irreparable, prejudice.
(iii) For goods and services that are subject to regulatory control, in
determining whether a quia timet action is maintainable, it is essential to
consider whether the defendant holds the required licences or permissions,
statutory or otherwise, required for their sale, use or any other commercial
exploitation. For instance, for the commercial sale of liquor, an excise
license under the State‘s specific Excise Act is required; for manufacturing a

178
pharmaceutical product, a license under the Drugs and Cosmetics Act, 1940
may be required; and for the sale of insecticides a license under the
Insecticides Act, 1968 may be required.
D. CAUSE OF ACTION (WHOLLY OR IN PART) FOR A PASSING
OFF ACTION
(i) Cause of action (wholly or in part) can be said to have arisen within
the territorial jurisdiction of a court only if the defendant has made sales of
the goods carrying the impugned trademark within the jurisdiction of a given
Court. Direct sales are made by the defendant not to an individual consumer
but to a distributor, wholesaler or a retailer. Such sales are at a commercial
scale. Mere availability of goods within the jurisdiction of a forum Court
does not confer jurisdiction.
(ii) Sale of goods within the territorial jurisdiction of the forum court by a
firm which does not act as a distributor, wholesaler, retailer, or agent of the
defendant, does not confer jurisdiction. Where goods are procured by a firm
from another firm that is outside the jurisdiction of the forum court and in
turn sold within the territory of the forum court, jurisdiction shall not be
conferred.
(iii) Additionally, delivery of goods to the agent/carrier of the plaintiff or
its investigator, for onward transmission, is deemed delivery upon the
plaintiff or its investigator. This conclusion is not restricted to suits for
passing off, but is a general conclusion arrived at by the application of
Section 39 of the Sale of Goods Act.

179
E. CAUSE OF ACTION (WHOLLY OR IN PART) FOR AN ACTION
FOR INFRINGEMENT IN THE OFFLINE WORLD
(i) Cause of action (wholly or in part) can be said to have arisen within
the territorial jurisdiction of a court when the defendant, in the course of
trade, uses a mark which is identical with or deceptively similar to the trade
mark, in relation to goods or services in respect of which the trade mark is
registered, within the jurisdiction of the forum court.
(ii) Registration of a mark within the jurisdiction of a given court does not
constitute part of the cause of action.
F. CAUSE OF ACTION (WHOLLY OR IN PART) FOR AN ACTION
FOR INFRINGEMENT IN THE ONLINE WORLD
(i) To establish that cause of action (wholly or in part) has arisen through
the means of website activity, the plaintiff must demonstrate that the
defendant has purposefully availed itself of the jurisdiction of the forum
court by specifically targeting persons within its territory and it resulting in
an injury or harm to the plaintiff within the forum state.
(ii) Additionally, the plaintiff must also demonstrate the conclusion of
commercially concluded transactions between the defendant and persons
within the forum court‘s jurisdiction through the website in question.
(iii) The requirement of prima facie demonstrating commercially
concluded transactions taking place can be relaxed in a case where the
plaintiff cannot invoke the provisions of Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks

180
Act and Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act, and the defendant has no
presence in India at all.
G. “DYNAMIC EFFECT” AND WHETHER IT CAN CONFER
JURISDICTION
(i) If the principle of dynamic effect is applied to cases of infringement,
the amendment to the Trade Marks Act and the Copyright Act shall be
rendered otiose. Jurisdiction, therefore, cannot be established on the basis of
this principle.
(ii) An attempt to find jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of the CPC on the
basis that the effect of the infringement is felt at the place where the plaintiff
has its presence, also, goes against the spirit of the specialised provisions. It
also renders the amendments nugatory. Such an interpretation, thus,
ordinarily ought to be avoided.
H. WHETHER THE USE OF AN “INVESTIGATOR” IS
PERMISSIBLE
(i) As a rule, the use of an investigator or any other person set up by the
plaintiff to find jurisdiction before a forum court amounts to concocting
jurisdiction. The findings of such an investigator, thus, ordinarily, cannot be
relied upon.
(ii) The two exceptions to this rule are: first, where the plaintiff can prima
facie demonstrate, on the basis of his plaint and the documents attached with
it, that but for such an ―investigator‖ being hired to plead jurisdiction, cause
of action under Section 20(c) of the CPC could not be maintained before

181
another court. And second, where ex facie it can be demonstrated that sales
of the impugned goods are taking place within a forum court, and an
investigator merely provides evidence. For instance, if the defendant has
readily supplied its goods within Delhi to various retailers, distributors and
stockists, and the plaintiff‘s investigator buys these goods from these classes
of persons, that report may not be discarded.
I. THE DOCTRINE OF FORUM NON-CONVENIENS
(i) The doctrine of forum non-conveniens shall apply to civil suits. Once
a plaint is found to be maintainable under the CPC or the special provisions,
an inquiry may be undertaken as to whether the plaint is to be entertained.
(ii) The application of this doctrine shall take into account the entirety of
avenues that are competent to adjudicate a lis and have jurisdiction to
entertain a suit as per the applicable law. Specifically, in the context of
Section 20(c) of the CPC, this doctrine shall determine whether ―part‖ cause
of action includes merely an incidental, minuscule, or slender part of the
cause of action. The doctrine shall apply to determine whether the court
before which a plaint is presented is the place where the material, integral
and essential part of the cause of action has arisen.
(iii) In order to determine where the material, integral and essential part of
the cause of action has arisen, the Court shall, unless an exceptional
situation otherwise warrants, be confined to the pleaded facts themselves. It
is on the basis of the facts pleaded by the plaintiff himself, the lis and
infringement described by him, that such a conclusion needs to be arrived at.
J. DOCTRINE OF DEMURRER

182
(i) The doctrine of demurrer which required every averment in the plaint,
whatever it may be, to be taken as truth at the pre-trial stage of a litigation,
has been diluted. Today, the Court is fully empowered to pierce through
clever drafting where pleadings have been made solely to take advantage of
the demurrer doctrine and to create an illusion of jurisdiction before this
Court. Bald, unfounded, and empty narrations unsupported by any
documents ought to be ignored while determining whether the Court has the
requisite jurisdiction to entertain a suit.
IV. ADJUDICATING THE ISSUE OF TERRITORIAL
JURISDICTION
296. Considering the prolonged discussion above, and the number of
conclusions reached, it is found appropriate to briefly discuss in a
methodological manner, the inquiry which a Court is to undertake while
deciding on the issue of territoriality. Though an exhaustive and straight-jacketed elucidation of cases which may arise before this Court is not
possible, an endeavour is being made, to at least lay down some basic tests
to be applied in the cases of copyright or trademark infringement and
passing off:

provisions, and the caselaw discussed in this judgment may be applied:

that the impugned products are

Such an empty averment without
supporting documents may amount to a

concoct jurisdiction. It shall amount to

cause of action at Delhi when there exists
none. Simply on the basis of the pleading,
jurisdiction of Delhi cannot be made out.
298. Once a plaint is found to be maintainable under the CPC or the
special provisions, in an appropriate case, the Court may decline to entertain
a plaint on grounds of forum non-conveniens. Therefore, notwithstanding the
finding rendered on whether a suit is maintainable under Section 20, or the
special provisions, the Court may, in an appropriate case relegate a party to
another forum by invoking the doctrine of forum non-conveniens.

188
299. While analyzing a case on the touchstone of this doctrine, the Court
must take notice of all jurisdictional avenues open to a plaintiff given the
facts pleaded in the plaint. Thus, if jurisdiction of this Court is invoked
under Section 20(a) of the CPC, but on the pleaded facts, jurisdiction of
Mumbai can also be invoked under Section 20(c) of the CPC, a scrutiny
must be made of the plaint as to why Delhi is being approached and whether
another forum is forum conveniens.
300. Illustratively, the following non-exhaustive examples are being given,
to elucidate the circumstances in which the Court may exercise the doctrine
of forum conveniens.
TABLE 3
S.
No.
Grounds pleaded to
invoke jurisdiction of
Delhi.
Application of forum non-conveniens
1. Defendant has had a
conference in Delhi that
it is about to release its
products in Delhi. A suit
is filed in Delhi as a quia
timet action on grounds
that an immediate
injunction restraining the
defendant‘s actions is
Such a suit ordinarily ought to be entertained. The
Court must, however, satisfy itself by meaningfully
reading the plaint and supporting documents that
the threat at Delhi is prima facie real and imminent.
For this, the Court may also, in an appropriate case,
invoke powers under Section 168 of the BSA and
question the parties.

189
required.
2. A suit for trademark or
copyright infringement
is filed at Delhi on the
sole grounds that the
goods of the defendant
are being sold pan-India
including at Delhi.
Delhi may not be the forum conveniens to entertain
such a suit. Goods of the defendant are also being
sold at the place where the defendant or the
plaintiff have its presence. The manufacturing of
the impugned product, its subsequent packaging
and labelling by using the plaintiff‘s mark or
copyrighted work constitute a significantly more
important part of the cause of action than the
fraction available at Delhi.
3. Both the plaintiff and the
defendant are outside
Delhi and a suit for
trademark or copyright
infringement is filed
before this Court
claiming that the goods
of the defendant are
being sold pan-India
including at Delhi.
The plaintiff in such a case leaves his territory,
does not exercise its statutory rights under the
special provisions to sue at his own place, neither
institutes a suit where the defendant has its
presence, but rather approaches this Court.
In addition to the inquiry noted at serial number 2,
the court engages in an additional inquiry.
Despite the availability of the same kind and
proportion of the cause of action existing at the
plaintiff‘s place, when the plaintiff approaches
another Court, an obvious question arises qua
plaintiff‘s bona fides and as to whether it is
intending to institute a suit before a court that is not
forum conveniens. He remains answerable for

190
choosing a court which, subject to pleadings to the
contrary, prima facie is forum non conveniens.
4. The goods of the
defendant were
purchased from Flipkart
or Amazon with a Delhi
address, or were
purchased from the
defendant who delivered
it at Delhi.
Delhi may not be forum conveniens to entertain
such a suit. Goods of the defendant can also be
delivered to the place where either the plaintiff or
the defendant has its presence.
The acts of the plaintiff which were consciously
and strategically targeted to cause certain acts to
happen within the local limits of this Court,
arguably may constitute a part of a cause of action,
but that ought not to make Delhi the forum
conveniens.
This purported cause of action is capable of being
created in any part of the country, if a plaintiff‘s
voluntary acts were to obligate a court to entertain
a lis, the concept of the ―local limits‖ of a court
shall be thrown to the winds.
5. A film or content is
accessible at Delhi.
Access to a film or content, particularly under the
Copyright Act may amount to publication. But this
sole fact occurring at Delhi may not be sufficient to
entertain a suit. The access to a content is merely
the incidental result of uploading/uprooting which
may occur at another forum. It is the place from
where uprooting is taking place, which is the forum

191
conveniens. There may be cases where the place of
uprooting is not discernible despite reasonable
efforts, in such a case, prima facie, subject to
pleadings to the contrary, the court within whose
―local limits‖ the plaintiff resides, carries on
business, or personally works for gain, is the forum
conveniens.
V. APPLICATION TO FACTS
301. Turning now to the facts of each case.
ABD Maestro Private Limited v. Great Galleon Ventures Ltd.
[CS(COMM)-987/2026]
302. The plaintiff has filed the suit for copyright, trade mark, and trade
dress/label infringement, along with an action for passing off, and other
consequential reliefs thereto. The plaintiff-company is engaged in the
business of, inter alia, manufacturing, marketing, sale and distribution of
alcoholic beverages. The plaintiff claims to be the proprietor and exclusive
user of the ‗YELLO‘ trade dress, mark and label, which encompasses a
vibrant yellow colour, a specific arrangement of the label, and a distinctive
overall presentation. It is contended that the defendant by its product ‗V21‘
vodka is infringing upon the intellectual property rights of the plaintiff. It is
claimed that the defendant‘s product has a deceptively similar yellow-and-black bottle design, label and overall trade dress, thereby misappropriating
the plaintiff‘s goodwill and causing a likelihood of consumer confusion.

192
303. Paras. 50 and 51 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
“50. This Hon‘ble Court has the necessary jurisdiction to try and entertain
the present suit as per Section 20 of Code of Civil Procedure, 1908, on
account of the following factors:
a. The Defendant is actively engaged in the commercial
distribution and sale of its products, which are found all over the
markets of New Delhi.
b. The Defendant‘s website is accessible from any location within
New Delhi and the National Capital Region (NCR) and provides an
interactive platform through which the Defendant advertises,
markets the impugned products and further, prospective customers
can contact the Defendant by submitting enquiries and
communicating with the Defendant for commercial purposes. The
website is directed at consumers across India, including within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The Defendant‘s
various social media accounts are also accessible within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and advertise the products of the
Defendant.
c. The Defendant is also registered in Delhi, bearing GSTIN No.
07AABCK1743N1ZE, with its Principal Place of Business situated
at A-33, Ground Floor, Naraina Industrial area, Phase l, New
Delhi, South West Delhi, Delhi, 110028, which falls within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The said GST
registration evidences the Defendant‘s commercial presence and
business activities within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble
Court.
51. This Hon‘ble Court has the necessary jurisdiction to try and entertain the
present matter as per Section 134 (2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999 read with
Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act, 1957, the present suit being one inter
alia for infringement of the Plaintiffs copyright in the original artistic work
comprising the ‗YELLO‘ label, as the Plaintiff, is carrying on business
within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The Plaintiff is carrying out its
business within the territorial limits of this Hon‘ble Court in a variety of
ways such as (but not limited to) sale of its products under the ‗YELLO‘
trade dress. Thus, the Hon‘ble Court has the necessary territorial jurisdiction
to try and entertain the present suit. Furthermore, and independently, the
Plaintiff, being the owner of the copyright subsisting in the original artistic
work comprising the ‗YELLO‘ label and carrying on business for gain

193
within the territorial limits of this Hon‘ble Court, is entitled to institute the
present suit before this Hon‘ble Court under Section 62(2) of the Copyright
Act, 1957.‖
304. The plaintiff admits that its principal place of business and registered
office is at Mumbai, Maharashtra and outside the jurisdiction of this Court.
The defendant, also, has its registered office outside the jurisdiction of this
Court, at Indore, Madhya Pradesh. For the plaintiff to invoke the jurisdiction
of this Court under Section 20(a) of the CPC, the defendant must ―carry on
business‖ within Delhi. The explanation to the said section provides that in
order for Section 20(a) of the CPC to apply, a corporation shall be
considered to ―carry on business‖ at its sub-ordinate office, only when cause
of action also accrues within the territorial jurisdiction of the Court where
the sub-ordinate office is located.
305. In the instant case the impugned product of the defendant is not sold
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. The picture of the defendant‘s
product attached with the plaint also notes in bold letters ―FOR SALE IN
MADHYA PRADESH ONLY‖. No cause of action, therefore, can accrue
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court.
306. Additionally, applying Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, since the
explanation is deemed to have been incorporated in Sections 62 and 134 of
the Copyright Act and Trade Marks Act, respectively, the option of Delhi is
also not available to the plaintiff under the specialised statutes.
307. The defendant‘s GST registration showing its ―principal place of
business‖ to be at Delhi also does not change the aforenoted finding. As
noted in paras. 115-117 of this judgement, the ―principal place of business‖

194
under the Goods and Services Tax Act is a defined term, having its distinct
meaning. It does not have any bearing to determine as to whether the
defendant carries on business for the purposes of Section 20(a) of the CPC.
308. Insofar as the argument concerning the invocation of jurisdiction on
the basis of the defendant‘s website is concerned, the Banyan Tree standard
has not been satisfied. There is no commercially concluded transaction
between the defendant and persons within the forum court‘s jurisdiction
through the website in question.
309. Lastly, it may also be noted that the plaintiff has cleverly omitted to
plead as to whether the defendant has an excise license in Delhi concerning
the impugned product. As noticed in Shree Nath Heritage, liquor is an
excisable commodity. The sale of such a product, which arguably gives rise
to a cause of action, can only take place when a given corporation has an
excise license. It was essential, therefore, for the plaintiff to plead about
facts concerning a defendant‘s excise license.
310. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC and under
Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act or Section 62(2) of the Copyright
Act. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which
may be presented before the Court having the requisite territorial
jurisdiction, if so advised.
Beiersdorf AG & Anr. v. Duvac Cosmedics & Ors.
[CS(COMM)-1081/2026]

195
311. Neither the plaintiffs nor the defendants are located within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Court. Plaintiff no. 1 is Beiersdorf AG, a
German company, plaintiff no. 2 is Nivea India Pvt. Ltd., having its
registered office at Mumbai. Defendant no. 1 is Duvac Cosmedics,
defendant no. 2 is Glexon Healthcare Pvt. Ltd., and defendant no. 3 is M/s
Jahnavi Medical Store, all of them are located at Gujarat. The suit is
instituted, primarily, for trademark infringement and passing off of the
plaintiff‘s registered ―EUCERIN‖ trademark, used in relation to skincare and
dermatological products, including face washes. It is alleged that the
defendants are manufacturing, marketing, distributing and selling skincare
products bearing the identical mark ―EUCERIN‖ without authorisation.
312. Para. 77 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―77. This Hon‘ble Court has jurisdiction to try and entertain the present suit
due to the following reasons:
Under Section 20 (c) of the Code of Civil Procedure 1908:
(a) The infringing products of the Defendants who are inextricably
interrelated are being made available within the jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court. Therefore, the Defendants have purposefully availed of the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and are specifically targeting customers
based in Delhi.
(b) Furthermore, since the Defendants have supplied the infringing
products in Delhi thereby exists a reasonable and credible apprehension
that the Defendants will expand its commercial operations within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.‖
313. In Banyan Tree, Mr. Anand had argued before the Division Bench
that purposeful availment must be replaced with purposeful avoidance i.e.,
merely because a defendant has not avoided a jurisdiction would show that it

196
has availed the jurisdiction of a given Court. This argument was, however,
rejected by the Division Bench. The requirement of Banyan Tree i.e., of
purposefully availing the jurisdiction of a forum Court by specifically
targeting customers at Delhi as also commercially concluding transactions
have not been proven. The ground pleaded in para. 77(a), at least in the
context of online availability, thus deserves to be rejected.
314. The second ground taken by the plaintiff to invoke jurisdiction under
Section 20(c) is the findings of an investigator engaged by the plaintiff. It is
pleaded that the investigator visited the premises of defendant no. 1 at
Vadodara, Gujarat and found that a multi-storey building, located in a
commercial area, was erected. Upon inquiring from individuals at that place
in Vadodara, Gujarat, it was found that business of the impugned product
was being conducted. At the premises of defendant no. 3 at Vadodara,
Gujarat, the investigator is stated to have purchased three units of the
impugned product. It was discovered by the investigator that defendant no. 3
had been selling the impugned product approximately from the past one to
one-and-a-half years. A visit is also stated to have been made at defendant
no. 2‘s premises at Bakrol, Gujarat, where it was found that the impugned
product is manufactured at that place for defendant no. 1, who is at
Vadodara, Gujarat. One Prisha Pharmacy, again located at Vadodara,
Gujarat is also stated to have sold the impugned product to the investigator
of the plaintiff.
315. It is the pleadings made from para. 58 to 60 of the plaint that bring
Delhi into the picture. It is contended that on 30.07.2026, the investigator
contacted defendant no. 3 to purchase the impugned products and get them

197
delivered at Delhi. It is stated that defendant no. 3 dispatched the products to
Delhi on a cash-on-delivery basis to the investigator in Delhi. On
03.08.2026, the products are stated to have been received at the Delhi
address of the investigator and payments are claimed to have been made. It
is claimed that no invoice or rough bill was raised.
316. To support these pleadings, the plaintiff has filed the affidavit of the
purported investigator one Mr. Nripendra Kashyap. This investigator, as the
affidavit records, works with an investigative agency called ―E-Cluewise‖
located at Noida. However, despite the ordinary residence, it appears that the
investigator while making the affidavit was at Delhi. The averment made in
the affidavit is ―presently in New Delhi‖.
317. The affidavit makes mention of certain ―associates‖ that helped Mr.
Kashyap during the course of the investigation. They are unnamed and not
identified. To support the pleadings made concerning the activities at
Gujarat, the following has been stated in the affidavit:
9. On 13th July 2026, my associate visited the premises of Defendant No. 1
situated at B/403, Samanvay Silicon, Opp. Kalyan Hotel, Sayajigunj,
Vadodara. My associate noticed that the said address was a multi-storey
building, located in a commercial area and no signage of the subject entity
was displayed around the premises. It was occupying approximately 40-50
square yards of floor area and around 3 people were seen working inside the
premises.
10. During the course of the visit, my associate met one Mr. Vivek Vasani,
Territory Manager of Defendant No. 1. Mr. Vasani stated that Defendant
No. 1 is engaged in the business of skincare products, including facewash
and sunscreen products, and stated that the entity markets products under
various brands including EUCERIN. He further stated that the said entity is
a partnership firm which is owned and managed by Mr. Kamlesh Joshi, Ms.
Alkaben Kamlesh Joshi, and Mr. Bhavinkumar Yogeshkumar Joshi.

198
11. Mr. Vasani informed my associate that Defendant No. 1 has been selling
facewash products under the EUCERIN mark for approximately one to one-and-a-half years. Upon being questioned regarding the adoption of the mark,
Mr. Vasani stated that the decision had been taken by the partners of the
firm and that he was not aware of the reason behind such adoption.
12. Mr. Vasani further informed my associate that the present premises is
their office, and they do not have any other office located anywhere else.
13. He further added that they get their products manufactured by a few
third-party skincare manufacturing companies, however he did not disclose
the details of any third-party manufacturer to the investigator.
14. Mr. Vasani further informed my associate that Defendant No. 1 does not
directly sell its products to end consumers and instead supplies its products
