through dealers. In this regard, he directed my associate to M/s Jahnavi
Medical Store, Defendant No. 3 herein, for the purchase of products sold
under the impugned mark.
****
19. Pursuant to the information furnished by the representative of Defendant
No. 1, my associate visited the premises of Defendant No. 3, M/s Jahnavi
Medical Store, at the address SF 216, Siddharth Crest, Opposite Nizampura
Bus Stand, Vadodara – 390024, Gujarat on 13th July 2026.
20. My associate met with a staff member working at the said premises who
informed him that they are engaged in the business of dealing in medical
and cosmetic products.
21. At the said premises, my associate purchased three units of the
impugned EUCERIN product against Invoice No. CA-BS/6069 dated 13th
July 2026 for a total amount of INR 891/- paid in cash. The purchased
products prominently bore the trademark EUCERIN and specifically
identified Defendant No. 1 as the marketer and Defendant No. 2 as the
manufacturer. Furthermore, the details on the packaging reflect further
details on Defendant No.1, including its phone number being 9825096755.
22. Upon enquiries, the staff member at Defendant No. 3‘s premises stated
that they had been selling impugned EUCERIN branded products for
approximately one to one-and-a-half years. My associate further observed
that around 7-8 units of the infringing products were stocked and offered for
sale at the premises. Defendant No. 3 also supplied to the investigator a
brochure of Defendant No. 1 wherein the impugned mark EUCERIN was
prominently used as a brand name for facewash products.
****

199
24. On 17th July 2026, my associate visited the premises of Defendant No.
2 situated at Shyam Industrial Hub, Bakrol, Gujarat. During the visit, my
associate met Mr. Ritesh Chauhan, a representative of Defendant No. 2, who
confirmed that Defendant No. 2 is engaged in the manufacture of cosmetic
and skincare products for third parties.
25. Mr. Chauhan stated that Defendant No. 2 manufactures skincare
products for Defendant No. 1 under the brand names EUCERIN and
DERMCAL.‖
318. Specifically to support the pleadings made in paras. 58 to 60 of the
plaint, which provide the Delhi connection, the affidavit has stated the
following:
―34. During the course of the conversation, I and/or my associate
expressed interest in purchasing products under the EUCERIN brand and
enquired whether the same could be supplied to a Delhi address. Mr. Joshi
readily agreed to dispatch the products and thereafter the investigator shared
his requirement and delivery address with him on WhatsApp. Thereafter, the
infringing products were dispatched by the Defendants on a cash-on-delivery basis to me and/or my associate in Delhi.
35. Thereafter, on 3rd August 2026, I and/or my associate received four
units of EUCERIN Foaming Face Wash at his Delhi address and made
payment of INR 1,188/- through a QR code provided by Mr. Bhavinkumar
Joshi over WhatsApp. However, no invoice or rough bill was raised against
the said purchase.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
319. The verification appended to the affidavit reads as under:
―VERITICATION:
Verified at New Delhi on this the __ day of September, 2026 that the
contents of my above affidavit are true to my knowledge and nothing
material or relevant has been concealed therefrom.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
320. Order XIX of the CPC deals with ‗Affidavits‘. Rule 3 deals with
matters to which an affidavit shall be confined and reads as under:

200
―3. Matters to which affidavits shall be confined.
(1) Affidavits shall be confined to such facts as the deponent is able of his
own knowledge to prove, except on interlocutory applications, on which
statements of his belief may be admitted: provided that the grounds thereof
are stated.
(2) The costs of every affidavit which shall unnecessarily set forth matters
of hearsay or argumentative matter, or copies of or extracts from documents,
shall (unless the Court otherwise directs) be paid by the party filing the
same.‖
321. Further, the Delhi High Court (Original Side) Rules, 2018 under
Chapter XIX deals with ‗Affidavits‘. While sub-rule 4 deals with the form of
the affidavit, sub-rule 5 deal with the contents of the affidavit, they read as
under:
―4. Form.
Every affidavit shall be drawn up in the first person, and shall be divided
into paragraphs to be numbered consecutively, and shall state the
description, occupation if any, and the true place of abode of the deponent.
5. Contents of affidavit.
Affidavits shall be confined to such facts as the deponent is able, of his own
knowledge to prove, except on interlocutory applications, on which
statements of his belief may be admitted, provided that the grounds thereof
are stated.‖
322. A Constitution Bench in A.K.K. Nambiar v. Union of India,
176 noted
the importance of a verification in an affidavit. It was opined by the
Supreme Court that a verification tests the genuineness and authenticity of
the allegations and also makes the deponent responsible for the allegations.
A genuine verification, most importantly, enables the Court to find out
whether it will be safe to act on such an affidavit. Para. 8 of the said decision
is extracted as under:

176 (1969) 3 SCC 864.

201
―8. …The reasons for verification of affidavits are to enable the Court to
find out which facts can be said to be proved on the affidavit evidence of
rival parties. Allegations may be true to knowledge or allegations may be
true to information received from persons or allegations may be based on
records. The importance of verification is to test the genuineness and
authenticity of allegations and also to make the deponent responsible for
allegations. In essence verification is required to enable the Court to find out
as to whether it will be safe to act on such affidavit evidence. In the present
case, the affidavits of all the parties suffer from the mischief of lack of
proper verification with the result that the affidavits should not be
admissible in evidence.‖
(Emphasis supplied)
323. A perusal of the affidavit would reveal that the visit made to Gujarat,
and the investigation undertaken there, was not made by the deponent of the
affidavit. Rather it was an associate of Mr. Kashyap who had travelled to
Gujarat. This associate is unnamed and unknown. The statements made in
the affidavit are stated to be true to the knowledge of the deponent. Unless
the deponent can be at two places at once, or can see events taking place
hundreds of kilometres away, it seems impossible that Mr. Kashyap could
have personal knowledge about the events which took place at Gujarat.
324. What is even more concerning is the use of the expression ―I and/or
my associate‖ at para. 34 and 35 of the affidavit which are intended to
support the pleadings which state that the delivery of the impugned product
took place at Delhi. ―I and/or my associate‖ is not a fact. Only facts can be
stated in an affidavit. This expression as found in the affidavit has no
meaning or weight in law. It has to be discarded.
325. The affidavit and verification are, thus, found to be non-compliant
with the CPC, the Original Side Rules of this Court, and the decision of the
Supreme Court in A.K.K. Nambiar. Resultantly, both are discarded in toto.

202
326. The plaintiff has, along with the plaint, tendered pictures showing a
parcel purportedly from ―Duvac Cosmedic Vadodara‖ being sent to ―RK
Verma c/o DC Sharma, 29, 1st Floor, Masjid Road, Bhogal, New Delhi -
110014‖. Who this ―RK Verma‖ is, to whom is he related, what is the
association with the plaintiff, appears to be unknown. There is no pleading
regarding ―RK Verma‖ in the entire plaint. The plaint had narrated that the
investigator had contacted the defendants through WhatsApp and received
the goods. Para. 28 of the plaint notes that the affidavit of the investigator is
tendered with the plaint. This affidavit is of Mr. Kashyap not Mr. Verma.
327. Is Mr. Verma the associate of Mr. Kashyap? The plaint has no
answers. The affidavit of the investigator mentions, at variance with the
plaint, that an associate had engaged with the defendants and gotten the
delivery of the impugned product at Delhi. That affidavit, as has been noted
above, is being discarded. Effectively, therefore, the pictures are without any
supporting pleadings and cannot, at all, be looked at by this Court.
328. Arguably, the defect highlighted above may be a curable defect. Even
if cured, as the discussion below shall reveal, jurisdiction cannot be made
out at Delhi.
329. Notwithstanding this, even if the WhatsApp chats are looked at, they
demonstrate that Mr. Verma had sought the price of the impugned products
inclusive of courier charges. The courier/delivery partner, thus, was an agent
of the plaintiff. As noted in Glen and para. 305 of this judgment, by
application of Section 39 of the Sale of Goods Act, the delivery upon the
delivery agent at Gujarat shall be deemed to be delivery upon the buyer. The
transaction, thus, concluded at Gujarat only.

203
330. Even otherwise, such trap transactions, even in the offline world, as
noted at paras. 208 and 216 of this judgement, have been prohibited by the
decision in Banyan Tree. No reliance can, therefore, be placed upon such
transactions to concoct jurisdiction before Delhi Courts. The requirements of
Section 20(c) of the CPC are, thus, not satisfied.
331. Assuming that an investigator, in law, has some role to play, he
cannot, under the guise of purported investigation, be permitted to
selectively create jurisdiction, and compel a court to entertain a suit which
otherwise lies within the ―local limits‖ of another court, which is forum
conveniens. If at all such an investigator was to get from a distributor,
retailer or stockist from Delhi, impugned products, which ex facie were sold
in Delhi, the situation perhaps may have been different.
332. In the instant case, the plaintiff no. 1 is at Germany, plaintiff no. 2 is
at Mumbai, all three of the defendants are at Gujarat. The defendants at
Gujarat include the manufacturer, distributor as also the retailer. The
material, essential and integral part of the cause of action, has, therefore,
arisen outside the jurisdiction of this Court. Merely because an investigator
hired by the plaintiff selectively purchased the impugned product at Delhi
the jurisdiction of this Court is invoked. If this exercise is allowed, in law,
the plaintiffs will be capable of filing a suit in any nook and corner of the
country through engaging an investigator who gets the product delivered at
that place. Such a recourse cannot be countenanced in law. Even if it is
assumed that the delivery did constitute a part of the cause of action, this
minuscule part of cause of action, which is capable of being generated by
the plaintiff in any part of the country, ought not to be the reason to

204
entertain a suit in Delhi. This, thus, would be a fit case to refuse to exercise
jurisdiction on grounds of forum non conveniens.
333. Cumulatively, no cause of action can be said to have arisen within the
jurisdiction of this Court under Section 20(c) of CPC. Even if it is assumed
that a part cause of action has accrued within Delhi, on grounds of forum
conveniens, the suit ought not to be entertained. Accordingly, the plaint
deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the
Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Amber Nutrition Private Limited v. Mr. Manish Chandak & Anr.
[CS(COMM)-996/2026]
334. None of the parties are at Delhi. The plaintiff is at Madhya Pradesh,
defendant no. 1 and 2 are at Rajasthan. The plaintiff‘s suit in this case is for,
inter alia, trademark and copyright infringement along with a claim for
passing off. Reliefs are sought for permanent and mandatory injunctions,
damages, delivery up, rendition of accounts. The plaintiff claims to be the
owner of the registered trademark ―AMBER KREAM TOFFEE‖. It is averred
that defendant no. 1 has been marketing and selling ―PANKAJ CREAM
TOFFEE‖ using deceptively similar packaging and artwork. Defendant no.
2, Mr. Jai Kishan Boob trading as M/s Poona Sweet Products, is stated to
have participated in the distribution of the impugned product.
335. Paras. 88 to 92 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―88. That, this Hon‘ble Court has territorial jurisdiction to entertain, try and
adjudicate upon the present suit on the following, amongst other, grounds:

205
89. That, pursuant to an order placed with Defendant No. 2, the Impugned
Product/infringing product were, in fact, delivered at Delhi, within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court, as is evident from the Kacchi
Invoice and delivery receipt filed herewith, which shows that Defendants‘
infringing products are available in Delhi. Accordingly, a substantial and
material part of the cause of action qua the present suit has arisen within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. It is further submitted that
Defendants‘ goods are also available at various retail and wholesale shops in
Delhi specifically by various confectionary wholesalers and retailers.
90. The Defendant No. 1, despite being fully conscious of the infringing
nature of the Impugned Product, has deliberately and prominently displayed
the Impugned Product, bearing artwork that is deceptively similar to, and
infringing of, the Plaintiffs Trademarks and Copyrights, on its official
website, namely https://gdigitalindia.in/sites/Pankaj-Confectionary, with the
express object of promoting and soliciting the sale thereof. The said display
continues to remain available and accessible to the public in Delhi on
Defendant No. 1‘s interactive website even as on the date of institution of
the present suit. The aforesaid conduct unequivocally demonstrates the
wilful, deliberate and brazen disregard, despite service and receipt of Cease
and Desist Notice, of Defendant No. 1 for the Plaintiff‘s statutory and
common law rights, and establishes that the infringement is neither innocent
nor inadvertent, but is calculated and contumacious in nature.
91. The Impugned Product is, in addition, presently listed, displayed,
advertised and offered for sale on multiple major e-commerce platforms and
online business directories which are widely accessible and operational
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court, including but not
limited to Google.com, Justdial.com, Indiamart.com and Tradeindia.com.
These listings directly target, and are designed to solicit orders from,
merchants, retailers, wholesalers and consumers located in Delhi and the
National Capital Region. Through each such listing, the Impugned Product
bearing the Plaintiff‘s infringing artwork is presented to Delhi-based users
as available for bulk purchase, distribution and retail supply, thereby
multiplying the reach, visibility and commercial exploitation of the
infringing gel-up within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
The presence of the Impugned Product on these high-traffic platforms
accessible to Delhi consumers constitutes a continuation and amplification
of Defendant No. l‘s calculated infringement, and occasions direct and
measurable injury to the Plaintiff‘s Trademarks, Copyrights, goodwill and
commercial interests within this jurisdiction. The aforesaid conduct
substantially reinforces that Defendant No. 1 has not merely made the
infringing Impugned Product available, but has actively targeted and
solicited the Delhi consumer market, thereby rendering this Hon‘ble Court
the appropriate forum for adjudication of the Plaintiff‘s claims.

206
92. That, the Plaintiffs goods are also sold and marketed within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court at Delhi, and that the Plaintiff
has substantial goodwill and reputation amongst the consumers situated
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court, which goodwill and
reputation is being damaged by the Defendants‘ infringing activities, and
accordingly, the Plaintiff is suffering injury within the territorial jurisdiction
of this Hon‘ble Court.‖
336. The plaintiff has its registered office at Indore, Madhya Pradesh. Both
the defendants have their offices at Jodhpur, Rajasthan. Jurisdiction of this
Court is sought to be invoked on grounds that the website of the defendant
and third parties, purportedly having the impugned product, is accessible in
Delhi. This falls foul of the Banyan Tree standard. There is no indicia of the
defendant purposefully availing itself of the jurisdiction of this Court by
specifically targeting customers within Delhi. Nor are there commercially
concluded transactions between the defendant and persons within the forum
court‘s jurisdiction through the website in question. Jurisdiction, therefore,
on the basis of websites cannot be claimed.
337. Next, it is pleaded that the plaintiff‘s goods are sold and marketed
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. Though not pleaded, perhaps
the plaintiff wishes to invoke the provisions of the specialised statutes under
Sections 62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright and Trade Marks Act,
respectively. At the outset it may be noted that in order to entertain a plaint,
a Court must have jurisdiction qua each of the prayers made in the suit. In
the instant case, since there is also a prayer for passing off, reference cannot
be made to the specialised provisions for this given prayer. Even otherwise,
as held in Dhodha House, paras. 47 and 51, the plaintiff cannot be claimed

207
to be ―carrying on business‖ in Delhi for the purposes of the specialised
statute merely because its goods are sold in Delhi.
338. The plaintiff has also claimed that the ―defendants goods are
available at various retail and wholesale shops in Delhi specifically by
various confectionary wholesalers and retailers‖. Not a single such
reference has been given in the plaint, and no document at all, has been
annexed with the plaint to make good this pleading. Upon being inquired
about the basis of this pleading, the plaintiff roars ‗demurrer doctrine‘.
339. This is a classic case of clever drafting where jurisdiction is sought to
be pleaded by hoodwinking the court and abusing the doctrine of demurrer.
The plaintiff has asked this Court to be a mute spectator and look only at the
averments without asking more. This is a fit case where the pleading can be
considered to be clever drafting, engaged in, only for the purposes of
concocting territorial jurisdiction. As held by the Supreme Court in
Manjula, the Court needs to pierce through such averments. Thus, on this
ground as well, jurisdiction cannot be invoked.
340. Lastly, it has also been claimed that an order was placed with
defendant no. 2, and the impugned product was delivered at Delhi. In para.
50 of the plaint, the plaintiff has made his averments concerning this
transaction with defendant no. 2; the paragraph reads:
―50. That, upon further investigation, and to narrow down upon the further
infringing channels of Defendant No. 1, Plaintiffs representatives came
across further infringers and got to know that Defendant No. 2 has also been
selling well. Thereafter , the Plaintiff, through its representative , placed an
order for the Impugned Product through WhatsApp of the mobile number of
the Defendant No. 2 obtained by Plaintiff through its dealer network.
Pursuant thereto , the Impugned Product were duly delivered the infringing

208
product as to the Plaintiffs representative in Delhi along with the Kacchi
Invoice bearing particulars and details of the Impugned Product….‖
341. The plaintiff has also attached pictures of a carton containing the
impugned products, a parcel having an A4 sheet cello-taped over it, bearing
the following text typed out:
―To,
B-222, 3rd floor, Layak Ram complex Badarpur
South Delhi – 110044
Near deepa medicos.
Contact – 9560930499
Bhanu Gupta
From = SURAJ BHAI JODHPUR
889092758‖
There is another ―Kacchi Invoice‖ appended with the plaint and also a
delivery invoice showing the consignor to be one Mr. Suraj Bhai and the
consignee to be Bhanu Gupta, Delhi.
342. For quite a few reasons, this narration as well would not confer
jurisdiction on this Court. First, the Court is truly unsure, even on a prima
facie basis, who ‗Suraj Bhai Jodhpur‘ is. The memo of parties shows
defendant no. 2 to be Mr. Jai Kishan Boob trading as M/s Poona Sweet
Products. The documents, however, show that goods were sent by one Suraj
Bhai Jodhpur. There is no pleading that connects Suraj Bhai with Jai Kishan
Boob. In the absence of it, the averments seem to be foundationally hollow.
Second, the plaintiff cannot set up an individual at Delhi, who enters into a
transaction with the defendant, and gets goods purportedly delivered at
Delhi, and then claim jurisdiction of this Court. Trap transactions of this
nature are prohibited by Banyan Tree. As analysed in this judgement, the
dictum of Banyan Tree on trap sales was not restricted to online

209
transactions. Gaming of jurisdictional rules through setting up individuals
simply cannot be tolerated. Thus, on this ground as well, jurisdiction cannot
be invoked.
343. Third, the ―Kacchi invoice‖ demonstrates that the courier charges
were included in the cost of goods. Prima facie, therefore, the delivery
charges appear to have been paid by the plaintiff. This being the case, the
delivery partner/courier shall, as was held in Glen, be deemed to be the
agent of the plaintiff. As per Section 39 of the Sale of Goods Act, 1930, the
procurement of the impugned goods at Rajasthan, by the agent, is deemed
delivery upon the plaintiff. The plaintiff is at Madhya Pradesh. No delivery
thus, takes place, at Delhi.
344. Even if the trap transaction in question was considered to be a genuine
sale for reasons stated in the analysis for CS(COMM) 1081/2026, this suit
ought not to be entertained on grounds of forum conveniens.
345. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC. The plaintiff‘s
sale of goods in Delhi also does not establish jurisdiction under Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act or Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act. Even
if it is assumed that a part cause of action has accrued within Delhi, on
grounds of forum conveniens, the suit ought not to be entertained.
Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may
be presented before the Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if
so advised.
Amritsar Haveli Cuisines Private Ltd. and Anr. v. Amarbir Singh and Ors.

210
[CS(COMM)-611/2026]
Haveli Restaurant and Resorts Ltd. v. Shubham Food Enterprises
[CS(COMM)-980/2026]
346. The suit in CS(COMM)-611/2026 has been instituted seeking,
primarily, reliefs of permanent injunction and damages for the alleged
infringement and passing off of their registered trademarks ―AMRITSAR
HAVELI‖/―AMRITSARI HAVELI‖, used in relation to restaurants, food and
hospitality services. Plaintiff no. 1 is a company having its registered office
at Amritsar, Punjab, and branch outlet at Delhi. Plaintiff no. 2 is claimed to
be the founder and managing director of plaintiff no. 1, and as per the memo
of parties, also resides at Amritsar. Defendant nos. 1 to 4 are partners of the
firm ‗Café Green Brew‘ which was constituted on 05.02.2026. The said firm
is stated to be engaged in the restaurant business and is using the trademarks
―The Amritsar Haveli‖ and ―Amritsari Haveli‖ in the course of its trade and
business.
347. Paras. 23 and 24 of the plaint reveal that previously, defendant no. 1
along with plaintiff no. 2 had formed a partnership firm under the name and
style of M/s Café Green on 24.06.2013. During the subsistence of the
partnership, plaintiff no. 2 is stated to have coined the term ‗Amritsar
Haveli/Amritsari Haveli‘, and procured trademarks for the said term. It is
clarified in the plaint that the said mark was not made part of the
partnership. Cause of action, primarily, is pleaded to have arisen owing to
the defendant applying for registration of trademarks which are deceptively
similar to the plaintiff‘s mark.

211
348. Paras. 60 and 61 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―60. This Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to try and adjudicate
the instant suit under Section 134(2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999 and
Section 62 of Copyright Act, 1957 as the Plaintiff has a branch
office/restaurant/outlet in Delhi. It is also submitted that the Plaintiff is a
copyright owner of the label whereof is part of the cause of action of the
present suit. Therefore, this Hon‘ble Court has jurisdiction to adjudicate the
present suit under section 134(2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999, Section 62
of Copyright Act, 1957.
61. That this Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to try and
adjudicate the present suit as the Plaintiffs carry their business within
jurisdiction of this Court and Defendants are soliciting and networking,
through their website and social media handles all of which are accessible
within territorial limits of this Hon‘ble Court, for giving franchises and
opening restaurants under the impugned trademark/trade name in Delhi and
have all intention to sell, offer for sale, their impugned services under the
impugned trademark within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
Defendants are committing act of infringement of Plaintiffs‘ said trade mark
and passing-off within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
Defendant infringing activities are likely to have a dynamic effect on the
Plaintiff‘s business, both current and upcoming, within the jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court. It is evident that the dynamic effect of the defendant‘s
activities is being felt in Delhi and there exists an undeniable nexus between
the cause of action in the present suit and the territory of Delhi. The
Plaintiffs‘ above said proprietary rights are being prejudicially affected or
likely to be so affected in Delhi due to the defendant‘s impugned activities.
Plaintiffs thereafter made inquiry in the market and trade which revealed
that the Defendants are soliciting trade and business in Delhi with the
proposal/promise of extending franchise of the impugned trademarks. The
carrying on of business by the Defendant as well as whole or part of cause
of action for filing the suit has arisen within the territorial jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court within the meaning of Section 20 of Code of Civil Procedure,
1908. Further the Plaintiffs are working for gain and also carrying on its
business & services under its said trade mark in Delhi which is being
tarnished or likely to be tarnished within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble
Court by the impugned activities of Defendant. The Defendant website,
social media website and promotions are targeting consumers in Delhi.
Therefore, this Hon‘ble court has jurisdiction to try and entertain the present
suit.‖

212
349. The suit in CS(COMM)-980/2026 is instituted by Haveli Restaurant
and Resorts Ltd., against the defendant-Shubham Food Enterprises. It is
contended that the defendant is running a restaurant called ―Dharampur
Haveli‖ at Himachal Pradesh. In doing so, submits the plaintiff, the
defendant has adopted the impugned mark together with deceptively similar
branding, architecture, trade dress, menu cards, creating a false association
with the plaintiff‘s establishments. It is contended that the defendant‘s
business at Dharampur, District Solan, Himachal Pradesh, infringes the
plaintiff‘s trademark and copyright. Owing to customers at Delhi
purportedly identifying the defendant‘s establishment as that of the plaintiff,
a claim for passing off is also made. Reliefs for, inter alia, permanent
injunction and damages have been sought.
350. Paras. 104 to 108 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―104. That this Hon‘ble Court has the necessary territorial jurisdiction to
entertain and adjudicate the present suit, by virtue of the Plaintiff‘s carrying
on business within the territorial limits of this Hon‘ble Court and by reason
of the Defendant‘s infringing activities having a direct, substantial, and
continuing impact within this jurisdiction. The present suit has been
instituted under Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act, 1999, to be read
with Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act, 1957. Section 134(2) of the Trade
Marks Act to be read with Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act, 1957, confers
jurisdiction on a Court within the local limits of whose jurisdiction, at the
time of the institution of the suit, the Plaintiff actually and voluntarily
resides or carries on business or personally works for gain. The Plaintiff
herein carries on business within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble
Court. The Plaintiff is carrying on business through its office located within
the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
105. It is further submitted that the Plaintiff is also advertising their services
on the interactive third-party website www.tripadvisor.com, which is
accessible within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and allows any
consumer across India, including within NCT of Delhi, to view content, post

213
and read reviews, make enquiries, interact with the business, and form
consumer perceptions based on the representations made by the Plaintiff.
Therefore, by operation of law, the Plaintiff is entitled to institute the
present action for Trade Mark infringement and passing off before this
Hon‘ble Court. The Defendant is engaged in the business of rendering
hospitality and restaurant services under the impugned mark/label/trade
name ―Dharampur Haveli‖ and is actively marketing, promoting, soliciting,
and advertising their infringing services through various online platforms
and social media, which are interactive in nature and accessible within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The relevant digital platforms being used
by the Defendant include:
Domain Name: https://havelidharampur.com/
Instagram: www.instagram.com/havelidharampur/
Facebook:
www.facebook.com/people/HaveliDharampur/61579838786917/#
106. These platforms are not merely passive but allow consumers across
India, including within NCT Delhi, to view content, post and read reviews,
make enquiries, interact with the business, and form consumer perceptions
based on the representations made by the Defendant. The interactive nature
of such platforms brings the infringing activity directly within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
107. Furthermore, the Defendant is promoting, advertising, offering for sale,
and soliciting bookings in respect of its impugned services under the
impugned mark on the interactive e-commerce platform, i.e.,
www.tripadvisor.com. The said interactive website is accessible to
consumers within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court, and
enables prospective customers, including those within the jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court, to access information regarding the Defendant‘s services,
make enquiries, and avail bookings.
108. Accordingly, the Defendant is carrying on commercial activities and
purposefully targeting consumers within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble
Court. The presence and accessibility of infringing material online,
combined with its commercial intent and interactive features, are sufficient
to confer jurisdiction in the forum where such material is viewed and has a
deleterious impact, which also gives rise to a part of the cause of action in
the present suit. The infringing activities of the Defendant are not
geographically limited but are borderless and accessible from anywhere,
including New Delhi, in view of the online activities being conducted
through various online platforms. The Defendant‘s unauthorized adoption
and use of a deceptively similar trade name, label, trade dress, and brand
identity, identical setup and ambience coupled with their promotional

214
activities targeting consumers across India through digital means, has
resulted in actual deception and confusion amongst the public, particularly
in the minds of consumers who are familiar with the Plaintiff‘s well-known
brand ―HAVELI‖, which enjoys considerable goodwill and reputation in
and around the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The Plaintiff has
legitimate apprehension that consumers, traders, and members of the public
located within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court are being misled into
believing an association between the Plaintiff and the Defendant owing to
the latter‘s unauthorized use of the impugned mark/label/trade name.
Accordingly, in view of the Plaintiff‘s place of business being located
within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court, the accessibility of infringing
material within this jurisdiction, and the continuing nature of the harm
caused to the Plaintiff‘s brand within the territorial limits of this Court, this
Hon‘ble Court has the necessary jurisdiction to entertain and try the present
suit.‖
351. The plaintiffs in both of the aforenoted suits, have their registered
office at Punjab. As per the decision of the Supreme Court in Sanjay Dalia,
specifically the observations in para. 16, the registered office shall be treated
to be the principal place of business. Applying the dictum of Ultra Home,
the plaintiffs can be considered to be ―carrying on business‖ at Delhi, only if
cause of action also accrues at Delhi. In this connection, firstly, it may be
iterated that similar to the decision in Impressario, mere viewing of content,
posting/reading of reviews, making of enquiries, or even making of
bookings will not confer jurisdiction as the transactions get concluded after
visiting and dining at the restaurant/café, which lies outside the jurisdiction
of this Court.
352. Secondly, the social media handles and websites of the defendant, or
listings on third party websites, do not satisfy the Banyan Tree standard.
Commercially concluded transactions between the defendant and persons
within the forum court‘s jurisdiction through the website in question have
not been shown, nor is there any indicia of the defendants in the said cases,

215
purposefully availing the jurisdiction of Delhi by specifically targeting
customers here. Instagram or Facebook data indicating that Delhi users are
the most active is immaterial to the defendant‘s objective intention to target
customers at Delhi. A statistic showing most traffic from Delhi may be a
result of population disparity or a wide variety of other factors. Traffic, in
and of itself, does not indicate targeting of a given forum. Cause of action,
thus, cannot be said to have accrued at Delhi. Resultantly, neither under
Section 20(c) of the CPC nor under the specialised statutes, jurisdiction can
be established.
353. Separate from jurisdictional grounds concerning the online activity of
the defendants, there is another claim made by the plaintiff that the
defendants are purportedly soliciting trade and business through proposals of
franchise, which if accepted by the public in Delhi, may infringe the
plaintiff‘s rights. This is yet again a desperate and unsuccessful attempt at
cleverly pleading jurisdiction; on the one hand, this is a bald averment
without any supporting document and attracts the ruling in Manjula, on the
other hand, it is inadequate to satisfy the requirements of a quia timet action
as noted at para. 140 of this judgement.
354. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC. The plaintiff‘s
sale of goods in Delhi also does not establish jurisdiction under Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act or Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act.
Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may
be presented before the Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if
so advised.

216
Namastu Enterprise Pvt. Ltd. v. Kajalben Maheshbhai Kaklotar and Anr.
[CS(COMM)-1010/2026]
355. Neither the plaintiff nor the defendants are in Delhi. While the
plaintiff has its registered office at West Bengal, the defendants are at
Gujarat and Karnataka. The suit is for trademark and copyright
infringement, copying of trade dress, and passing off. The plaintiff claims to
be the registered proprietor of the mark ―PHITKU‖ and alleges that
defendant no. 1 is offering personal-care products under the deceptively
similar marks ―PITTCU‖ and ―PHITKARI‖ through the Amazon platform
operated by defendant no. 2.
356. Para. 50 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―50. This Hon‘ble Court has territorial jurisdiction to entertain and try the
present suit under Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act, 1999 and Section
62(2) of the Copyright Act, 1957, as the Plaintiff carries on business within
the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court through its official website accessible
across Delhi, and Defendant No. 1 offers its infringing products for sale to
consumers located in Delhi via Defendant No. 2‘s platform. This is further
evidenced by a test purchase order placed by the Plaintiff on 12.08.2026 for
the Infringing Product ―PHITKARI‖ sold by Defendant No. 1, which was
delivered on 20.08.2026 at D-377, Basement, Defence Colony, New Delhi –
110024, thereby confirming that Defendant No. 1‘s infringing goods are
offered for sale to, and delivered within, the territorial jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court; therefore, the Commercial Division of this Hon‘ble Court
has the requisite pecuniary jurisdiction as a Commercial Division of this
Hon‘ble High Court.‖
357. Merely because the plaintiff‘s official website is accessible in Delhi
would not amount to the plaintiff carrying on business in Delhi. Reliance on
the decision of this Court in WWE is misplaced. As noted in para. 87 of this
judgement, the decision in WWE no longer governs the field on this issue.

217
358. Mere accessibility of the defendant‘s website also does not confer
jurisdiction; what is required is the purposeful availment of the jurisdiction
of a given Court by specifically targeting customers within that territory.
Additionally, commercially concluded transactions are required. A sole
transaction cannot form the basis for conferring jurisdiction as has been
noted in Banyan Tree.
359. Even otherwise, the sole transaction mentioned by the plaintiff was by
a person set up by the plaintiff. The same is impermissible as was held in
Banyan Tree. Even assuming that the sale in question is treated to be a
genuine order by the plaintiff and a consequent supply by the defendant at
Delhi, a mere delivery or sale in Delhi, an act capable of occurring at any
place the plaintiff desires, ought not to obligate this Court to entertain this
suit. If this is allowed and a slender part of the cause of action makes a court
entertain a suit on the anvil of Section 20(c), specifically a part cause of
action, the entire jurisdictional jurisprudence shall become a nullity. The
plaintiffs will become capable of creating, concocting and establishing
jurisdiction in any part of the country, howsoever remote it may be.
360. At its highest, therefore, the sale may be part of a fraction/slender part
of the cause of action. The material, integral and essential part of the cause
of action has arisen outside the jurisdiction of this Court. This would,
therefore, be a fit case to invoke the doctrine of forum non-conveniens and
refuse to entertain the plaint.
361. Importantly, the Court may also note that the sole transaction was
entered into by the advocate who has filed the instant suit. The address given
for the delivery in the Amazon invoice is the same as the address of the

218
advocate as is found in their Vakalatnama. Rule 13, Section II, Chapter II,
Part VI of the Bar Council of India Rules prohibit an advocate from
accepting a brief in which he has reason to believe that he will be a witness.
The rule reads as under:
―13. An advocate should not accept a brief or appear in a case in which he
has reason to believe that he will be a witness, and if being engaged in a
case, it becomes apparent that he is a witness on a material question of fact,
he should not continue to appear as an Advocate if he can retire without
jeopardising his client‘s interests.‖
362. It is, but obvious, that the advocate would be called as a witness to
prove the transaction entered into. It would be for the good of the Bar if the
advocates do not indulge, at least themselves, in such trap transactions.
363. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section 134(2) of the Trade
Marks Act or Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act. Even if it is assumed that
a part cause of action has accrued within Delhi, on grounds of forum
conveniens, the suit ought not to be entertained. Accordingly, the plaint
deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the
Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
JSW MG Motor India Pvt. Ltd. and Anr. v. Tristar Auto Agencies (Vizag)
Pvt. Ltd.
[CS(COMM)-917/2026]
364. Yet again, none of the parties are at Delhi. Plaintiff no. 1 is at
Gurugram, Haryana, plaintiff no. 2 is at China, and the sole defendant is at
Visakhapatnam. The suit is filed for infringement of trade mark and passing
off. The plaintiffs claim to be the registered proprietors of the ―MG‖ trade-

219
marks. The defendant is stated to be the dealer of plaintiff no. 1 in
Visakhapatnam until its dealership was terminated on 29.11.2024. It is
alleged that despite the determination of the contract, the defendant
continues to use the MG Marks and dealership branding at its showroom,
online listings and in official communications, thereby falsely representing
itself as an authorised MG dealer.
365. Para. 50 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―50. This Hon‘ble Court has the jurisdiction to entertain and try the present
suit as stated below:
a. As per Clause 64.2 of the Dealership Agreement, disputes between the
parties are subject to the exclusive jurisdiction of the Courts of New Delhi.
It is settled law that where a jurisdiction clause employs words such as
―alone‖, ―only‖, ―exclusive‖ or the like, an intention to oust the
jurisdiction of all other competent courts stands squarely established.
Clause 64, operates as a distinct covenant conferring exclusive jurisdiction
upon the Courts at New Delhi over all disputes, including the present suit,
arising out of or in relation to the Dealership Agreement.
b. Plaintiff No. 1 carries on business within the territorial jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court through its network of authorised dealers and
showrooms operating within Delhi. Plaintiff No. 1‘s authorised dealerships
within Delhi include, inter alia, dealerships located at Prashant Vihar,
Karol Bagh, Lajpat Nagar, Dwarka and Safdarjung. Plaintiff No. 1
therefore carries on business within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court
within the meaning of Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act, 1999 and
this Court has jurisdiction to entertain the present suit.
c. The Defendant‘s acts of unauthorised use of the MG Marks, passing off,
and misrepresentation are not confined to Visakhapatnam alone but have a
direct, continuing, and injurious effect on the Plaintiffs‘ goodwill,
reputation, and trade within Delhi, where the Plaintiffs transact substantial
business, where consumers and trade are exposed to and influenced by the
Defendant‘s wrongful conduct, and where the Plaintiffs suffers loss of
reputation, dilution, and tarnishment of its marks. An integral and material
part of the cause of action has therefore arisen within the jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court. The unlawful acts of the Defendant are causing loss

220
and injury to the Plaintiffs within Delhi and within the jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court where a large part of the Plaintiffs‘ business and goodwill
is based which is being severely affected due to the acts of the Defendant,
and thus, this Court has territorial jurisdiction under Section 19 of the
Code of Civil Procedure, 1908.
d. The Defendant‘s activities have a nexus with the territory of this
Hon‘ble Court. The Defendants are tarnishing the Plaintiffs‘ reputation
and diluting the exclusivity associated with Plaintiffs‘ trademark and this
has a dynamic effect on the Plaintiffs‘ reputation under the MG trademark
within the territory of this Hon‘ble Court. Therefore, it is humbly
submitted that an integral and substantial part of the cause of action has
arisen within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and this court has
territorial jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of the Code of Civil Procedure,
1908. In a suit for infringement and passing off, injury or damage suffered
by the Plaintiffs are an essential part of the cause of action. Where the
Plaintiffs carries on business within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court,
any harm caused to its business, goodwill, or reputation by the
Defendant‘s wrongful acts gives rise to a cause of action within this
jurisdiction, regardless of where the infringing act took place.
e. The Defendants‘ online listings on Just Dial and IndiaMart are intended
to generate commercial enquiries and business from members of the public
across India, including consumers within Delhi, thereby causing injury to
the Plaintiffs‘ statutory and common law rights within the jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court and giving rise to a part of the cause of action herein.‖
366. Jurisdiction cannot be conferred on a Court that has none. An
exclusive jurisdiction clause merely curtails jurisdiction to a given place and
does not create it.177 It is, essential therefore, for the plaintiff to establish that
jurisdiction of Delhi is in fact made out.
367. Since the plaintiffs have also made prayers for passing off, reliance on
Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act is impermissible. Notwithstanding
that, as per Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, a place separate from the
plaintiff‘s principal place of business178 can be considered as the place

177 Swastik Gases (P) Ltd. v. Indian Oil Corpn., (2013) 9 SCC 32 and ABC Laminart (P) Ltd. v. AP
Agencies Salem, (1989) 2 SCC 163.
178 Which as per para. 16 of Sanjay Dalia, is the place of registered office of a corporation.

221
where a corporation ―carries on business‖ only when cause of action also
accrues at that place. Mere sale of goods, also, does not constitute carrying
on of business.179
368. The defendant‘s website and online listing on third-party websites do
not confer jurisdiction as they fall foul of the Banyan Tree standard. There
is nothing to demonstrate that the defendant purposefully availed itself of the
jurisdiction of a forum Court by specifically targeting customers at Delhi.
Commercially concluded transactions have also not been proven.
369. Reliance has been placed on Section 19 of the CPC to claim that the
wrong has occurred in Delhi, as its effects are being felt in Delhi. The
underlying premise of this argument is that wherever be the wrong
committed, at all such places where the plaintiff has its presence, effects are
being felt which constitute ―wrong‖ under Section 19 of the CPC. If this is
the case, the dictum of this Court in Sameer Dnyandev Wankhede v. Red
Chillies Entertainments Pvt. Ltd.,
180 shall have full force. The Merger Rule
as expounded in the said decision shall apply, and the common place where
the wrong has occurred and the defendant also resides/carries on business,
shall be the only place where a suit could be instituted.
370. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 19 and Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. The exclusive jurisdiction clause does not
independently confer jurisdiction upon this Court. Accordingly, the plaint

179 Dhodha House, para. 47 and 51.
180 2026:DHC:701.

222
deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the
Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
KBB Nuts Pvt. Ltd. v. Mr. Suresh Chand and Ors.
[CS(COMM)-444/2026]
371. The plaintiff has its registered office at Amritsar, Punjab and a sub-ordinate office at Delhi. Each of the defendants is at Rajasthan. The plaintiff
has filed this suit for trade mark infringement and passing off. The plaintiff
claims to be the registered owner of the trade mark ―TULSI‖ for dry fruits
and other food products. The suit alleges that the defendants are selling dry
fruits under the deceptively similar marks ―SHRINATH JI TULSI‖ and
―RAGHUNATHJI TULSHI‖ which infringe the plaintiff‘s IP rights.
372. Para. 27 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―27. It is submitted that the Defendants are selling their impugned
products in Delhi. Thus, a part of cause of action has arisen within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. Furthermore, the Defendants
have filed their impugned trademark applications without any restriction to
any specific territory or other geographical boundaries. Thus, the
impugned trademark applications have been filed by the Defendants with
an all-India effect i.e. the said application has a dynamic effect, operating
throughout the territory of India, including Delhi. Moreover, since the
Plaintiff carries on its business in Delhi, based out of its office in Delhi
itself, the sales by the Defendants of infringing products in Delhi is
causing great harm to the business of the Plaintiff in Delhi. Thus, a part of
the cause of action has arisen in Delhi. Therefore, this Hon‘ble Court has
jurisdiction to try and entertain the present suit under Section 20 (a) and
(c) of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908. Moreover, since the Plaintiff is
carrying on business in Delhi through its office in Delhi, this Hon'ble
Court also has the jurisdiction to try and entertain the present suit under
the provisions of Section 134(2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999.‖

223
373. In the application bearing IA 15483/2026 it has been pleaded by
defendant nos. 3-5 that the registered office of the plaintiff is at Amritsar,
Punjab. Learned counsel for the plaintiff upon being inquired as to the same,
does not deny that the registered office of the plaintiff is at Amritsar, Punjab.
As per the decisions of Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, under Section 134(2)
of the Trade Marks Act, the suit ought to have been brought before the
courts at Amritsar, Punjab, unless it could be shown that cause of action has
also accrued at the place of the sub-ordinate office. Since the plaintiff had
invoked Section 134(2) of the Trade Marks Act in its plaint, it was
incumbent upon it to disclose where its registered office is. A suppression
qua this aspect is material to the lis, and warrants the suit being thrown out
in limine, without hearing the plaintiff on merits.
374. Notwithstanding the same, jurisdiction on merits may be tested. As
noted above, in order to claim the benefit of Section 134(2) the plaintiff
must demonstrate that cause of action (wholly or in part) has also accrued
within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. The plaintiff has pleaded that
the defendant‘s goods are sold in Delhi. There is no document at all to
support this plea. The dictum of Manjula, thus, applies with full force. The
Court is not to be a mute spectator and digest averments made by a claimant
without any basis.
375. The bald statement that the defendants‘ goods are sold in Delhi needs
to be looked through. Additionally, it may also be highlighted that in
Indovax, while rejecting the plaint, the Court had observed that ―the plaintiff
only states that the goods of the defendants are available for sale in Delhi
but this averment is not supported by any documentary evidence‖. Thus,

224
jurisdiction on the basis of the empty statement of the plaintiff that the
defendants‘ goods are sold in Delhi, cannot be established.
376. The filing of a trademark application by the defendant neither gives
rise to a cause of action for a conventional suit, nor for a quia timet action.
Reliance may be placed on Dhodha House and paras. 124, 127 and 129-130
of this judgment. An application may be accepted or rejected. It may be
decided one way or the other. Its mere filing does not give rise to a cause of
action.
377. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be
returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the Court having the
requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Marico Ltd. v. Shree Uday Oil and Food Industries LLP
[CS(COMM)-1115/2026]
378. Neither the plaintiff nor the defendant is from Delhi. While the
plaintiff is from Mumbai, the defendant is from Gujarat. The plaintiff‘s suit
is for trade mark and copyright infringement along with passing off. The
plaintiff claims to be the registered proprietor and prior user of the well-known ―PARACHUTE‖ trademark and the copyright owner of its original
artistic works, specifically its distinctive blue packaging, ―Flag Device‖ and
―Broken Coconut Device‖. The suit alleges that the defendant is infringing
upon the plaintiff‘s statutory and common law rights by manufacturing,
marketing, and selling a competing product named ―UDAY COCORICH

225
COCONUT OIL‖ by means of adopting a deceptively similar trade dress,
deceptively similar bottle and packaging, and substantially copied layout to
ride upon the plaintiff‘s established goodwill.
379. Para. 60 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―60. That this Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to entertain and
try the present suit under Section 20 (c) of the Code of Civil Procedure,
1908 as the infringing goods of the Defendant are available for sale in Delhi
as well as being delivered in Delhi i.e. within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble
Court and therefore part of the cause of action has arisen within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The same is also evident from the fact
that the reviews on the Defendant‘s own website are from customers based
in Delhi. Further, the impugned product is also available for purchase within
the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court through various interactive websites
such as www.indiamart.com and also through the Defendant‘s website
www.udayoilindia.com and for this reason also this Hon‘ble Court has the
territorial jurisdiction to entertain and try the present suit.
That even otherwise the Defendant has agents, stockiest, distributors, etc.
who are responsible for marketing and selling the said impugned product of
the Defendant within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and for this
reason also, this Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to entertain
and try the present suit.
380. Yet again it may be considered that mere accessibility of websites
which list the impugned products either on the defendant‘s website or on
third parties‘ does not give rise to a cause of action under Section 20(c). The
standard of Banyan Tree has not been satisfied. The plaintiff has made a
bald averment without any substantiation that the defendant has ―agents,
stockiest, distributors, etc.‖ at Delhi who are responsible for selling the
impugned products in Delhi. No document has been annexed in support of
this plea.

226
381. The affidavit of the ―investigator‖ annexed with the plaint cannot be
relied upon for more than one reason: first, there is no pleading concerning
the appointment and use of an investigator; second, the verification
appended to the affidavit is faulty insofar as it conjointly avers that facts are
both true to the deponent‘s personal knowledge as also belief; third, the
investigator being a person set up by the plaintiff cannot, at the threshold, be
used to concoct jurisdiction;181 fourth, the delivery appears to have taken
place through an agent, who prima facie is an agent of the buyer. The
delivery as per Glen and Section 39 of the Sale of Goods Act took place at
Gujarat.
382. Even if the defects in the affidavit were to be cured, and the
transaction in question was to be considered as a genuine sale, for similar
reasons noted in the analysis of CS(COMM) 1081/2026 and CS(COMM)
1010/2026 this Court ought not to entertain the suit on grounds of forum
conveniens.
383. In view of the foregoing reasons, no part of the cause of action has
arisen in Delhi so as to confer territorial jurisdiction of this Court under
Section 20(c) of CPC. Even if it is assumed that a part cause of action has
accrued within Delhi, on grounds of forum conveniens, the suit ought not to
be entertained. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be returned to the
plaintiff, which may be presented before the Court having the requisite
territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Zydus Wellness Products Ltd. v. Tushar Chimanlal Chitalia and Ors.

181 See paras. 208-212 and 216 of this judgement. The said portions also note that the dictum of Banyan
Tree being confined to online sales is incorrect and it also applies to offline transactions.

227
[CS(COMM)-1041/2026]
384. The plaintiff and the principal contesting defendants are situated
outside Delhi. However, defendant nos. 4 and 5, IndiaMART Intermesh Ltd.
and Infocom Network Pvt. Ltd., respectively, have addresses in Delhi and
have been impleaded as intermediaries. The suit is filed for trademark and
copyright infringement along with passing off. The plaintiff claims to be the
registered proprietor of the ―Glucon-D‖ family of trademarks and its
associated green packaging/trade dress for glucose-based beverage
premixes.
385. The suit alleges that defendant no. 1 is unlawfully manufacturing and
selling an identical product under the deceptively similar mark ―Glucose-D‖
while copying the plaintiff‘s distinctive packaging, including the ―Instant
Energy‖ tagline, color scheme, and the ―Happy Family‖ motif. Defendant
no. 2 is stated to be the distributor, defendant no. 3 is the domain registrar
for the infringing website (www.glucosed.com, https://www.glucosed.com),
and defendant nos. 4 (IndiaMART) and 5 (TradeIndia) are interactive e-commerce platforms actively promoting and selling the impugned products.
386. Para. 142 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―142. This Hon‘ble Court has the necessary territorial jurisdiction to
entertain and try the present Suit under Section 20 of the Code of Civil
Procedure, 1908 for the following reasons –
a. The products of Defendant Nos. 1 & 2 bearing the impugned marks and
trade dress are being advertised, promoted, offered for sale and made
available to consumers throughout India, including within the territorial
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court without any caveat of ―not available in
Delhi‖, via the website operated by Defendant Nos. 4 & 5;

228
b. The availability and offer for sale of the infringing products within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court constitute infringement of
ZWPL‘s trademark;
c. Pertinently, the impugned products are being sold and are available for
purchase in New Delhi since Plaintiff has obtained delivery of a sample of
the impugned products at D66 A, 1st floor, Chhabra Complex, Munirka,
New Delhi - 110067, within the territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
Courier receipt of the said sample is filed with the Plaint.
d. Defendant No. 3 is the domain registrar of https://glucosed.com/ and
carries out its business within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. Its
website is also accessible within the territorial jurisdiction of this Court. It is
the entity responsible for maintaining and transferring the domain name
services of https://glucosed.com/ which enables Defendant No. 1 to engage
in his infringing activities.‖
387. The mere availability or offer of sale of the impugned good via
websites is not sufficient to constitute cause of action for the purposes of
Section 20(c). The requirements of Banyan Tree have not been satisfied.
Similarly, the purchase of the impugned product by the plaintiff itself within
Delhi is not sufficient to confer jurisdiction. The ―field personnel‖ of the
plaintiff who purchased the product was a person set up by the plaintiff, for
cause of action to be established under Section 20(c), Banyan Tree requires
commercially concluded transactions through the defendant‘s websites by
persons unconnected with the plaintiff. Defendant nos. 3-5 being the pro-forma defendants, their residence/situs shall not determine whether a given
Court has territorial jurisdiction.182
388. Even if the transaction by the ―field personnel‖ is assumed to be a
genuine transaction for reasons noted in the analysis of CS(COMM)

182 Sector Twenty-one Owners Welfare Association v. Air Force Naval Housing Board, 1996 SCC OnLine
Del 42.

229
1081/2026 this Court ought not to entertain this suit on grounds of forum
conveniens.
389. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(c) of CPC. Even if it is assumed that a part
cause of action has accrued within Delhi, on grounds of forum conveniens,
the suit ought not to be entertained. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be
returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the Court having the
requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Laborate Pharmaceuticals India Limited v. M/s JMBS Healthcare
[CS(COMM)-1120/2026]
390. The plaintiff, having its office at Delhi, has filed the present suit for
trademark and copyright infringement along with a claim for passing off.
The plaintiff claims to be the registered proprietor and prior user, since
1996, of the trade mark ―LABOCOF‖ for cough and cold tablets, and the
copyright owner of the yellow-and-white packaging of its export variant.
The suit alleges that the defendant is manufacturing and exporting identical
tablets under the deceptively similar mark ―LAPNACOF‖ suffixed with
―JM‖, in near-identical packaging.
391. Paras. 63 to 64 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―63. The present suit is a commercial suit as described in Section 2(c)(xvii)
of the Commercial Courts Act, 2015, hence this Hon‘ble Court has the
pecuniary and subject matter jurisdiction to try, entertain and adjudicate the
present suit. Additionally, the present suit is liable to be heard and disposed
of by the Commercial Court in view of provisions of Section 6 of the
Commercial Courts Act, 2015. Additionally, this Hon‘ble Court also has the
territorial jurisdiction to try, entertain and adjudicate the present suit under

230
Section 134 of the Trade Marks Act, 1999 and Section 62 of the Copyright
Act, 1957, as the Plaintiff‘s corporate office is situated at Plot K -17,
Second Floor, Udyog Nagar, Industrial Area, Rohtak Road, Nagloi, New
Delhi-110041 and the Plaintiff is carrying on its business in Delhi.
64. The Defendant also maintains an IndiaMart listing and an interactive
website with a ‗Contact Us‘ tab, through which it is promoting and
soliciting its goods and business and targeting the customers in Delhi. In the
said listings, Defendant has also provided its contact numbers, email IDs
and a detailed form on its website through which potential customers can
reach out to it. Furthermore, Defendant is exporting its counterfeit
pharmaceutical products through the logistic channels operating from Delhi,
including the ports and export facilities in Delhi, within the jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court, which is also corroborated from the publicly available
data on the popular platform- Eximpedia. Furthermore, Plaintiff also gained
knowledge that Defendant is also approaching the market sources in Delhi,
being a major export market, to export its impugned counterfeit products.
Hence, there is an imminent and credible apprehension that Defendant is
likely to expand its export activities and dealings with traders in Delhi in
regard to the counterfeit products. Therefore, owing to Defendant‘s acts, the
Plaintiff‘s goodwill and reputation are being diluted in Delhi and there is a
potential loss of business for the Plaintiff in Delhi. Therefore, the cause of
action has partly arisen in Delhi, and this Hon‘ble Court has the territorial
jurisdiction under Section 20(c) of Code of Civil Procedure, 1908.‖
392. The plaintiff has its registered office outside the jurisdiction of this
Court. Resultantly, as per Sanjay Dalia, specifically para. 16, the principal
place of business shall be considered to be the place of registered office.
Resultantly, the option under Sections 62(2) and 134(2) of the Copyright
and Trade Marks Act, respectively, shall be made available at Delhi only if
cause of action h the place of its registered office as also accrued within the
jurisdiction of this Court. To establish cause of action, the plaintiff has
pleaded that the defendant is exporting its counterfeit pharmaceutical
products through the logistic channels operating from Delhi, including the
export facilities at Delhi. To support this pleading, the plaintiff has annexed
with the plaint a page from ―Eximpedia‖ which states that the export

231
shipments are routed through ―Panchi Gujaran (Sonepat ICD followed
DELHI AIR CARGO and NHAVA SHEVA SEA‖.
393. While it may indeed be the case that the products of the defendant are
being routed through Delhi, there is nothing to even prima facie demonstrate
that the impugned infringing goods are being routed through the Delhi air
cargo. A general supply of goods does not constitute cause of action; the
movement must be of the infringing goods. Even the plaintiff appears to be
unsure if actual exports are taking place. That is why, in the same paragraph,
it has pleaded that there is an imminent and credible apprehension that the
defendant is likely to expand its export activities to Delhi. Both the pleadings
cannot simultaneously be made. It is either the case that exports are taking
place in Delhi or that there is an imminent threat that they are to take place.
The plaintiff and its plaint are conflicted.
394. Even if the plaint is to be tested on the apprehension to export ground,
the requirements for a quia timet action, as noted in para. 140 of this
judgement, are not made out. There is nothing on record to demonstrate that
the defendant has expressed a deliberate intention to engage in an activity
the result of which would raise a strong possibility of infringement. By no
stretch of imagination can the activity be considered to be imminent, and of
such a character that if the defendant were to engage in the activity, the
plaintiff would suffer substantial, if not irreparable, prejudice.
395. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section 134(2) of the Trade
Marks Act, or Section 62(2) of the Copyright Act. Accordingly, the plaint

232
deserves to be returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the
Court having the requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Jangeer Singh v. Yogesh Jangid
[CS(COMM)-598/2022]
396. The plaintiff in CS(COMM)-598-2022, as per the amended memo of
parties, has filed the suit against the defendant for infringement of trademark
and a claim for passing off. Both the plaintiff and the defendant are outside
the jurisdiction of this Court.
397. Paras. 36 to 37 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―36. That this Hon‘ble Court has the territorial jurisdiction to try and
adjudicate the present suit. The Defendants are offering to sell and are
distributing and supplying their impugned goods bearing the impugned
mark to the dealers, distributors and customers in the markets of inter alia in
New Delhi. The Defendants are carrying out their impugned activities under
the impugned mark by inter alia selling, soliciting the impugned products
through e-commerce websites namely www.indiamart.com, ie. Defendant
no. 2 and www.tradeindia.com within the jurisdiction of the Hon‘ble Court.
Products of the Defendants are available on the aforesaid interactive
websites which are accessible to the consumers within the jurisdiction of
this Hon‘ble Court. The consumers can ask queries about the price and
products and can place order and purchase the impugned products. The
Defendant no. 1‘s product is also advertised and sold through social media
platform www.facebook.com. The Defendant no. 1 has given email id and
mobile no. through which the Defendant no. 1 sells the impugned products.
The goods of the Defendants are also made available to the places situated
within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. By their impugned activities,
the Defendants are passing off their goods as that of the Plaintiff within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The Defendants are carrying
out the impugned activities within the jurisdiction of the Hon‘ble Court
thereby committing the impugned acts of passing off within the jurisdiction
of this Hon‘ble Court by selling and soliciting trade, distribution and
marketing networks in relation to the impugned goods under the impugned
trade mark in Delhi market. Hence, a part of cause of action arose within the
territorial jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court thereby this Hon‘ble Court has

233
jurisdiction to entertain and try and adjudicate the present suit under Section
20 of the Code of Civil Procedure. The Defendants offer, advertise, and sell
their impugned products within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court and
the wrong is done within the local limits of this Hon‘ble Court and
therefore, this Hon‘ble Court also has jurisdiction to entertain and try the
suit under the provision of Section 19 of the Code of Civil Procedure. This
Hon‘ble Court thus has the jurisdiction to try and adjudicate the suit.
Further, the goods of the Plaintiff are also sold through various ecommerce
websites including www.indiamart.com and www.tradeindia.com. The
Plaintiff also conduct business through their interactive website
www.jangeer.com. The Plaintiff business are being done inter alia in Delhi
which is within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. The Plaintiff has
tremendous goodwill and reputation in its said trademark within the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court on account of voluminous sales and
distribution. The Plaintiff actually and voluntarily resides and carries on
business and works for gain within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court
and therefore the Hon‘ble Court has a jurisdiction to try and adjudicate the
present suit by virtue of Section 134 of the Trademarks Act, 1999.
Thus, this Hon‘ble Court has jurisdiction to entertain and try the present suit
under Sections 19, 20 of the Code of Civil Procedure and under Section 134
of Trade Marks Act, 1999 as the Plaintiff is carrying on the business within
the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court. This Hon‘ble Court thus has the
jurisdiction to try and adjudicate the suit.
37. Thus, this Hon‘ble Court has jurisdiction to entertain and try the present
suit under Sections 19 and 20 of the Code of Civil Procedure as the
Defendants are carrying on the business within the jurisdiction of this
Hon‘ble Court. This Hon‘ble Court thus has the jurisdiction to try and
adjudicate the suit.‖
398. There is no document on record to support the averment that the
defendant is distributing and supplying the impugned goods to its dealers,
distributors and customers in the markets of New Delhi. This empty
averment is required to be looked through as per the dictum of the Supreme
Court in Manjula. In Indovax as well, such an averment without supporting
documents has not been given any weight by this Court. The averment is an
attempt at clever drafting and hoodwinking the Court.

234
399. Mere availability of the defendant‘s products on IndiaMart or other
websites, including the website of the defendant does not attract Section
20(c) of the CPC as the Banyan Tree standard has not been complied with.
400. The plaintiff‘s website being accessible in Delhi also does not
constitute it carrying on business in Delhi. The decision of WWE is, as has
been analysed in this judgment, does not govern the field on this issue.
Insofar as the pleading on Section 19 is concerned, it may be noted that even
if under Section 19 jurisdiction is made out, since the plaint is not
maintainable qua the suit cannot be instituted before this Court. The plaint
cannot, therefore, be entertained.
401. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be
returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the Court having the
requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Kajaria Ceramics Ltd. v. Yomesh Vijaya
[CS(COMM)-111/2026]
402. The plaintiff has filed a suit for trademark and copyright infringement
along with a claim for passing off. The plaintiff claims to be the proprietor
of the registered trademark ―KAJARIA‖, used in relation to its
ceramic/vitrified tile business, and the ―KEROVIT‖ family of trademarks,
used in relation to faucets, bathware and sanitaryware products. The plaintiff
alleges that the defendant, trading as M/s Western Drymix, adopted the mark
"KEROVIT" in relation to adhesives and construction-related products and

235
filed trademark applications in Classes 1 and 2 in 2024 and 2025. Despite
receiving a cease-and-desist notice dated 27.10.2025, the defendant
allegedly continued using the impugned mark, advertising and offering its
products through online platforms, thereby causing infringement, consumer
confusion and dilution of the plaintiff‘s goodwill.
403. Para. 47 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of which
jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―47. The Plaintiff is the registered proprietor of the trademark ―KEROVIT‖
and variations thereof in India, which are duly registered with the
Trademarks Registry, Delhi. The Plaintiff has its Corporate Office at J-1/B-1 (Extension), Mohan Cooperative Industrial Estate, Mathura Road, New
Delhi- 110044, and carries out its business in Delhi. Further, the Plaintiff‘s
official website www.kerovit.com is an interactive website, which is
accessible to consumers all over India, including Delhi. It is also pertinent to
note that the Defendant has filed caveats against the Plaintiff in this Hon‘ble
Court and as such has admitted its jurisdiction for this suit. Therefore, this
Hon‘ble Court has the jurisdiction to try, entertain and adjudicate the
present suit under Section 134 of the Trade Marks Act 1999. Furthermore,
the Defendant in its Reply to Examination Report has stated that its
impugned mark is also available online on ecommerce platforms such as
India Mart, and on social media platform Instagram, and against said
submission it has also filed snapshots of its product listings on Social Media
platforms wherein the said Defendant is soliciting business from customers
across the country, which includes Delhi. The Defendant‘s trademark
applications are also filed without any disclaimer as to any region of India.
Hence, this Hon‘ble Court has jurisdiction under Section 20(a) and (c) of
CPC as well.‖
404. The registered office of the plaintiff is outside the jurisdiction of this
Court. As per the decisions of Sanjay Dalia and Ultra Home, the plaintiff
under the specialised statutes, and the defendant under Section 20(a) of the
CPC, can be considered as ―carrying on business‖ at Delhi only if cause of
action also accrues at Delhi. Mere accessibility of the plaintiff‘s website at
Delhi does not constitute carrying on of business. The decision of WWE

236
supporting such a view no longer governs the field on this issue. The listing
of the impugned products on websites alone also does not satisfy the
Banyan Tree standard. Resultantly, under Section 20(c) as well, jurisdiction
is not made out.
405. In view of the foregoing reasons, territorial jurisdiction of this Court
is not made out under Section 20(a) or Section 20(c) of CPC, and Section
134(2) of the Trade Marks Act. Accordingly, the plaint deserves to be
returned to the plaintiff, which may be presented before the Court having the
requisite territorial jurisdiction, if so advised.
Pharmed Ltd. v. Best Biotech and Ors.
[CS (COMM)-874/2026]
Society for Human Resource Management v. Strategic HRM
[CS(COMM)-128/2026]
406. In CS (COMM)-874/2026, the plaintiff‘s suit is for trademark and
copyright infringement, along with a claim for passing off. The plaintiff
claims to be the prior adopter, user, and registered proprietor of the ―MGD3‖
family of trademarks (including ―MGD3‖, ―MGD3 10K‖, and ―MGD3
60K‖) and holds the copyright in its artistic product labels for its
pharmaceutical and nutraceutical products. The suit alleges that defendant
no. 1 is marketing, and defendant no. 2 is manufacturing, identical
pharmaceutical products under the deceptively similar mark ―CALCIMATE
MGD3‖, deliberately incorporating the plaintiff‘s coined trademark in its
entirety and copying its trade dress. Defendant no. 3 is impleaded as a pro
forma defendant, acting as an online listing platform/intermediary through
which the impugned products are actively promoted.

237
407. Paras. 103 and 104 of the plaint contains the grounds on the basis of
which jurisdiction of this Court is sought to be invoked, it reads as under:
―103. This Hon‘ble Court has the necessary jurisdiction to try and entertain
the present suit under Section 134(2) of the Trademarks Act, 1999 and
Section 62 of the Copyrights Act, 1957 as the Plaintiff is carrying on their
business in their corporate office at Delhi and the products, under the
Plaintiff‘s trademarks which are the subject matter of the suit, are
extensively marketed and sold in Delhi through various distributors and
dealers, within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
104. This Hon‘ble Court also has jurisdiction to try and entertain the present
suit under Sections 20 (a) and (c) of the Code of Civil Procedure, 1908:
b. Defendant No.1 and Defendant No.2 are conducting their infringing
activities and are soliciting business through their interactive websites,
www.bestbiotechindia.com, https://www.savyapharma.com/about-us/ such
which are accessible in New Delhi. The accessibility of these websites from
within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court results in detrimental harm to
the Plaintiff being felt in Delhi as a part of Plaintiff‘s business is getting
adversely affected. Defendant No.3 is also operating its website
https://www.justdial.com/ which are accessible in New Delhi. These
websites enable users in New Delhi to interact with the Defendants, reach
out to them for business related queries, or avail themselves of their services
online. Thus, these Defendants have purposefully availed themselves of the
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court by targeting consumers to conclude
commercial transactions within the jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court.
c. Defendant No.1 conducts and solicits business within the territorial
jurisdiction of this Hon‘ble Court through their Facebook page and
